# Pulsix backlog

Planning backlog for Pulsix, the NoiXdev server management and monitoring platform.
One line per deliverable, grouped by milestone. Update the status column as work moves.

Status legend: `idea` · `mockup` · `spec` · `planned` · `in progress` · `done` · `superseded` (closed in favor of another row that covers the same ground, named in its own Notes)

The status column is only useful if it is true. `done` means the thing
ships and is covered by tests — not that a plan once named it. When part
of a row ships and part does not, the row says which part (see 1.1b,
1.12, 1.19): half-truths here cost more than an open row, because the
next person reads this table instead of the code.

Last updated: 2026-11-04

## Milestones

| Version | Theme | Goal |
|---------|-------|------|
| v0.0 | Brand & design | Name, CI, logo, GUI mockups, architecture spec |
| v0.1 | Core | Orgs, auth, hosts, tags, REST API + explorer, single-node compose |
| v0.2 | Inventory & Ansible | Groups, variable schema builder, Git repos, job templates, worker, runs |
| v0.3 | Agent & monitoring | Cross-platform agent, enrollment, checks, time series, graphs |
| v0.4 | Alerting | Monitoring groups, rules, schedules, channels, escalation, maintenance |
| v0.5 | Status pages | Public pages, components, incidents, subscriptions |
| v0.6 | Scale & HA | Multi-instance manager, zone workers, horizontal scaling |
| v1.0 | Release | Hardening, docs, upgrade path, public launch |

The shell's global zone control was a label and a chevron that opened nothing; it is removed rather than wired, because only two endpoints take a zone filter — the fleet overview, whose page has its own filter row, and the host list, where GET /hosts supported ?zone= all along and the page never offered it. The hosts list now does. A control on the page it acts on beats a global one that would do nothing on most pages and fight the fleet's own on one |

A rejected attempt consumes nothing, held open by its own test |

## v0.1 follow-ups from the final branch review

Items the final whole-branch review deferred. The first group blocks a `v0.1.0` release tag;
the rest are follow-ups. Recorded 2026-09-11.

| # | Item | Status | Notes |
|---|------|--------|-------|
| 1.20 | Rate limiting on login, TOTP verify, passkey finish and invite accept | done | v0.3a Task 1 (with 1.15): middleware at the route registration before the handler, address via `PULSIX_TRUSTED_PROXIES` resolution; refused calls answer 429 without running the handler, a 4xx outcome charges, 2xx/5xx never; `PULSIX_AUTH_RATE_LIMIT=0` documented as the tests' disable switch, not a production setting |
| 1.21 | Reapers for expired `webauthn_ceremonies` and `sessions` rows | done | v0.3a Task 2: hourly `auth.Reaper` (ticker, injected clock) deletes ceremonies past their expiry (`expires_at` carries the begin + `CeremonyTTL` written by PutCeremony) and sessions past a 7-day retention grace (expired sessions are already unauthenticated at read; grace is forensic window, not a security margin); strict predicates, boundaries pinned; advisory key 8312 against multi-manager double-runs |
| 1.22 | Throttle `TouchLastUsed`; skip the session lookup for static assets | done | v0.3a Task 3: `TouchAPIToken`'s UPDATE carries the SQL predicate `last_used_at IS NULL OR last_used_at < now() - interval '1 minute'` — at most one write per token per minute, the predicate (not an in-memory map) as the source of truth so it holds across managers, a throttled touch a no-op rather than an error; the session-cookie middleware is short-circuited for every path outside `/api` (the SPA routes and embedded assets web.Handler serves as the NotFound fallback, plus `/healthz`), so static requests cost no session read; all API routes (under `/api`) keep the lookup |
| 1.23 | Batch `set_config` with the statement in one round trip for org-scoped queries | done | db.Exec and db.QueryRow send BEGIN, set_config, the statement and COMMIT as one batch instead of four round trips. BEGIN/COMMIT are explicit rather than relying on the pipeline's implicit transaction, since set_config's transaction-local binding is what the tenancy story rests on — out of that transaction the org_isolation_bypass policy would expose every organization, and the existing isolation and leak tests are what hold it. Query is untouched: its rows outlive the call and hold the transaction open. No speed-up is claimed — the saving is round trips, so it scales with latency to Postgres and sits below the noise floor locally; a timing assertion would be a randomly failing test |
| 1.24 | SMTP settings `PUT`: an omitted password keeps the stored one | done | the field was required in the schema, so the form — which can never show the stored password back — had to send `""` and wiped it on every save that changed something else. It is now optional: absent keeps, empty string clears, a value replaces. The settings page sends it only when typed and grew an explicit "Remove the stored password" checkbox, without which the GUI could no longer clear one at all |
| 1.25 | Invite cleanup on mail failure: detached context and a dial timeout; do not delete after DATA was accepted | done | the dial timeout the row asked for already existed (mail.sessionTimeout, 30s, via DialContext and SetDeadline). What was wrong was the end of the conversation: deliver returned c.Quit()'s error, so a hang-up after w.Close had already accepted the message counted as a delivery failure and the caller deleted an invitation whose mail was on its way. QUIT's error is now dropped. The invitation send is also detached from the request with its own deadline — in the caller, not the mailer, because Send honouring its context is deliberate and tested (a cancelled request must end the conversation at once) and the test-mail button should die with its request |
| 1.26 | Make TOTP confirmation and the session upgrade atomic | done | `auth.Enrolment.ConfirmTOTP` runs both writes in one `db.WithInstanceTx` (new: the org-free sibling of `WithWriteTx` for instance tables), so a failed session upgrade no longer leaves eight recovery codes on an account that never saw them. Found on the way: `MarkSessionMFAComplete` was an `:exec`, so an unknown or revoked session reported success — now `:execrows` + `ErrNotFound`, which is also what makes the rollback testable |
| 1.27 | Re-enrolling TOTP keeps the confirmed secret until the new one is confirmed | done | migration 00040 adds `pending_secret_sealed`/`pending_created_at`; `BeginEnrol` writes only the pending columns and `ConfirmTOTP` promotes them, so an abandoned re-enrolment takes nothing away. `last_used_step` is deliberately NOT reset on promotion: the step counter is secret-independent, and resetting it would let the confirming code be replayed as a login inside the same window |
| 1.28 | Step-up (fresh second factor) for passkey registration and deletion | done | migration 00041 adds `sessions.mfa_at`; `Principal.FactorFresh` over a 5-minute `auth.StepUpWindow` guards passkey register (begin AND finish), passkey delete, TOTP re-enrolment and TOTP disable. Refusals carry `step_up_required` so the SPA re-prompts and repeats the action. An account with no factor yet is exempt (its first enrolment has nothing to prove) — which is also what makes the guard meaningful for passkey-only accounts, where TOTP enrolment's own code check proves nothing. `CreateSession` stamps `mfa_at` for a session created already complete, without which a passkey login would never count as fresh and could never manage credentials |
| 1.29 | Security headers (CSP, frame-ancestors, nosniff, referrer policy) | done | none of them were set at all before: the SPA was framable, responses were sniffable, and a full authenticated URL travelled as the referrer. Strict CSP everywhere (`script-src 'self'`), with one documented exception on exactly `/api/explorer` for the CDN-loaded viewer — no inline scripts even there. `style-src` admits `'unsafe-inline'` for the SPA's style attributes. The row's `/schemas/*` half is dropped: no such route exists |
| 1.30 | Composite foreign keys tying `host_tags.org_id` and `hosts.zone_id` to their owners | done | migration 00051: hosts.zone_id gained FOREIGN KEY (org_id, zone_id) REFERENCES zones (org_id, id), and host_tags' plain host_id reference became FOREIGN KEY (org_id, host_id) REFERENCES hosts (org_id, id) ON DELETE CASCADE — host_tags.org_id is what that table's RLS policy filters on and referenced nothing at all before. The full suite passed across all 58 packages with the constraints in place, so no fixture was relying on a mismatch: the application code was already correct, the schema just could not prove it. No data repair runs; a violating row fails the migration rather than being silently rewritten |
| 1.31 | `If-Match` optimistic concurrency on config objects, starting with host updates | done | PATCH /orgs/{org}/hosts/{id} requires If-Match, in UpdateInventoryGroup's shape: hosts gained a revision column (migration 00051), the UPDATE carries AND revision = @revision and raises it, and no row back is resolved by a read into ErrNotFound (404) or ErrRevisionMismatch (412). GET and both PATCH routes return the ETag. The SPA needed no change — it never called the full update |
| 1.32 | Release workflow publishes only commits on `main` with green CI; `ci.yml` permissions block and SHA-pinned actions | done | the SHA pinning was already in place and stays. tag.yml now cuts the tag through NoiXdev/github-workflows' shared Go release workflow, triggered by ci *completing* on main — so "only main" and "only green" hold by construction rather than by a check. release.yml still builds images on a tag but guards itself first, since a tag can be pushed by hand: it asks whether the tagged commit is on main (pure git) and whether ci passed for that exact SHA, treating "no run at all" as a failure. ci.yml gained the permissions block it never had (contents: read; none of its seven jobs writes anything). Both guard queries were probed against real data; the workflows themselves could not be proven without pushing a tag, which is the very thing that publishes. The probe also found main's ci red for an unrelated reason — see the golangci-lint bump in the same branch |
| 1.36 | Decide deliberately what Go 1.27.2 does to coverage, then move off the pinned patch | done | **Decided and done 2026-11-02 (user's call: harnesses out, app tested).** \`scripts/coverage-gate.sh\` excludes the two test harnesses, internal/api/apitest and internal/db/testdb, as visible SKIP lines like cmd/... (after the FAIL check, so their failing tests still fail the gate). The packages under their floor with the honest count got tests instead of moved floors: internal/app 72.9 → 84.8 % (adapter tests in \`store_ports_test.go\`: host provisioning, checks and alert-route host adapters, template lookup mapping and nested secret opening, repo and vault identity conversion), internal/tenancy 88.4 → 94.6 % (7.29's list and revoke had no tests of their own), internal/atomicfile 63 → 96.9 % (temp-file creation injected into an unexported \`write\`, each failing step tested), internal/db 89.8 → 91.4 %, internal/jobs 89.5 → 90.1 % (among them the claim's re-check after a mid-build revocation and the sync_repo claim's redaction), internal/shared 79.9 → 80.1 %. CI's setup-go and both images' build stages move from 1.27.1 to 1.27.3 together (golang:1.27.3-alpine by digest; the worker's build stage built). The full gate passes locally under go1.27.3. Was: answered, and the other way round from the guess in this row: 1.27.2 is the honest one. 1.27.1 wrote each coverage block the statement count of its *enclosing function* instead of its own — block 416.2,420.1 of apitest.go is four assignments and was counted as 36; block 388.2,390.1 is two statements and was counted as 8. Across apitest that inflated 226 real statements to 704. Because the inflated blocks are all covered, numerator and denominator grew by the same 478, pushing the ratio toward 100%: 604/704 = 85.8% reported against 126/226 = 55.8% true. Both profiles hold the same 123 blocks with the same per-function percentages, so only the weights differ. The damage is therefore old and was merely invisible — the floors were never really met where large functions dominate, which is why only test harnesses and wiring moved: apitest 85.8 -> 55.8, app 86.7 -> 75.4, db/testdb 90.6 -> 83.3 (still over its 80 floor), db 90.4 -> 89.8, jobs 90.1 -> 89.7, and the other ~50 packages by tenths. Four fall below their floor under 1.27.2. Open, and the user's call: whether apitest and db/testdb belong in the gate at all (they are harnesses exercised by other packages' tests, the way cmd/... is already excluded), whether app's 75.4% is wiring or a real gap, and whether db and jobs — missing 90 by 0.6 and 0.3 points — get tests or a moved floor. Unpinning go-version waits on that decision, not on more measurement |
| 1.37 | Data race in the agent's gRPC session teardown | done | streamSender's mutex already serialised every send, because the check scheduler sends results from its own goroutines; closing the send side is the same kind of call and all four teardown exits made it straight on the stream, bypassing the lock. They now go through sender.closeSend(). The test does not wait for luck: its fake stream leaves the send-side counter unguarded on purpose, and the method was first written without the mutex to confirm the detector fires — it did, immediately. Noted in the code: the two renewal exits close before cancelling (the manager should see a clean half-close), so closeSend can wait on an in-flight send — bounded by the same single stream.Send every other sender already waits on |
| 1.38 | Re-arm `tag.yml` once the shared Go release workflow is on `NoiXdev/github-workflows`' `main` | planned | tag.yml calls that repository's create_go_changelog_version_release.yaml@main, which exists only on its chore/pin-actions branch — three commits, no pull request. Every automatic run therefore failed, five in a row, the last on a commit whose own CI was entirely green. The workflow_run trigger is commented out and the workflow is workflow_dispatch-only until that lands; the job's guard already accepts either event, so re-arming is restoring the commented block and nothing else. Opening the pull request on the shared repository is the user's own step (decision: Pulsix pushes to main, the shared repository stays a branch) |
| 1.39 | No write or handler timeout on the manager's HTTP server | idea | `cmd/manager/main.go` sets only `ReadHeaderTimeout`, so a single slow handler is bounded by nothing. v0.4c's final review raised it after finding one unbounded endpoint (the roster range, now capped at its own source) and one expensive one (`minOccurrenceGap`, now probe-bounded). Deliberately NOT fixed with a blanket `WriteTimeout` or `http.TimeoutHandler`: the server carries a real streaming route (`internal/api/job_sse.go`, `text/event-stream` with its own flush loop) that either would sever, so this is a change to every endpoint's contract rather than a fix to one. The pattern that works is bounding each cost at its source, which is what both findings did; this row exists so the general question is not lost with them |
| 1.40 | A real web build drops `robots.txt`, which the committed placeholder carries | done | 2026-10-25. Decided to KEEP one, and to make it stricter than the placeholder's: `web/public/robots.txt` (Vite copies that directory verbatim, so it ships with every artefact) says `Disallow: /`, not the placeholder's `Disallow: /api/`. The API was never the exposure — it answers nothing without a session. The sign-in page is: a crawler that indexed it would publish a private instance's existence and address, which is the one thing a self-hosted panel has no reason to announce. `internal/web/dist/robots.txt` keeps a matching copy for the `go:embed` placeholder a build made without `make` serves. A Go test pins both the content and the two copies AGAINST EACH OTHER — they are a pair that would otherwise drift with nothing to notice. When the public status page (v0.5) ships it will need its own allow for its own path; the blanket rule is right until something is actually meant to be found |
| 1.41 | Multilingual GUI: English and German, a default chosen at install, changeable per user | done | **Done 2026-11-02.** The instance default language is chosen in the first-run wizard and changeable by instance administrators under Settings → Language; each user's own choice is stored on the server (`PUT /api/v1/auth/me/locale`, `locale` and `effective_locale` on `/auth/me`) so it follows them across devices. Every SPA page is available in English and German through a typed catalogue in which a missing German string fails the build, and dates and numbers are formatted per language. Deliberately left out: server-produced messages, e-mails and chat notifications, and the end-user documentation stay English (the docs page says so). Spec `docs/superpowers/specs/2026-11-02-gui-languages-design.md`, plan `docs/superpowers/plans/2026-11-02-gui-languages.md`. Was: Measured rather than estimated: 102 SPA files carry user-visible text, about 419 strings in markup and 181 more in labels, placeholders, titles and aria-labels, and there is no i18n scaffolding at all — no library, no catalogue. Deliberately NOT folded into the GUI fixes it was raised beside: a sweep over 102 files collides flatly with every parallel branch, while the dozen strings those fixes add cost nothing to pick up afterwards. Three things decide whether this is a frontend project or one through the whole stack, and the first is the real question: (1) server-side messages. Text like "this changed since you loaded it; reload and apply your change again" comes from Go and lands straight in the GUI — either it stays English, and the translation is holed exactly where somebody is already having a problem, or the server learns the caller's language, which is a data-model and API change. (2) The end-user documentation is English; a German GUI beside English docs is a decision, not an omission. (3) Dates and numbers need locale formatting, and the GUI shows a lot of timestamps. Needs its own spec |
| 1.42 | A wording pass over the German GUI, and English singulars | planned | Collected 2026-11-02 from the reviews of 1.41 so they survive its scratch ledger; none is grammatically wrong, all are polish. **German:** mixed unit abbreviations ("Min."/"Std."/"T." in checks, hosts and workers against "min"/"h"/"d" in schedules and notifications) and dash styles (em dash in setup, members, account and instance against the spaced en dash elsewhere); "1 Messwerte" in the chart's accessible name (de/ui.ts) has no singular; stilted or ambiguous phrasings: routes "jetzt einen hinzufügen" (gender), the "Abmeldung" title, the routes text that lost its call to action, instance checks.hint ("und aktiviert"), hosts "entfernte Elemente", the lone "Lösen" button ("Anheftung lösen"), "das letzte Lesen", checks.notMonitored, workers dialog.tokenNote, agents dialog.maxUses, checks items.deleteConfirm "(ignorieren)" and groups.deleteConfirm "mit gelöscht", alerts timeline "Objekt" (vs "Element"), checks origin "Standard", schedules column "Nächste" ("Nächster Lauf"), triggers "Secret ist hinterlegt" (reads as a status, it is a button), runs detail "nur nicht mehr", templates vaultHelp, credentials installHint. **English:** count messages that read "1 members", "1 tokens", "1 samples", "1 … definitions", "1 schedules" — kept byte for byte during 1.41 so no test changed; give them a singular in one pass with their tests. **Small code notes:** app.go does not inject a logger into the setup service (it defaults to slog.Default, the same thing today); the instance language page's success toast re-derives the resolve rule instead of reading the refetched effective_locale |
| 1.42 | Sidebar grouped into Infrastructure / Monitoring / Automation, Credentials and Jump hosts promoted out of Settings | done | v0.4f. Fourteen flat entries had grown past the point where their order said anything, and nine Settings tabs were invisible from outside the section. Three headings now separate them (`ui/NavGroup.tsx`); they do **not** collapse, so no page costs a second click and there is no open/closed state to remember. NavGroup renders nothing — heading included — when every child is gated away, which is the one rule with teeth: three entries are permission-gated, and a heading over nothing claims there is something the reader then cannot find. Credentials and Jump hosts left the Settings tab strip for Infrastructure: they are how a run reaches a host, not a property of the installation. Their routes moved with them (`/o/{org}/credentials`, `/o/{org}/jump-hosts`) because a page absent from the strip must not keep living under `/settings` — the old paths redirect rather than 404, since they are in bookmarks and in older plan documents. Variants were decided at a mockup rather than in prose |
| 1.43 | Every select a searchable one, with quick-create where it makes sense | done | Shipped as its own nine-task sub-project (v0.4g, branch `feat/v0.4g-searchable-selects`). `ui/Select.tsx` is a combobox — always searchable, never a plain listbox, no threshold that switches between the two — built on `downshift`, the SPA's first UI dependency beyond React, React Router and TanStack Query. All 73 native `<select>` elements (the true count; 4 of the original 76 matches the spec counted were inside comments) are converted; `eslint.config.js` now bans a new one outright (`JSXOpeningElement[name.name="select"]`, with `src/ui/Select.tsx` itself exempted). 11 of the 73 pickers gained a quick-create row, judged individually against three conditions (the signed-in role that may fill the form may also create what the row offers; the created object's own form is self-contained; the field's absence blocks the form, or attaching the new object is the field's whole purpose) and a counter-test (no self-nesting, no duplicating a page's own create button): credential ×5, jump host ×2, zone ×3, channel ×1. The other 62 were evaluated and explicitly declined — filters that narrow a view rather than build one, fixed enums with nothing to create, a picker that would nest a dialog inside itself, assignment-only pickers a page-level button already serves, and the organization switcher (`ui/shell.tsx`), left out on purpose as the most deliberate object in the product. Two helpers (`src/test/pickOption.ts`, `e2e/pickOption.ts`) replaced `userEvent.selectOptions`/`locator.selectOption` at every call site (72 Vitest, 47 Playwright) |
| 1.44 | The Playwright suite went red on `main` and nobody noticed for a day | done | Commit `07166c1` (the GUI fixes: dialog centring, the repeat-password field, slug generation) broke it twice over and shipped unverified. First, `setup.spec.ts` filled only *Password*, so the wizard refused to advance and **every one of the eighteen journeys** failed at the first step — the suite is one dependency chain from `setup`. Second, the slug field's new refresh button carries `aria-label="Generate slug from the name"`, which Playwright's substring-matching `getByLabel` resolves for both `'Name'` and `'Slug'`, so the zone dialog in `hosts.spec.ts` and `inventory.spec.ts` hit a strict-mode violation; those three locators now pass `{ exact: true }`. Found while verifying v0.4f's own two edited specs, fixed in the same branch. CI runs `npm run e2e` and would have caught both on the push; it did not, because the workflows are prepared but not enabled while the repo is private. The gap this row records is therefore not the three missing lines — it is that a GUI change can land on `main` with no Playwright signal at all until CI is switched on (7.6), while the suite costs a few minutes locally. Whoever touches a form the wizard or an early journey uses runs `npm run e2e` before merging |
| 1.33 | End-user docs (`noix-docs`, branch `pulsix`): reconcile with what Pulsix actually ships | planned | deliberately deferred until the release (decision 2026-09-25). The section is not published — `main` of the docs repo carries no `pulsix` folder at all, so nothing public is wrong today; the 46 commits stage the v1 target. Reconciling now would re-edit the same pages at every milestone. Pages touched by a shipped feature are still corrected as that feature lands (v0.3a did this for the agent) |
| 1.34 | Regenerate recovery codes from the account page | done | POST /auth/recovery-codes replaces the set in one transaction behind the same freshness gate as removing a factor; the generation moved out of ConfirmEnrol into auth.newRecoveryCodes so enrolment and regeneration mint identical sets. GET /auth/me gained recovery_codes_remaining, counted as unused rows (UseRecoveryCode stamps used_at rather than deleting), and the account page shows it beside the action |
| 1.35 | Reap abandoned TOTP enrolments | done | the hourly auth.Reaper gained a third pass under the same advisory lock, clearing only the pending pair of a re-enrolment older than auth.PendingEnrolmentTTL (24h); secret_sealed and confirmed_at on the same row are untouched, and the cutoff is strictly less-than so a row aged exactly to the TTL survives, matching the ceremony sweep. Sweep now returns a SweepResult rather than a third positional count |
| 1.45 | Decide deliberately whether to enable the React Compiler, or stop citing it | done | `web/vite.config.ts` passes no babel options and `babel-plugin-react-compiler` is not installed — it appears in `package-lock.json` only as an unused optional peer of `@vitejs/plugin-react`. Found twice independently during v0.4g: once correcting the plan's own Tech Stack line (Task 1), and once in the wild — `ui/SlugField.tsx`'s own doc comment shaped that component's API specifically around avoiding react-hook-form's `watch` "because it opts the component out of the React Compiler", reasoning about a compiler that has never run. Either enable it (install the plugin, wire `vite.config.ts`, verify the suite under it) or remove every comment that cites it as a live constraint — leaving the claim uncorrected keeps costing real ergonomics for a protection nobody is getting. **Decided 2026-10-26: stays off, and the comments stay — the row's own premise was wrong.** The compiler's static analysis is NOT absent: `eslint-plugin-react-hooks` 7.1.1 ships it as rules, and `eslint --print-config` reports sixteen `react-hooks/*` rules active, thirteen at error (`immutability`, `purity`, `preserve-manual-memoization`, `set-state-in-render`, `static-components`, …) plus `incompatible-library` at warn — which is precisely what flags react-hook-form's `watch`. So every comment shaping code around "the compiler" describes a constraint that is enforced on every lint; `steps.ts` already said so correctly, and `ui/SlugField.tsx` has been corrected to name the lint rule rather than a build step. What is genuinely absent is only the optimization, and it was measured rather than argued: `@vitejs/plugin-react` 6.1.1 takes `compiler: true` with `oxc-transform-react` installed, and the entry chunk goes from **981.13 kB / 265.21 kB gzip to 1 238.08 kB / 362.24 kB gzip — +97 kB gzip, +37 %**, for automatic memoization in a logged-in admin SPA, with ten components bailing out anyway (mostly `try`/`finally`, which the compiler does not support: `StepUpDialog`, `LoginPage` ×3, `ItemDecisionButton`, `RotationDialog`, `RouteDialog`, `SetupWizardPage`, `ui/Select`, `ui/toast`). This project's own 3.24 went to trouble to keep 22 kB gzip out of the entry chunk; spending 97 kB here contradicts it. Revisit if the entry chunk is ever code-split properly, or if a measured render problem appears that memoization would fix. The v0.4g spec's "The SPA builds with it" is corrected in place |
| 1.46 | `ui/dialog.tsx` fires a call site's close handler twice on one real Escape | done | 2026-10-25. `onClose` and `onCancel` were both wired to the same callback, but a native `<dialog>` answers one Escape with BOTH events — `cancel`, then `close` once the cancel is not prevented — so one key press called the handler twice. Only `close` is wired now, and it is the right one of the two to keep: it also fires for the programmatic `el.close()` the open-prop effect performs, which `cancel` never does, so nothing is lost. A test pins both halves (one call on Escape, one when the parent stops passing `open`) and fails with "called 2 times" if `onCancel` comes back. It stayed invisible because every call site's closer happens to be idempotent — which is exactly why a test was worth more than a comment |
| 1.47 | `web/e2e/tsconfig.json` is not wired into the root project, so CI type-checks no Playwright spec | done | 2026-10-25, as its own CI step (`npx tsc -p e2e/tsconfig.json --noEmit`) rather than a `references` entry: the root project sets `noEmit`, which cannot be combined with the `composite` flag a referenced project needs, so `tsc -b` could not pick it up without restructuring both. Confirmed worth having while closing 2.68 and 2.78 — five new specs were written in this wave, and the command caught a wrong `getByRole` signature by hand that ESLint, being type-unaware, had passed |
| 1.48 | `JumpHostsPage`'s own "Add jump host", "Edit" and "Delete" buttons have no client-side role gate | done | 2026-10-25. All three now sit behind the same `canWrite` the host-key actions beside them already used. Not an authorization fix — the server enforces it either way — but a reader was being offered three controls that could only ever answer 403. **The test suite had the same hole and it showed when the gate went in:** the page's `base` mock carried no organization at all, which `may()` reads as no role, so the two tests that drive Add and Delete were exercising write actions as a session with no known role and passed only because the buttons were ungated. `base` is an admin session now. The viewer test already claimed "shows nothing interactive" and checked only pin/unpin; it checks all three as well |
| 1.49 | Copy buttons call the Clipboard API unguarded | planned | `navigator.clipboard` is undefined outside a secure context, and a compose install is often reached over plain `http://host:port`, so these buttons throw on click and give no feedback: `AddAgentDialog` (token and command), `TriggersDialog` (secret and URL), `PreviewTab`, `AddWorkerDialog` (token and snippet), `TokensPage` and `PublicKeyPanel`. `MembersPage` calls the API unguarded too but sits in a try/catch and shows its own failure toast. The Backups Restore… dialog (7.2, `CommandBlock` in `web/src/features/instance/RestoreDialog.tsx`) has the guarded pattern: try the API, and on failure select the text and say Ctrl/Cmd+C works. Extract that into a shared helper and use it everywhere. |

Concurrency tests race many pairs at once through a shared start barrier — a single pair does not reproduce the corruption reliably |

| 2.28 | Key rotation revokes the old key, orders verification after installation, one rotation per credential | done | `jobs.depends_on`, `jobs.rotation_credential_id` (migration 00022), `revoke_key` job kind and embedded playbook |
| 2.29 | Every terminal transition wipes the sealed payload and runs the finish sinks | done | payload-build failure, reaper failures, worker finish share one helper; a cancel wipes only when the job became terminal |

| 2.31 | Run events redacted on the worker; each ansible event stored once | done | claim `redact` list (credentials, secret extra vars, secret inventory vars, one-time logins); threat model in spec §14 |
| 2.32 | Run event seq monotonic across attempts, events stamped with their attempt | done | the run view groups attempts |
| 2.33 | The real-ansible gate runs | done | found and fixed: ansible-runner `-b` dropped the playbook; errored runs reported no reason |

| 2.35 | Smaller review items | done | generic SSE error frames; retest lock released on cancelled context; `job.result` audit carries the org; 00017 down order; `jobs (org_id, zone_id)` foreign key (migration 00023); git `--` separators and no leading `-` in repo URL/branch; rotate_key claim no longer ships the pending private key; ansible exit code 4 maps to failed |
| 2.36 | Template extra vars and claims cannot weaken host-key enforcement | done | re-review N1: connection-security vars refused (422) and forced by the worker as extra vars |
| 2.40 | Pin jump-host (bastion) host keys | done | v0.2 follow-ups: a bastion pin mirrors the host model of migration 00021 — `bastions` gained `ssh_host_key` (fingerprint) plus `ssh_host_public_key` (full key), each connection test keeps what its chain's hops presented in `host_connection_tests.hop_keys` (the probe captures per-hop host keys), the pin/unpin routes follow the host pattern (`PUT/DELETE /bastions/{id}/host-key`, `inventory.write`, pin-what-you-saw, audit) and the jump hosts read carries `ssh_host_key`/`pin_state`/`observed_key` (Task 8's deferred bastion `pin_state` landed here). Enforcement: the claim ships the route's pinned bastions' known_hosts (worker `bastion_host_keys`/`bastion_known_hosts`), the runner's ssh_config demands a pinned hop's key per alias (`HostKeyAlias=pulsix-jump-<id>`, `StrictHostKeyChecking=yes`) and a pinned hop whose credential cannot be scripted fails closed; a rotation into a jump host refuses a via-chain that includes a changed one, and a run whose route dials a changed jump host is dropped at claim time like a changed host key |
| 2.41 | Pin Git server host keys | done | v0.2 follow-ups: a repo pin mirrors the host/bastion model — `git_repos` gained `ssh_host_key` (fingerprint) plus `ssh_host_public_key` (full key, migration 00035), written by the sync sink from what the FIRST (unpinned) successful sync observed (the worker reports the presented key in the sync result event; the sink's WHERE clause is TOFU-only, a pin is never overwritten, and a malformed report is dropped). Enforcement: the claim ships the pin, the runner pre-seeds the run's own known_hosts (`UserKnownHostsFile=<work dir>/home/.ssh/known_hosts`, named explicitly because ssh resolves `~/.ssh` from the account's passwd home) with the pinned entry and composes `StrictHostKeyChecking=yes` — a changed key fails the fetch before any data is transferred, with the error naming the repo and the new fingerprint; a pin that is not one authorized-key line (or a URL that names no ssh host) fails closed before git runs. An operator unpins via `DELETE /repos/{id}/host-key` (repos.write, audited) — the next sync re-pins; a repo edit that changes the URL drops the pin. Repos read APIs expose `ssh_host_key`/`pin_state` (the hosts field shape) and the repo dialog shows the fingerprint with the unpin action |
| 2.42 | Run playbooks under a separate uid from the worker process | done | 2026-10-26, spec `docs/superpowers/specs/2026-10-26-playbook-uid-separation-design.md`: `PULSIX_WORKER_PLAY_UID`, unset by default (today's behaviour exactly), runs ansible-runner under a second uid instead of the worker's own; 0 and the worker's own uid are both refused at load as non-de-escalations. The one privileged step — changing uid — runs through a setuid-root helper (`PULSIX_WORKER_SETUID_HELPER`, default `/usr/local/bin/pulsix-setuid-exec`, must be absolute or refused at load) compiled into the worker image with its target uid fixed in; it execs one fixed program as one fixed uid, nothing taken from its environment or arguments, and refuses any uid but its own compiled-in one, so a worker whose `PULSIX_WORKER_PLAY_UID` disagrees with the image's compiled uid fails every playbook job loudly. The image deliberately does NOT set `PULSIX_WORKER_PLAY_UID` — the whole-feature review showed that baking it in would opt an existing installation in at the next `docker compose pull`, which the design forbids ("existing deployments keep working unchanged until an operator opts in"); `deploy/compose/docker-compose.yml` sets it instead, where a new installation can see and remove it. A worker running outside the image must ship its own such helper or leave the variable unset. File access needs no privilege: the play's primary gid is the worker's own, so everything it creates is already group-owned and readable back, and the data directory becomes `0710` (traverse-only) once a play uid is set so the play cannot list it or see `identity` by name — the identity directory itself stays `0700`, owner-only. The row's own suggestion of `CAP_SETUID` on the worker binary was **rejected**: a process holding that capability can `setuid(0)`, so it would have handed a compromised worker a path to root it does not have today — hardening in appearance, a weakening in fact (the design's anti-goal). Non-goals, unchanged from the trust model: this does not isolate a play from its own job's revealed credentials or from the worker's network, so the per-trust-domain recommendation (separate organization, or at least separate zone and worker) stays the stronger answer. Docs: `admin/security.md`, `admin/configuration/environment-variables.md`, spec §14. **Done does not mean safe to enable:** the whole-feature review found two defects that gated turning it on. **2.105** (the cleanup child inherits `PULSIX_ENROLL_TOKEN`) is fixed. **2.104** (a cancelled playbook keeps running, because the worker cannot signal a process of another uid) is fixed for the half it named: 2026-10-28, spec `docs/superpowers/specs/2026-10-28-cancel-across-the-uid-boundary-design.md`, a worker can now reach and signal a process of another uid, and a cancel reports `cancelled` in ~5 s where before it never reported at all — proven inside the real worker image. Both only bit a deployment that sets the variable, which is why the image deliberately does not. **2.107 was the last thing that gated it, and it closed on 2026-10-29 (see its row): a cancel now stops the play and everything it started, under a play uid or not, so nothing gates enabling `PULSIX_WORKER_PLAY_UID` any more.** What follows is the state on 2026-10-28, before that fix. It was found proving that same fix: the play's own processes — not the worker's — survive a cancel regardless of uid, because `ansible-runner`'s pty-fork and `ansible-core`'s own worker isolation put the play in process groups the worker's signal never reaches. 2.107 is independent of this feature — the identical escape reproduces with no uid separation configured at all — so enabling `PULSIX_WORKER_PLAY_UID` makes a deployment no worse on this axis than it already is today; it is still not a reason to turn it on, since a destructive play outliving a cancel the operator was just told succeeded is exactly the failure this note exists to gate |
| 2.43 | Show "pin not enforceable" for fingerprint-only pins in the API/UI | done | v0.2 follow-ups: hosts/bastions read APIs — `GET /hosts/{id}/ssh` and `GET /hosts/{id}/connection-test` — carry `pin_state` (`full` / `fingerprint_only`, absent while unpinned; bastion pins themselves arrive with 2.40, whose DTOs will carry the field then). Claims for fingerprint-only hosts still fail with the connection-test remedy, and the daily re-test completes such pins automatically. The SPA's SSH card and ConnectionTestCard show the badge ("pin complete" / "pin needs confirmation") |
| 2.44 | Option to refuse jobs against unpinned hosts | done | v0.2 follow-ups: org-level boolean `jobs_require_pinned_hosts` (default false — the spec §5 TOFU-for-one-job stays the default) on the new org-keyed `org_job_settings` table (migration 00033), exposed as `GET/PUT /orgs/{org}/settings/jobs` (read: org.settings.read/viewer; write: org.settings.write/admin, audited). When on, the enqueue paths that drive SSH — template runs (incl. retry), ad-hoc commands, bootstraps and rotation host targets — refuse hosts without a FULL pin (no pin, or a fingerprint-only pin) with 422 naming the hosts; connection tests are never refused. SPA: the toggle has no natural settings page yet (API-only) |
| 2.45 | Evaluate the template `limit` per zone at enqueue | done | v0.2 follow-ups: the scope resolution gates zones on the run's effective limit (a run request's own limit replaces the template's, the claim's precedence) — a zone no host name matches gets no job, a limit matching nothing anywhere is `ErrEmptyScope`; the gate never narrows a zone's host list (the limit string still ships on the claim, ansible applies it too — defense in depth). Matching: literal names, fnmatch globs and `!`-exclusions over `:`/`,` unions, case-sensitive; `~`/`@`/`&`/brace/space/backslash patterns and negated classes (fnmatch `!` vs Go `^` semantics diverge) are undecidable and keep every zone |
| 2.46 | Revocation targets snapshot | done | v0.2 follow-ups: `EnqueueRotation` snapshots the referencing host and jump host ids into `rotation_groups` (`{"host_ids": [...], "bastion_ids": [...]}`, ids only) in the same transaction as the group's jobs, and the revoke_key phase reads its targets from that SNAPSHOT — direction resolved to the plan's decision text over the older sign-off 3 note here: a target that stopped referencing the credential mid-rotation IS still revoked, because the old key is still in its authorized_keys and must be removed (removing a stale entry for a key that no longer authenticates anything is harmless; leaving it is not). A group without a snapshot row (enqueued before this change) falls back to the live references, the previously accepted semantics; a snapshot target whose host row is gone is skipped (nothing left to revoke); a rotation of a referenced-by-nothing credential re-keys immediately with no snapshot row. `rotation_groups` is org-owned by the standard RLS pair and its credential FK cascades (a hard reference would make rotated credentials undeletable) |
| 2.49 | Events of an old attempt's still-open stream are stamped with the new attempt and keep its binding alive | done | v0.2 follow-ups: the stream registry keys on (job id, token) so a re-claim registers a new binding instead of evicting the old one; `BindStream` pins the stamp to its binding — a re-claimed job's old stream keeps org and its OWN attempt, the reaper counts a job live while ANY binding is alive (an old attempt still streaming blocks requeue and exhaustion), the cancel poll touches and closes every binding of the job, and only an unbound late append falls back to the job row (current attempt) via the newest-live-binding adoption |
| 2.50 | Event append scans every hypertable chunk for max(seq) | done | v0.2 follow-ups: the unbound append path already read FROM jobs (the E2E org fix) and now also fetches `created_at`; the append's max(seq) subquery filters `at >= created_at` so TimescaleDB prunes the scan to the job's own chunks (SQL-text regression guard in place) |
| 2.51 | Event payload truncation's UTF-8 back-off is quadratic and can empty the head | done | v0.2 follow-ups: `BoundEventPayload`'s head cut is one `strings.ToValidUTF8` pass — a rune straddling the 64 KiB boundary becomes a replacement character instead of backing the head off byte by byte, so the head keeps its bound and the pass is linear |
| 2.52 | Templates with legacy plaintext secret extra vars cannot be saved with keep markers | done | v0.2 follow-ups: `sealSecrets` keeps a marker whose stored value is a non-empty plaintext string (the pre-2.34 shape) as well as a sealed envelope — the save seals the stored plaintext fresh with the row's AAD; a marker over no stored value, an empty stored string, or a value of any other shape (e.g. a number after a string→secret schema change) stays the "enter it again" validation problem |
| 2.53 | Reaper exhaustion does not re-check staleness at the transition | done | v0.2 follow-ups: the exhaustion transition's UPDATE re-checks the staleness it was listed under (`claimed_at < cutoff AND attempts >= cap` in the statement, `FailExhaustedStaleJob`), so a job re-claimed between the reaper's scan and its write is skipped, not failed (SQL-text regression guard + a mid-sweep re-claim race test) |
| 2.47 | Re-seal legacy plaintext template secrets without a save | done | v0.2 follow-ups: resolved as on-save sealing — the only possible one, since sealing needs the master-key box at app time and no data-only migration or background pass has it; the claim path serves a stored plaintext as the value unchanged, so a legacy template keeps working until its next save, and that save seals the value (2.52) |
| 2.48 | Redaction limits | done | 2026-10-25, partly: **base64 is covered, the rest is reasoned and left**. The three limits the row lumped together are not equal. *base64* had a named producer — `slurp` returns file contents encoded and `b64encode` is an everyday filter — so a secret read out of a file and printed never appeared as itself and went into the log untouched, which is precisely what made it look safe. `newRedactor` now collects all four alphabets (standard and URL, padded and not) beside the plaintext and its JSON-escaped spelling. *Hashes* are deliberately NOT collected, and the reasoning is in the code so it is revisited rather than re-litigated: a digest is not the secret, and whether one ever reaches a run's output is a guess where base64 had a source — every entry is replaced wherever it appears, so guessing costs log. *Values under four characters* stay out for the reason `minRedactLen` already gives: they cannot be told from ordinary text, and removing them would shred the log. Every encoding is at least as long as its input, so the additions cannot reintroduce that risk — pinned by a test. Docs: `admin/security.md`'s "Two limits remain" said transformed secrets "(encoded, hashed, cut apart) cannot be recognized", which is now wrong for the encoded case |

## v0.2c follow-ups

Decisions and limits recorded while planning v0.2c (2026-09-18).

| # | Item | Status | Notes |
|---|------|--------|-------|
| 2.54 | A revocation cuts open event streams on every manager | planned | v0.6: today only the manager that served the revocation cuts the worker's streams; every manager refuses its next RPC |
| 2.55 | Retry-failed of a triggered run keeps the run's overrides | idea | overrides travel sealed in the job payload, which is wiped when the job ends; a retry uses the template's own limit and extra vars (documented, and the run view says so next to "Retry failed" for schedule and webhook runs); a later step could expose whether the run actually carried overrides |
| 2.56 | Delete job templates from the SPA | done | `TemplateDialog.tsx` has a Delete button (copies `RepoDialog.tsx`'s confirm-then-delete pattern), shown for an existing, non-projected template; `useDeleteTemplate` (already existed, unused) is now wired up. The API already cascaded the delete to schedules and webhook triggers |
| 2.57 | Stop a revoked worker's running ansible process | idea | the manager cannot reach into the worker, so a play already executing keeps running on the customer's hosts until it ends by itself. **Corrected 2026-10-22, having checked the code:** this row previously said the claim query does not check worker status and that an in-flight claim "can still finish and be accepted". Both are wrong, and the second reading sent a later reader down the wrong path. `grpcsrv/auth.go:141` runs `lookupWorker` on every call carrying a worker certificate, `workers.GetBySerial` returns `ErrRevoked` once `status = 'revoked'` (`service.go:230`), and the interceptor maps that to `Unauthenticated` — so a revoked worker can neither claim a new job nor report a result, and `auth_test.go:97` pins it. The claim query needs no status predicate; adding one would be defence in depth, not a closed gap. What remains real is narrower: an ALREADY-OPEN `RunEvents` stream was authenticated once at its start, so after `RequeueWorkerJobs` one in-flight event can still be appended and land in the requeued job's log (final review minor 6) — and on any manager other than the one that served the revocation, that stream is never cut at all (2.54). Stopping the play itself is worker-side work and the only part that needs new machinery |
| 2.58 | Per-address limits shared across managers | won't do | Measured 2026-10-24 and declined; the per-trigger token bucket stays shared, these two do not. **What sharing would buy:** the stated limits (`HookIPLimit` 300/min, `agentEnrollLimit` 10/min) become exact instead of per manager. **Against what they guard, that is worth nothing.** The hook limit exists so "a single source cannot probe ids or secrets at will" — ids are UUIDv7 and secrets are HMAC keys, so 300/min and 300N/min are equally hopeless (the same arithmetic 2.62 settled for the timing oracle). The enrollment limit faces a SHA-256 token secret: 10 against 10N, likewise. **What it would cost is real:** a database write per hostile attempt on paths deliberately kept cheap. An unknown enrollment token currently costs ONE unscoped SELECT and no transaction — exactly the property 2.69 measured and chose to protect; a shared counter turns every refusal into a write. **And it would make 2.67 WORSE, which is the part the row does not say.** Behind Docker's userland proxy every worker arrives from the bridge gateway and shares one address budget. Counting per manager is what currently gives a fleet starting together more than ten attempts a minute in total; sharing removes that slack and makes a compose stack's simultaneous start fail harder. **A shared table is itself a target:** `clientip.Limiter` bounds its map at `maxTracked` with eviction and counts IPv6 per /64; a shared table needs the same bounding plus a sweep, under keys an attacker chooses. **If the aggregate ever must be exact, the cheap answer is arithmetic, not coordination:** each manager enforces L/N for the replica count the deployment already configures — no per-request coordination at all. Recorded here so that option is on the table rather than rediscovered as a distributed counter |
| 2.59 | Five pre-existing gofmt deviations from v0.2b escape CI lint | done | v0.2 follow-ups: the five files are `gofmt -w`-clean and the CI `go` job runs a `gofmt -l .` gate (exit-nonzero) ahead of golangci-lint, so any future deviation fails the build |
| 2.60 | Schedule ticker robustness under connection loss and shutdown | in progress | v0.2c ticker deferrals, two of five now closed. **The central one:** the decide-before-savepoint ordering — `due_sql.go`'s `processRow` commits decide's enqueue through the jobs service's own transaction *before* opening the row's savepoint, so a failure between the two (a write error, a panic, a crashed manager) left the row due with the enqueue already committed, and the next tick's retry re-enqueued it — is fixed by making the enqueue idempotent per occurrence rather than dragging it into the ticker's transaction: migration 00067 adds `job_idempotency_keys` (org_id, idempotency_key → run_group_id); `jobs.RunRequest.IdempotencyKey`, set only by the schedule ticker's own enqueue call (`occurrenceKey(scheduleID, dueAt)`, service.go) to the schedule and the due instant it fires for, makes a retried `EnqueueRunWith` for the same occurrence replay the first attempt's run group — filling in any zone a partially-completed first attempt missed, never duplicating one it already has — instead of creating a second one. `TestRetriedOccurrenceAfterAWriteBackFailureEnqueuesOnlyOnce` (internal/schedules) drives a real write-back failure after a real enqueue and checks the job count in the group ledger, not merely that the retry returned no error; `internal/jobs`'s own `TestEnqueueRunWithIsIdempotentPerKey`/`...RetryFillsInZonesAPartialAttemptMissed`/`...RetriesIntoAnAlreadyReservedEmptyGroup` and `idempotency_store_test.go` cover the mechanism itself against the real ON CONFLICT round trip. **The spin:** `ProcessDue` used to report how many rows it had *locked*, not how many it actually got to *decide* before its own deadline, so a batch the deadline cut short while locking a full `batchSize` still looked like a batch that genuinely finished one — `Tick` then started a fresh batch immediately instead of waiting for the next tick interval, hammering whatever made the batch slow. It now reports the decided count (`TestProcessDueReportsRowsDecidedNotRowsLockedWhenTheDeadlineCutsItShort`). The outer commit/rollback's unbounded context was already closed by 2.86. **The occurrence cap turned out to be a bug, not just a test gap (closed 2026-10-24):** `Between` stops counting at `maxCountedOccurrences` and returns the cap-th occurrence as `latest`, but `Decide` read `latest` as the NEWEST missed occurrence and chose between on-time, catch-up and missed by how old it is. A per-minute schedule past ~7 days of downtime therefore saw a `latest` a week old and recorded **missed**, declining catch-up in exactly the long-outage case catch-up exists for — confirmed, the decision came back `missed` with "10000 occurrence(s) missed" while the newest occurrence was a minute away. `Expr.Lookback` answers the bounded question instead (the newest occurrence within grace plus the catch-up window, itself capped at `maxCatchUpWindow`), so the second scan is ~1440 steps, not a raised cap. The count stays capped and now reads "at least N", because it is a floor. Two decide cases (one landing on-time-with-a-backlog, one a genuine catch-up past the cap — both `missed` without the fix) plus `TestBetweenAtItsCap` and `TestLookback` for the primitives. **The history half of the partial enqueue is fixed (2026-10-25); the retry half is measured and left, deliberately.** `enqueueRunScope` creates one job per zone in a sequential loop and used to DISCARD the jobs already created when a later one failed — so a manager shutting down mid-loop left real, running jobs while the decision recorded the occurrence `failed` and linked the run that did start to nothing. It now returns what it created alongside the error, and `Decide` records a partial as triggered, naming the shortfall ("started in N zone(s); the rest could not be enqueued") and linking the group. **Why the missing zones are not retried:** leaving the occurrence due means setting `NextRunAt` back to `DueAt` — nil CLEARS it (`AdvanceSchedule` writes `db.NullTime(next)`) and would stop the schedule outright. Setting it to `DueAt` does cause a retry, which the idempotency key would fill in correctly, but only inside `grace` (30 s at the default tick): past that the late path reaches the decision first and records `missed` WITHOUT enqueuing, so the zones stay empty anyway. It also writes a second run row for one occurrence. Both are history decisions worth making deliberately rather than as a side effect of a bug fix |
| 2.61 | Schedule service edge cases | done | v0.2c deferrals, five of six closed. `RunNow` now calls `Store.TrimRuns` (the same `TrimScheduleRuns`/`keepRuns` retention the ticker's own write-back already applied) after a successful `RecordRun` (`TestRunNowTrimsRunHistory`). `Resume` is a no-op when the schedule is not already paused instead of unconditionally recomputing `next_run_at` from now, which could jump an already-due occurrence forward and silently skip it (`TestResumeOnAnActiveScheduleIsANoOp`). `RunNow` enqueuing successfully but `RecordRun` failing now logs the bookkeeping failure and still reports the real enqueue result instead of an operator-facing error that would invite a retry and a genuine second run (RunNow carries no idempotency key, unlike the ticker's own decisions — see 2.60); a `RecordRun` failure on top of a real enqueue failure still surfaces the original enqueue error, not a generic one that hides it (`TestRunNowReportsSuccessEvenWhenRecordRunFails`, `...ReturnsTheEnqueueFailureEvenWhenRecordRunAlsoFails`). `Service.Runs` now clamps its own `limit` (1–100, default 50, mirroring the API's `clampListLimit`) instead of relying solely on the API layer (`TestRunsClampsAnOutOfRangeLimit`). `LatestScheduleRuns` (the list's "Last" column) is rewritten from a `DISTINCT ON` over the organization's entire run history to a `LATERAL` join against `schedules`, one index descent per schedule on the existing `schedule_runs_schedule_idx` — O(schedules), not O(all runs) — proven still correct per-schedule, not mixed across schedules, by `TestLatestRunsPicksTheNewestPerScheduleNotAcrossSchedules`. **One sub-item struck as stale, not implemented:** "`Update` computes `next_run_at` before override validation" is false at the current code — `build()` (service.go) validates overrides first (`s.tmpl.ValidateOverrides`) and only then computes `next_run_at` from the already-compiled timetable; whoever wrote this read the timetable's own compilation (which has to happen before validation, since validation needs nothing from it anyway) as the `next_run_at` computation. **The other half of that sub-item, closed 2026-10-24.** It was real and had an avoidable part nobody had separated out: `build()` took ONE `now` at the top and still used it for the next-occurrence computation at the bottom, with `compileTimetable` and `ValidateOverrides` in between — and the second reads the template and seals every secret override. For a frequent schedule that stretch alone can cross an occurrence, so an edit wrote a `next_run_at` already in the past and the ticker recorded a missed or caught-up occurrence the EDIT had manufactured. Reproduced with validation taking two minutes of a per-minute schedule; the computation now reads the clock after validation. **The residue is the row lock itself, and it stays, as a decision:** `UPDATE ... WHERE revision = @revision` may wait on a ticker decision holding the row, and nothing short of locking the row BEFORE `build()` closes that — which would hold it across the template read and the seal, lengthening exactly the hold that causes the wait. And the residue only misbehaves when the wait exceeds `grace` (2x the tick, 30 s by default): below that the ticker reads the schedule as due and triggers it, which for "every minute" plus a minute of waiting is the right answer, not a wrong one. A wait longer than that means the ticker has held one schedule's row for over half a minute, which is its own problem and not this one |
| 2.62 | Webhook and enrollment timing/ordering hardening | done | v0.2c deferrals plus final-review minors 2-4, all eight settled 2026-10-24. **Four fixed.** *The bucket's clock:* `updated_at` took whichever manager's wall clock made the take, so a manager running BEHIND wrote a timestamp in the past and the next take measured elapsed time from there — refilling tokens for seconds that never passed. `GREATEST(updated_at, @now)` lets it move only forward, erring toward refilling too slowly, the safe direction. Confirmed: without it a take with no elapsed time took the bucket from 8 tokens to 9. *Editing reset the throttle:* `Update` refilled to capacity on EVERY edit, so anyone who could rename a trigger could clear its rate limit by saving it again; the store now reads the stored row inside its own transaction and refills only when the limit itself changed. *Wildcard order:* `[*]` over a JSON OBJECT walked a Go map, so the same payload mapped to a differently ordered list each delivery — and for `limit`, joined with ",", a different host pattern with no way to reproduce what ran. `SelectLocated` + the library's `Sort` orders by normalized path: keys lexically, indices NUMERICALLY, so an array's own order survives past index 9. *Unchecked target names:* a template with no schema accepted any mapping target, `""` and `"a b"` included, and wrote the payload under a key Ansible cannot address — the mapping silently did nothing. Now checked against `schema.ValidFieldName`, the schema's OWN rule, so a declared name satisfies it by construction and there is no second definition. **The webhook timing oracle, equalised.** An unknown id answered without computing an HMAC while a known id's rejection hashed the whole body, so the difference grew with the body the caller chose. `equaliseSignatureWork` hashes it anyway on all three 404 paths. Only that term: the rest is constant-size work below network noise. Worth stating — trigger ids are UUIDv7, so a perfect oracle answers against a space nobody can enumerate; this removes the class, not a live hole. **The enrollment oracle is NOT equalised, measured and declined.** The secret check is SHA-256 over a short token plus a constant-time compare — tiny and constant. What differs is that a known id opens a write transaction with a row lock (`SQLStore.Claim`) and an unknown one returns after a single SELECT. Equalising means a pointless write transaction per unknown token: a DoS lever (cf. 2.69) bought with nothing, since the oracle reveals only "this UUID is a token id", useless without the secret. **The skip race: documented, not serialised.** `on_active=skip` and `overlap=skip` are check-then-enqueue, so two deliveries in the same instant — or one racing the schedule ticker for the same template — can both enqueue. Closing it means holding a per-template advisory lock across the check AND the whole enqueue, and the enqueue is not one transaction: it resolves scope against the inventory and creates a job per zone. Serialising all of that to turn a rare duplicate into none is a poor trade; the guard stops runs PILING UP, which it still does. Said plainly in `user/schedules.md` rather than left as an unqualified promise. **Distributed sources:** a per-address limiter does nothing against them by construction — that is 2.58's subject, not a gap here. **The bucket's untested time edge** is now `TestABehindClockManagerCannotGrantFreeTokens`. *Note on the equaliser's test:* the first version compared wall-clock cost against a real HMAC and failed under the parallel suite — a wall-clock assertion in a loaded test run measures the scheduler. It asserts the DIGEST instead: same bytes hashed, a longer body gives a different answer, and the decoy key is not a real one |
| 2.63 | `worker_enrollments.worker_id`/`created_by` reference org-owned rows without a foreign key | done | Migration 00065 adds the FK on `worker_id` only, `ON DELETE SET NULL (worker_id)` (PostgreSQL 15+ column-scoped SET NULL — a plain composite SET NULL would also null the NOT NULL `org_id`). `created_by` deliberately stays without one: `check_definitions` (00043) and `check_item_decisions` (00048) already document that choice for their own `created_by` columns (the audit log is the record of who, not a referential identity this table has to keep valid) — the row's own title assumed both needed one, which the codebase's own later convention disagrees with. Review finding: a plain `ADD CONSTRAINT` validates every existing row, and `worker_id` has had no FK since 00027 while `workers.Delete` has always been reachable — on an installation where that already happened, the migration would abort the deploy. The migration now nulls orphaned `worker_id` values first, matching the `ON DELETE SET NULL` it adds; `TestWorkerEnrollmentOrphanBackfill` seeds a dangling row at schema version 64 and migrates through 00065, confirmed to fail against the un-cleaned version |
| 2.64 | Worker certificate and identity hardening | done | v0.2c deferrals. **Five of seven closed 2026-10-24.** *A rate limit is not a refusal:* `ResourceExhausted` sat with the definite refusals in BOTH `internal/agent` and `internal/worker`, so an agent or worker that hit the per-address enrollment limit gave up for good and told its operator to make a new token — when the manager's own answer was "retry in a minute". It bites hardest where several start together: behind Docker's userland proxy they share one address budget (2.67). Now transient, which the existing jittered backoff already handles. *The renewed leaf was never chain-checked:* `Identity.Replace` checked that the certificate parsed and matched the agent's key, not that the pinned CA signed it — so a leaf from anywhere else would have been written over a WORKING identity, surfacing at the next handshake as an opaque TLS error with nothing to fall back on. Verified against the agent's own `ca.pem`; there is no CA rotation for this to get wrong. This needed a harness fix first: `newTestCA(t)` minted a FRESH CA on every call, so two certificates inside one test did not chain to the same root — it is one per test now, which is also what the real arrangement looks like. *`writeFileAtomic` x3 → `internal/atomicfile`:* three copies had already drifted in what they flushed, and the manager's bundle copy flushed nothing at all. *And the fsync gap behind it:* none of them flushed the DIRECTORY, and POSIX does not promise a rename is on disk until the parent is — a crash just after the write returned could leave an identity absent, old content gone too. The shared one flushes both. *The re-issue storm:* once the gRPC listener's leaf entered its last third, a FAILING re-issue was retried on every handshake — a CA issue (a database write) and a log line per connection, so an unwell database plus a reconnecting fleet becomes a hammered database with the reason buried. Confirmed: 50 handshakes made 50 attempts. Now one attempt per `certRetryAfter`, cleared on success, through an injected logger. **The worker's renewal path now checks the chain too (2026-10-24), and the symmetry is restored.** Not through a signature change across nine call sites: `WithRoots` is a `RenewerOption`, and `CAPool` — extracted from `dialIdentity`, which duplicated it — gives the two production sites the same roots the dial already trusts. The option is NOT optional in effect: a renewal with no roots configured is REFUSED rather than performed unchecked, so a caller that forgets it finds out at the first renewal instead of silently losing the check. That is exactly what happened when the change landed — the tests that actually renew failed loudly until they were given the harness's pool, which is the behaviour working. A renewal CSR is still not cryptographically bound to the worker's current key. **The `embedded` row, checked and closed as unfixable-by-construction:** the reservation itself is airtight — `Register` and `CreateEnrollment` both call `checkNotReserved`, the unexported `register` is reachable only from those two, and the worker `Enroll` path takes its name from the TOKEN (`e.Name`, already checked) rather than from anything the caller presents; `EnrollInput` carries no name at all. `TestEmbeddedNameIsReserved` pins both entry points. So no new such row can appear. What cannot be done is DETECTING an old one: a marker added now is absent from the manager's own pre-marker row exactly as it is from a legacy one, so it would refuse the working case along with the broken one. A legacy row can only exist in a database that predates the reservation, which — the repo being private with no release shipped — means a development install. **The global logger in the request path is converted (and the "package-wide change" was an overstatement in this row):** ten call sites, all in `*Server` methods, now go through an `s.log()` accessor that prefers `Deps.Log`. Until now a host configuring its own handler saw none of the gRPC server's request logging |
| 2.65 | Revocation response and audit gaps | done | `POST /workers/{id}/revoke`: a new `recordAuditFailure` helper records the audit event directly when job requeueing fails after the revoke commit, bypassing `auditMiddleware`'s success-only gate (status < 300) that would otherwise discard it even if the handler called `setAuditObject`. The response's `zone_slug` is filled in via a new `Service.ZoneSlug`, since `Revoke` returns a bare `Worker`, not a `View`. Review found two regressions in the first pass, both fixed: (1) `recordAuditFailure` ran on *every* error from `Revoke`, including the zero `Worker{}` it returns when its own store write never happened (unknown id, wrong org) — inventing a revocation of an empty-named worker in the activity feed for every 404; now gated on `w.ID != uuid.Nil`, with `TestRevokeUnknownWorkerWritesNoAuditEvent` confirmed to fail without the gate. (2) The zone-slug lookup ran after `setAuditObject`, so a `ZoneSlug` failure would make the handler return an error, `auditMiddleware` discard the already-set event, and a **committed** revocation leave no record — the exact defect this row exists to fix, on a new path. Resolved by degrading to an empty `zone_slug` (logged) rather than failing the request, since the revoke already committed and the slug is display-only |
| 2.66 | `PULSIX_GRPC_PUBLIC_ADDR` validation gaps | done | v0.2 follow-ups: a bare `:443` (empty host) is now rejected with a message naming `PULSIX_GRPC_PUBLIC_ADDR`, and a failed default derivation (a `PULSIX_GRPC_LISTEN` without a port) blames `PULSIX_GRPC_LISTEN` instead of the public-addr variable the operator never set; table-driven tests in `TestGRPCPublicAddrValidation` |
| 2.67 | `PULSIX_TRUSTED_PROXIES` and the Enroll rate limit can mistrust a Docker userland proxy | done | v0.2c: `Enroll`'s per-address limiter reuses the HTTP trusted-proxies list for gRPC metadata; behind Docker's userland proxy every external worker arrives from the bridge gateway and shares one 10/min bucket, and if that gateway address is itself inside a trusted range, a client can pick its own bucket through spoofed forwarded-for metadata; documented as a caveat (2026-09-19), not yet made safer by default. HTTP side (final review I1, fixed 2026-09-19): the compose stack now passes `PULSIX_TRUSTED_PROXIES` through and trusts the bundled Traefik's fixed address by default; the hook per-address limit counts only failed calls, so a flood cannot starve verified deliveries. Operator requirement, documented in the compose README and the user docs: behind a proxy of your own, set `PULSIX_TRUSTED_PROXIES` to it, otherwise webhook IP allowlists never match and audit entries record the proxy. **Closed 2026-10-25: gRPC enrolment no longer reads forwarded-for at all.** The HTTP side is sound because Traefik OVERWRITES the header, so the value is the proxy speaking. Nothing overwrites gRPC METADATA — Docker's userland proxy forwards TCP and leaves it untouched — so behind a gateway inside a trusted range an UNAUTHENTICATED enrolment caller was picking which bucket to spend, which is the one thing a limit on an unauthenticated endpoint cannot allow. `peerAddr` (both `grpcsrv` and `agentapi`) now returns the TCP peer and nothing else, and `Deps.ClientIP` is gone from both: a dependency that no longer decides anything, with a comment still saying it does, is the drift this same row's sibling items keep producing. The remaining cost — workers behind one gateway sharing a budget — is the lesser one and already handled: `ResourceExhausted` is transient since 2.64, so a fleet starting together backs off and retries instead of giving up. The existing test PINNED the hole ("another client behind the proxy" expected its own bucket); it now pins the opposite, including that a fresh forwarded-for buys nothing. Docs: `admin/configuration/environment-variables.md` no longer promises that the variable governs enrolment |
| 2.68 | v0.2c hygiene and test-gap deferrals, plus Playwright specs for the new pages | done | From the v0.2c final review. **Closed 2026-10-24: the mutable package state and the dead throttle argument** — the two items that were defects rather than gaps. *Package-level state (final review minor 10, and CLAUDE.md's own rule):* `triggers.presets` and the exported `triggers.DefaultRateLimit` var and `grpcsrv.unauthenticatedMethods` were all writable by anything that could reach them, and what they decide is how a webhook proves itself, what a trigger's rate limit defaults to, and which RPCs may be called with NO client certificate. All three are functions now — a switch for the two lookups, which also allocates nothing where a fresh map would, on a path `Auth()` and `SignatureHeader()` take per call. The allowlist's test got stronger on the way: it asserted `len(map) == 2`, and now enumerates every method and stream of both services, so an RPC added without a certificate check is caught whether or not anyone remembered to count. *The dead throttle argument:* `RecordDelivery(ctx, d, throttle bool)` ignored the bool (`_ bool`) — the throttling is `trigger_deliveries_throttle_idx`'s own outcome list. So the rule was stated twice, once unread, free to drift from the outcome beside it. Gone from the port, the store, the fakes and `finish`, which only carried it through. The two receive-level tests that asserted the flag now assert the recorded OUTCOME; the throttling itself is pinned where it lives, against real Postgres. **Still open, and they are mostly test gaps rather than defects:** Playwright specs for the schedules, triggers and workers pages (Vitest and the Go E2E cover them today — same shape as 2.78). **T1, both halves, 2026-10-25:** the `coverage-gate.sh` header named NINE packages at the 90 % floor while the awk condition enforced twenty-two, and still said "once those packages exist"; the list now lives in one place and the header points at it, because a comment that disagrees with the code it introduces is worse than none. The audit-`meta` half is **backwards and struck**: `marshalMeta` already turns a nil map into `{}`, the column is `jsonb NOT NULL DEFAULT '{}'`, and writing jsonb `null` instead would leave the column inconsistent with its own default and with every row that has metadata. T3: `internal/templates/service.go` is past the 400-line split signal (2.71 added to it); stale `sealExtraVars` doc comment. T4: the store maps an empty trigger to `manual` (test fixtures only); no partial index for `HasActiveRepoSync`. **The `parsePath` message is fixed:** it answered EVERY parse failure with "start with $", so an operator whose path did start with $ and broke further along was pointed at the one character that was right; it quotes the parser now, which is safe because the expression is the operator's own configuration, never payload. **The dead `CreatedBy`/`LastRunAt` assignment is gone, and the interesting part was WHY it looked alive:** `UpdateSchedule` does not write `paused`, `created_by` or `last_run_at` and reads them back with `RETURNING *`, so copying them in `Service.Update` was inert — but `fakeStore.Update` echoed its input, so the test suite could only pass BECAUSE the service copied them by hand. The fake now mirrors the real statement, which is the half that matters: it could otherwise hide a service that forgets a field the database would have kept. **The `-count=20` comment is corrected:** every CI invocation is `-count=1`, so the comment promised a safety net that does not exist. **The double ERROR log was looked for and is not where the row implies:** both `logPendingWrite` callers in the renewer return nil or nothing, so one failure produces one line; whatever T14 saw is not in that path and needs its own sighting. **The T5, T9, T10, T12, T13 and T15 test gaps are closed, 2026-10-25.** T15's four were named and are all pinned: the track-then-recheck ORDER in `RunEvents` (the existing test proves the re-read happens but stays green with the two statements swapped, and swapped they close nothing); the enrollment refusals, which declared a status per case and then accepted that OR 422 for all of them — measured, two are 400s with distinct reasons and one is a 422 from the request schema, and each now pins its own status and message; cross-organization isolation across all six worker routes, with one user owning both organizations so a 404 cannot be membership answering; and the finish-versus-requeue race, which asserts the pair of states is consistent rather than which side wins (both orders occur in practice). T13's three lenient "Unauthenticated or Unavailable" asserts are split to the code each case really produces, and measuring them found the foreign-certificate test was not testing what it said: crypto/tls filters `tls.Config.Certificates` against the authorities the server names, so an honest client never put the certificate on the wire and the SERVER's verification of a foreign chain was never exercised — both halves are covered now. Only the codes are pinned for the two handshake refusals: under TLS 1.3 the alert races the client's first write, so the message is sometimes the reason and sometimes a closed pipe. T12's CSR SAN injection asks for everything a request can ask for — subject, DNS/e-mail/IP SANs, forged `urn:pulsix:org` and `urn:pulsix:zone` SANs, `basicConstraints CA:TRUE` and `extKeyUsage serverAuth` as carried extensions — and none of it reaches the certificate; the URI half is the one with teeth, since `identityFrom` reads the organization and zone out of those SANs, and the agent counterpart is sharper still because an agent certificate is told from a worker's by the ABSENCE of a zone SAN. T5, T9 and T10 named no specific gap, so they were MEASURED instead of guessed: `triggers.firstSegment` — the redaction that keeps a sender's own payload keys and array indices out of the 422 answer and the delivery log an operator reads — was at **0 %**, and `processRow`'s write-back failure paths ("leaving it due for the next tick") at 0 % next to a tested panic path. Both are covered now, the latter with the failure injected in the database where a real one lands, in two places: before anything was written, and after the run row was — which also pins that the savepoint takes the earlier write back, or the retry would add a second run row for one occurrence. `schedules` 89.6 % → 90.4 %, `processRow` 76.7 % → 86.0 %. Two apparent gaps were checked and are not gaps: `workers.ZoneSlug` and the renewal's key-binding refusal read as uncovered per package but are exercised from `internal/api` and `internal/grpcsrv` — per-package coverage does not see across packages. **Closed 2026-10-25.** *T4, both halves:* `HasActiveRepoSync` runs on every delivery and had no index while its template sibling has had one since migration 00024, so each check scanned `jobs` evaluating `payload->>'repo_id'` — a cost growing with the whole run history rather than with the syncs in flight. Migration 00071 adds a partial index; partial is where almost all of the win is, since a finished job leaves it. **Measuring the plan changed what the test could honestly claim:** under RLS the payload expression does NOT become an `Index Cond` — `->>` is not leakproof, so Postgres may not evaluate it before the policy predicate — and with `org_id` leading, forcing the index proves nothing about the expression, so a mis-spelled JSON key would still produce that plan. The expression is pinned in the index's own definition instead. The other half: `createJobParams` substituted `manual` for an empty trigger, for test fixtures, and the substitution is not neutral — the trigger is how a run is ATTRIBUTED, so an unattributed job silently became a claim about who started it, in the run list and the audit trail. Refused on both insert paths now. *The grpcsrv logger:* the row called it "a package-wide change", but the call-site conversion was already complete; what was missing was the other end — `internal/app` never set `Deps.Log`, so `log()` always fell through to `slog.Default()` and the indirection bought nothing in production. *T3:* `service.go` split 748 → 489 + `validate.go` 154 + `sealing.go` 136, a seam `sealing_test.go` had already drawn.
