The stale `sealExtraVars` comment was a condensed DUPLICATE of `sealSecrets`' own account that had already lost 2.52's empty-string and wrong-type cases, so the two disagreed about when a marker is a validation problem; it now says only what `sealExtraVars` adds. *T14's docs nuance:* "A restart also saves it immediately" was wrong — the pending certificate lives only in memory, so a restart discards it, starts from disk and renews AGAIN; same outcome for the operator, a different certificate. *Playwright:* `workers.spec.ts` and `triggers.spec.ts` added (22/22 green); the schedules page is blocked on the worker image and moved to **2.96**. **Two items declined, with the arithmetic in the code so they are revisited rather than re-litigated:** naming the columns in the trigger management queries costs four more generated row types and four adapters — five places per future column, a forgotten one silent — to stop reading a SEALED value whose plaintext needs the box key anyway; and the `CAPin`/`CAPEM` nil guard would have to return a ZERO pin, answering a security question wrongly instead of failing loudly where the wiring bug is |
| 2.69 | Per-address hook limit bounds answers, not the database work behind them | won't do | v0.2c final re-review (sign-off 3 accepted it for v0.2c). Measured 2026-10-23 and closed as a regression not worth its gain. **Why the proposed fix is wrong:** the limiter is charged only for `hidden()` outcomes — notfound, disabled, rejected_ip, rejected_auth (`trigger.go`) — so an address that proves the secret is never charged at all. That is deliberate: a caller who can authenticate is bounded by the trigger's own `TakeToken` budget, not by its neighbours' failures. Checking the budget BEFORE `receive()` cannot preserve that, because whether the outcome is hidden is exactly what the lookup-and-verify decides. It would refuse a verified caller whose address budget someone else spent — and a shared egress address is the normal case, not the exotic one: one stale secret or one disabled trigger on a CI runner would start 429-ing every WORKING trigger called from the same runner. **What the work actually costs, measured:** an unknown id costs one primary-key lookup and nothing else (`OutcomeNotFound` never records). A known id costs that lookup, a `Box.OpenAAD` — a plain AEAD open against an already-built `b.aead` (`internal/crypto/secretbox.go`), microseconds, not a KDF — an HMAC compare, an `INSERT … ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING` that `trigger_deliveries_throttle_idx` collapses to at most one row per (trigger, outcome, minute), and the `NoteRateLimited` update of that same row. Two write round trips and an index lookup, with NO table growth. The row's description is accurate — only the answers are bounded — but the unbounded thing it implies (storage, CPU) is not there. Supersedes nothing in 2.58 or 2.62, which are about different limiters |
| 2.70 | A known-id call refused after the per-address budget is logged with its real reason | done | A new `NoteRateLimited` store call amends the delivery row once the address limit kicks in, matched by `(org, trigger, outcome, minute)` — the same key the throttle index dedups inserts on — rather than the call's own delivery id, which is almost always the very insert that got deduplicated away. The row now says both: the real pre-budget reason, plus a note that the sender's actual answer was 429 |
| 2.71 | Refuse malformed Ansible markers in template extra vars at save time, and a clear error when Ansible cannot load a run's extra vars | done | v0.2c final re-reviews 2 and 3. **(a)** `build` (`internal/templates/service.go`) ran only `ansiblevars.ForbiddenIn`, which looks at TOP-LEVEL names; `CheckMarkers` now runs over every variable's value on both create and update, before sealing, and returns a `schema.ValidationError` naming the variable. Well-formed `__ansible_unsafe`/`__ansible_vault` markers still save — they are legitimate — and the check finds a malformed one at any depth (`TestCreateRefusesAMalformedAnsibleMarker`, `TestUpdateRefusesAMalformedAnsibleMarker`). **(b)** `summarize` (`internal/worker/runner/runner.go`) dropped stderr on the non-recap branch, so the operator got `ansible-runner exited with code 1` while the run log carried Ansible's YAML complaint at the guard's column with the hint "Colons in unquoted values must be followed by a non-space character" — a quoting error in a document they never wrote. `extraVarsLoadFailure` recognises the guard's own name in stderr (sound precisely because the name is ours and appears only as the first thing on the quoted line) and reports what is actually wrong. **The nesting cap was deliberately NOT applied, and that is a decision, not an omission:** `MaxUntrustedDepth` is 32, chosen as a bound on UNTRUSTED input, and nothing establishes 32 as the depth at which Ansible's JSON load actually fails — Python's recursion limit is far higher. Applying it at save time would refuse trusted templates that work today, for a failure mode the evidence does not support. A genuinely too-deep template now fails loudly and legibly at run time through (b)'s message instead of silently falling back to YAML, which is the behaviour the cap was reaching for. Docs: `user/webhook-triggers.md` and `admin/security.md` both updated |

## v0.2d follow-ups

Decisions and limits recorded while planning v0.2d (2026-09-19).

| # | Item | Status | Notes |
|---|------|--------|-------|
| 2.72 | Notification rules for webhook-triggered and manual runs | idea | v0.2d rules hang off schedules only (spec §7); a webhook trigger or a template could carry the same `{on, channels}` rules |
| 2.73 | "Notify" column in the schedules list | done | A new `notify.Service.OrgScheduleCategories` (one query for the whole org, grouped by schedule) backs a `notify_on` field the schedules list endpoint reads; the list's own "Notify" column shows each schedule's configured categories as small pills. A single schedule's own notifications still come from the live `GET .../notifications` the detail panel already used |
| 2.74 | Per-recipient e-mail delivery | done | Both halves. **Disclosure:** one SMTP transaction addressed to the whole list put every address in the `To:` header of a message all of them receive, so each recipient learned who else is on the channel — for an alerting channel that is the on-call roster and often customers' addresses, and asking to be notified is not agreeing to have your address published to everyone else who did. `internal/mail`'s `deliver` now runs one MAIL/RCPT/DATA transaction per recipient inside the SAME session, so the cost stays one connection rather than one per address (`TestEachRecipientGetsTheirOwnMessage`, which checks the message body as well as the envelope — the `To:` header is what a reader actually sees). `TestSendManyDeliversOneMessageToEveryRecipient` asserted the old shape and was rewritten, keeping its real point. **Retry:** with one transaction for the list, a single refused mailbox failed the delivery and the next attempt went to everyone again, so the recipients the server had accepted received the same alert on every attempt until the delivery gave up. `mail.PartialError` names who is still owed the message — including the reached-nobody case, so the caller's question has one answer shape and no special case — migration 00068 adds `notification_deliveries.pending_recipients` (NULL = the channel's own list, which is what every first attempt and every pre-existing row means), and `Dispatcher.deliver` writes it on retry via `stillOwed`. `TestOneRejectedRecipientDoesNotCostTheOthers` covers the mail half against a server that refuses one mailbox; `TestARetryAfterAPartialSendGoesOnlyToTheRecipientsStillOwed` covers the queue half against real Postgres and was confirmed to fail (retry to all three) with the narrowing removed. `Sender.Send` now takes the whole `Claimed` instead of channel + id: what an attempt is FOR belongs to the delivery, not the channel. A session-level failure stops the per-recipient loop and reports the untried addresses rather than hammering a dead socket once each. Docs: `user/notifications.md` (`NoiXdev/noix-docs`, branch `pulsix`) |
| 2.75 | Resend and payload view in the delivery log | done | The log showed status, attempts and the last error. An operator looking at a failed row could neither see WHAT had been sent nor put it back on the queue once the receiver was fixed — the message was simply lost, and the only recovery was to make the underlying event happen again. **The payload view** is its own read (`GET …/deliveries/{delivery}`, `channels.read`), not a field on the list: the list returns up to a hundred rows and a digest's event is not small. What it shows is the stored EVENT, the input every channel's body was rendered from, not the rendered body. **Resend** (`POST …/deliveries/{delivery}/resend`, `channels.write`, audited — it puts organization data in front of an outside receiver, same as a test send) resets a FAILED row to pending with a fresh attempt budget and clears the stale error, so it does not read as both. Only failed: a pending delivery is already on its way, and a sent one would be a second copy in front of a receiver that has it. The status predicate lives in the UPDATE, not in a check before it, so two operators pressing it at once — or one racing the dispatcher, which may have claimed the row meanwhile — cannot both reset it; the service tells "gone" from "not failed" with a second read on the error path only. `pending_recipients` survives untouched, so a resend after a partial e-mail send owes the same addresses rather than the whole list again (backlog 2.74). Tests at every layer: store against Postgres (`TestResendDeliveryOnlyMatchesAFailedRow`, including the cross-channel refusal), service (`TestResendAndPayloadView`), API with the real permission split and the audit row, and the SPA with the admin-only gate confirmed to fail when the `canWrite` guard is removed. Docs: `user/notifications.md`'s delivery-log section |
| 2.76 | Teredo, ISATAP and operator-chosen NAT64 prefixes in the outbound policy | done | Teredo (`2001::/32`, `embeddingPrefix` gained a `complement` flag for RFC 4380's obfuscated client address) and ISATAP (no fixed prefix of its own — matched by interface-identifier pattern on the low 64 bits, independent of `embeddingPrefixes`' table) were unwrapped and re-checked recursively in the first pass. **The remainder, closed 2026-10-23:** an operator's own NAT64 prefix — an RFC 6052 network-specific prefix other than the well-known `64:ff9b::/96` — was indistinguishable from ordinary global space, so a deployment running its own NAT64 had a route to ANY blocked IPv4 address the policy could not recognise as one. `PULSIX_NOTIFY_NAT64_PREFIXES` names them; `Policy` consults the configured ones BEFORE the fixed table, since an NSP may sit inside a range a fixed form also matches and the operator's statement about their own network is the better answer. **Why it needed its own extractor rather than one more `embeddingPrefixes` row:** §2.2 does not lay the address down contiguously — bits 64-71 are the reserved "u" octet, so at /40, /48 and /56 the four octets straddle it. Only /32 and /96 are a plain span, which is exactly why the single `offset` field could carry the well-known /96 and nothing wider. `rfc6052EmbeddedIPv4` walks the octets and steps over index 8; pinned against the RFC's own §2.4 worked examples, all six lengths. The u octet is deliberately NOT checked against zero: a translator that sets it violates the RFC, but the address those octets name is still where the packet goes, and refusing to look would leave the hole open. Illegal lengths are refused at startup rather than guessed at — reading four octets out of an undefined layout would make the policy judge a destination nobody encoded. Docs: `admin/configuration/environment-variables.md` |
| 2.77 | Calendar in the schedule's or organization's time zone | idea | the SPA lays the calendar out in the browser's time zone; a team spread over zones may want a fixed one. Measured while closing 2.88: the grid is browser-local throughout — `calendar.ts` buckets by `at.getHours()` and builds every boundary with `new Date(y, m, d, h)` — so a fixed zone means redoing that arithmetic against extracted wall-clock fields (`Intl.DateTimeFormat(…, {timeZone}).formatToParts`, no dependency needed). The blocker is not the arithmetic but the zone: a grid spans schedules in many zones, so it needs ONE, and `organizations` carries no timezone column (00001). That is a migration plus a settings control plus a decision, i.e. a feature, not a leftover. The calendar now at least NAMES the zone it is drawn in (2.88) |
| 2.78 | Playwright specs for the notifications, maintenance and calendar pages | done | Three specs, wired into the chain after `alerts` and before `ansible`. None needs an agent or a worker, which is what made them cheap to add: `maintenance.spec` and `calendar.spec` work on organization-wide windows, and `notifications.spec` sends its test message through the stack's own mailpit the way `members.spec` reads an invitation. **maintenance:** create a one-off window, read its reason off the row, edit it into a silence, reload, delete. The reason is the point — the two look alike in a list and do entirely different things (maintenance lets schedules skip the hosts it covers, a silence only mutes their alerts), and the v0.4a final review found the dialog dropping `reason` on save, so every edit silently turned a silence back into a maintenance window. **notifications:** add an e-mail channel, Send test, find the message in the real mailbox, find the attempt in the channel's delivery log with its payload, point the weekly digest at it, reload to prove the digest was stored rather than merely accepted, remove the channel. The mail assertion is what makes Send test worth a browser test at all: the toast alone proves nothing. **calendar:** one maintenance window drawn by all three views (week bar, month dot, list row), and Next/Previous moving the range off it and back. A window rather than a schedule because a schedule needs a job template this suite's organization does not have; starting an hour ago and ending in three, because the grid buckets by the BROWSER's zone (2.88) and a fixed wall-clock time would land in a different cell on every runner. `calendar.spec` depends on `maintenance.spec` so the one window on the calendar is its own |
| 2.79 | Outbound client hardening deferrals | done | v0.2d final review, `internal/outbound`/`internal/mail`, all four closed. **Address fallback:** `DialContext` checked the policy against every resolved address and refused the destination unless all of them passed — then dialled only `addrs[0]`, so a receiver whose name carries an AAAA record this manager cannot reach and an A record it can was simply unreachable. It now tries each in turn and returns the first connection that succeeds; falling back widens nothing, since a later address was as allowed as the first. When none connects the error joins all of them, so a delivery's last error shows the whole picture rather than one arbitrary address. It stops early on a cancelled context — the remaining addresses would fail the same way (`TestClientFallsBackToAnotherResolvedAddress`, `TestClientReportsEveryAddressWhenNoneConnects`). **`To:` folding:** the header was one unfolded line, so a channel with enough recipients passed RFC 5322 §2.1.1's 998-character limit, which a strict server may reject and a lenient one may truncate — silently dropping recipients. `foldAddressHeader` folds with §2.2.3 continuation lines (never a repeated field), and deliberately emits a single address longer than the limit whole on its own line rather than splitting it, because a fold inside an address corrupts it (`TestToHeaderFoldsBelowTheLineLimit`, which also unfolds and compares the list back, `TestToHeaderKeepsAnOverlongSingleAddressIntact`). **Cancellation:** `deliver`'s `context.AfterFunc` ends a blocked read by pushing the connection deadline to now, which surfaced as a bare `i/o timeout` — a dispatcher could not tell "we are shutting down" from "the mail server failed" and treated an interrupted send as a server fault worth retrying. The error now carries the caller's own context error alongside the transport one, so `errors.Is` finds it; `ctx.Err()` is checked rather than the session's, so a conversation that merely ran long still reports as the timeout it is (`TestCancelledSendWrapsTheContextError`). **The untested branch:** the deadline choice is now `sessionDeadline(ctx, now)`, a pure function table-tested over all three cases (no caller deadline, a nearer one, a farther one) — rather than making the constant injectable for the tests' sake alone |
| 2.80 | Delivery queue: attempt accounting after a crash, and shutdown burns an attempt | done | v0.2d final review, `internal/notify`, all five closed. **The stuck row:** a delivery that reached the claim's `attempts < 20` backstop stayed `pending` for ever — never claimed again, never failed, and the trim only removes finished rows, so it sat in the channel's log reading as a message still on its way. `FailStuckNotificationDeliveries` ends these, swept once per `Tick` (`TestABackstoppedDeliveryIsEndedInsteadOfStayingPendingForEver`). **Shutdown:** a graceful shutdown cancels the in-flight sends, and `deliver` recorded that cancellation as an ordinary outcome — a retry with a misleading `last_error`, or, once `attempts` reached `MaxAttempts`, an outright **Fail**: a message the receiver may well have accepted was declared permanently undeliverable because the manager restarted at the wrong moment. The outcome of such a send is unknown, which is exactly the position a crash leaves the row in and what the lease already answers, so nothing is recorded and the lease expires normally with the attempt count untouched (`TestShutdownDoesNotFailOrRetryAnInterruptedSend`). Detecting it at all depends on 2.79's cancellation wrapping. **Clock skew:** the lease was written and compared with the claiming manager's own wall clock, so a manager running ahead judged a peer's fresh lease expired and re-claimed a row still being sent. The claim now takes only the lease's LENGTH and the database adds it to `now()` — and, since the claim compares `next_attempt_at` against `now()` too, `RetryNotificationDelivery` takes a DURATION as well rather than an instant from the manager's clock, which a manager running behind would otherwise have written already-due. The queue's timing is now entirely the database's, the one clock every manager shares; `Claim`/`Retry` lost their `now` parameters and the two tests that drove time with a fake clock now express elapsed time as the row's real state, which is the only way a second manager could ever see it. **Test-send growth:** the retention ran only on the dispatcher's `Complete`/`Fail`, so a channel an operator only ever exercised through *Send test* grew its log without bound; `SendTest` now applies the same trim, non-fatally (`TestSendTestTrimsTheChannelsDeliveryLog`). **Redaction:** `TestWebhookErrorsNeverCarryTheURLsSecrets` covers a token in the query string, a token in the path, and the refused-302 path, where the error comes from the client's own `CheckRedirect` rather than the transport. The row also assumed userinfo was a redaction case; it is not — validation refuses a webhook URL carrying a user name or password at save time, so that secret is never stored to leak. That stronger guarantee now has its own test (`TestWebhookURLWithUserinfoIsRefusedAtSaveTime`), so removing the check cannot silently shift the burden onto redaction |
| 2.81 | Run-group report accuracy | done | v0.2d final review, `internal/jobs`. **Both counting inaccuracies are fixed.** `HostsChanged`/`HostsFailed` used to count every distinct element of the workers' stats lists; those lists carry host UUIDs only where the claim's connection map named one, and a host it does not name — an implicit `localhost` — stays a bare name (`internal/worker/runner/stats.go`), so the counts mixed names into a total compared against `cardinality(host_ids)` and could exceed the run's real host count. `runGroupQuery` now intersects each job's lists with that job's own `host_ids`, the same thing `RetryFailed`'s `failedHostIDs` already did for the same reason (`pg_input_is_valid` screens an element before the cast — a bare name is not a uuid and the cast alone would abort the finishing transaction). Second: a job ending `error` or `cancelled` has no per-host stats at all, so its hosts counted toward `Hosts` but appeared in no failure list and `HostsOK = Hosts - HostsFailed` absorbed every one of them — a group whose jobs all died at the worker rendered "N hosts (ok N, changed 0, failed 0)" under a failed result. `HostsNoResult` now counts them, keyed on the absence of stats rather than on status (a job that marked itself failed but died before its recap has reached no per-host verdict either), `HostsOK` subtracts it, and `hosts_ok + hosts_failed + hosts_no_result == hosts` holds. The e-mail body adds ", no result N" only when there are any; `run.hosts_no_result` is a new webhook placeholder and `hosts_no_result` a new, always-present field of the `run` section. Tests: `TestStatelessErrorJobsHostsAreNotCountedAsOK`, `TestStatsHostsOutsideTheJobsTargetsAreNotCounted`. Docs: `user/notifications.md` (`NoiXdev/noix-docs`, branch `pulsix`). **The third item, closed 2026-10-24: a run's message now says when hosts were left out for maintenance.** With `exclude_hosts`, `Hosts` counts only what remained, so the message read "9 hosts (ok 9, changed 0, failed 0)" for a twelve-host scope — a run covering a quarter less of the fleet than the schedule says, with nothing to explain it. The aggregate query could not recover it: `RunRequest.ExcludeHosts` shapes the targets at enqueue time and was written down nowhere. Migration 00070 adds `jobs.hosts_excluded`, counted in `resolveRunTargets` over the deduplicated host set before and after the exclusion (hosts a CHANGED KEY removed are deliberately not in it — a different refusal with its own error). **It is the REQUEST's count on every job, read with `max()`, not `sum()`:** a zone all of whose hosts were excluded produces no job at all, so a sum would under-report exactly the case with the most to report — which is the case the store test covers. From there the usual path: `RunGroupResult.HostsExcluded`, the event's `hosts_excluded`, the `run.hosts_excluded` placeholder, and the e-mail's Hosts line, where it sits OUTSIDE the parentheses because the number in front of them is what the run targeted. Only when non-zero, like `hosts_no_result`. Docs: `user/notifications.md` says why it is outside the sum that the other counts belong to. The two transaction-safety items this row used to carry were **fixed** in the final fix wave, not deferred: the group read and the report claim run under a savepoint, and `GroupSummaries`' SQL and org scoping have tests |
| 2.82 | `internal/notify`: digest accuracy and the per-rule channel bound | done | v0.2d final review. (An earlier version of this row asserted as a **correctness bug** that `processOne` runs `handle` ahead of its timezone validation. That was **false** and has been removed: `digest_ticker.go` calls `NextDigestRun` — and therefore `LoadLocation` — before `handle`, exactly as its own comment says.) **Closed 2026-10-23, four of seven.** *Starvation (the worst of them):* `LockDueDigests` orders by `next_run_at` and takes the oldest `digestBatch` (20) rows, so an organization whose build keeps failing never advances, keeps the oldest timestamp and is re-selected every tick; with 20 of them no healthy organization ever gets a slot, and the jam only clears when the stuck rows pass `maxDigestLate` — by which point the digests queued behind them are themselves too late and are skipped for that week, silently. Migration 00069 adds `retry_after`, deliberately separate from `next_run_at` (which names the occurrence the message is ABOUT, so moving it would change which week is sent); a failed attempt is held out of the scan for `digestRetryDelay` (15 min, fixed — one delay is all it takes to let the rows behind through, and a row that keeps failing is given up on at `maxDigestLate` anyway). The delay is written on the OUTER transaction, not the savepoint, which has just been rolled back (`TestAFailingDigestDoesNotStarveTheOthers`, confirmed to show failing=2 / healthy=0 without it). *`last_period` lied:* the too-late skip returned nil from `handle`, which `processOne` could not tell from a send, so the row stamped `last_period` with an occurrence nothing was sent for. `ErrDigestSkipped` is now part of the exported `DigestQueue` contract — an implementation treating it as an ordinary failure would retry a skipped week for ever — and `AdvanceNotificationDigest` takes a nullable period that leaves the column alone. *The per-rule channel bound* lived only in the HTTP layer's `maxItems` tag; `maxRuleChannels` is now enforced in `mergeRules`, per rule, matching the digest's own `maxDigestChannels`. *The concurrency test* now exists (`TestTwoDigestTickersDoNotDoubleSendAPeriod`, two tickers racing one due row, exactly one delivery). **The last three settled 2026-10-24; two of them were not defects.** *The silent disable WAS one and is fixed:* settings whose timezone no longer resolves stop the digest — correct, since no retry fixes a zone name that is gone — but the only trace was a line in the manager's log. For the organization its weekly digest simply switched itself off, with an unticked checkbox as the entire explanation, where a schedule failing the same way records a visible failed run. `DigestSQLStore` now takes a `DigestAuditor` and records the disable against the organization, naming the zone, because that IS the repair instruction. Written after the savepoint commits: the disable must not roll back because recording it failed. *The "one open transaction" is measured and fine:* `ProcessDue` opens one transaction per TICK for a batch of at most `digestBatch` (20) rows, not for every organization, and the batch deadline ceilings it at `max(4 x tick, 10 s)` = 4 minutes at the default one-minute tick. Expected duration is seconds — twenty digest builds — and the ceiling is a bound, not a cost paid. Splitting it into twenty transactions would trade a short xmin hold for twenty commits and lose the single lock set; the per-row work is savepointed already. *`schedules.Service.Digest` returning a `notify` type is not a layering fault:* `notify` declares `DigestBuilder` next to itself, which is exactly where CLAUDE.md puts a port, and an implementer necessarily speaks its consumer's vocabulary. There is no cycle — `notify` does not import `schedules` — and the alternatives (a shared types package, or a near-duplicate type plus a field-by-field adapter in `internal/app`) add machinery and a chance to drift for no behavioural gain. Docs unchanged throughout: the user page's promise about a late digest is unaffected |
| 2.83 | `go test -race ./...` is flaky as one invocation on a loaded machine | done | v0.2 follow-ups: the race suite now runs as two disjoint package groups everywhere it is prescribed — coverage-gate.sh (CI), the Makefile `test` target and CLAUDE.md's pre-commit instruction — module minus `internal/e2e` first, then `./internal/e2e/...` on its own invocation |
| 2.84 | Digest body: cap the schedule list | done | v0.2 follow-ups: `schedules.Service.Digest` caps the summary's schedule list at 200 (`notify.MaxDigestSchedules`) — schedules with runs in the period first (runs desc, then name), the rest by name — and sets `DigestSummary`'s additive `truncated`/`schedule_count_total`. The e-mail body and a webhook template's `digest.schedules_note` placeholder say "+N schedules without activity"; the capped default webhook body renders far below `maxBodyBytes` (tested with a 1000-schedule org) instead of being judged permanent |
| 2.85 | SPA: queries with no error surface | done | v0.2 follow-ups: `ChannelsPage` guards the channels query like `orgInfo` (a 5xx on the list renders the error state instead of the permanent loading skeleton); `DigestCard` and `ScheduleNotifications` render the guard element for a failed digest/rules query in the section's place instead of returning `null`, while the schedule's channel-list failure keeps its bespoke reader-view alert (the rules stay readable); each has 5xx + refetch-recovery tests (`ChannelsPage.test.tsx`, `DigestCard.test.tsx`, `ScheduleNotifications.test.tsx`) |
| 2.86 | Shutdown and scan bounds left over from v0.2d | done | v0.2d final review, minors 27 and 29: `due_sql.go`'s final commit and its deferred rollback bound their detached context with `rowWriteTimeout`, mirroring the pairing `digest_ticker.go`'s `ProcessDue` already used; migration 00066 adds `schedule_runs_org_due_idx (org_id, due_at)` for `ListScheduleRunsBetween`. The separate, larger item this row deferred on purpose — the ticker's decide/write-back split itself, whose hazard is the enqueue committing outside the row's own savepoint — is now closed too, under 2.60 (its own title and history), with migration 00067's idempotency key |
| 2.87 | Outbox: the cancel path is not transactional, and `run.finished` is at-most-once | done | v0.2d final review. **Minor 31 done:** `internal/db/migrations/README.md` records that 00030 is permanently retired and why — `goose.UpContext` runs without `WithAllowMissing`, so a file numbered below the high-water mark in `goose_db_version` is an error, and a later-added 00030 would refuse to start the manager on exactly the installations that are furthest along. The file also writes down the directory's standing rules (additive only, the RLS policy pair with FORCE, composite `(org_id, id)` references, and repairing rows before adding a constraint that validates them). Only `*.sql` is embedded, so the README costs nothing at runtime. **Minor 30, the documentation half, done:** `Outbox`'s doc comment claimed all three sources write inside their fact's transaction, which is false for a group completed by a cancel, and `run.finished`'s at-most-once semantics were stated nowhere. Both are now written down at the type, with the reasoning for the at-most-once ruling (a sink failure that fails the finish wedges the job in `running`, and a scheduled template's overlap guard then skips every later occurrence). **Closed 2026-10-24, and NOT the way the row asked for — making the cancel path transactional is the wrong fix.** Checked against `terminal.go`: the finish path takes the group advisory lock FIRST and the job row second. A cancel done in one transaction would hold the job's row (from `CancelJob`) and then wait for the group lock — the exact reverse — so a cancel and a finish racing on one group would deadlock. Closing that needs the cancel to take the group lock before its own UPDATE, which is a restructuring of the package's most race-sensitive code for one path's crash window. (The nested-`WithWriteTx` obstacle the row already recorded is real too, but it is the lesser one.) **A sweep needs no lock-order change and covers EVERY path's window, not one.** `SweepUnreportedRunGroups` finds run groups whose jobs are all terminal and that nobody has claimed a report on (`run_group_reported` is only ever set on the group's first job, so "nobody in the group is marked" is the whole test), and reports them through the same locked path the cancel uses. Re-reporting is harmless twice over: `claimRunGroupReport` lets exactly one caller through, and the outbox dedupes on an event key that IS the group id. It runs in the reaper rather than a ticker of its own — that loop already runs every ten seconds, already has no organization, and already repairs what a dead manager left behind. Bounded by a six-hour window (a notification about last month's run is noise, not news) and a batch of 20, and its failure never fails the sweep: a requeue matters more than a late notification. Tests cover the lost report being delivered exactly once, a second pass finding nothing, and a half-finished or two-day-old group being left alone |
| 2.88 | Time zone presentation left over from v0.2d | done | v0.2d final review, minors 1, 2, 3 and 5, all four closed. **The placeholder pair:** `{{digest.from}}`/`{{digest.to}}` went through `formatTime`, which forces UTC, while `{{digest.period}}` formats each end in the organization's zone — a body carrying all three dated the week a day early east of UTC and a day late west of it, and a consumer joining them could not tell which week was meant. Both ends now print with the organization's offset; the instant is unchanged, so a consumer that only parses the timestamp sees no difference (`TestDigestFromAndToAgreeWithThePeriodLabel`, which round-trips the event through JSON first — that is where a named location is flattened to a bare offset — and covers Pacific/Auckland and America/Los_Angeles, confirmed to fail one day either side without the fix). **The digest card's next-run line:** rendered in the browser's zone with nothing saying so, directly above a Timezone field reading something else; now rendered in the digest's own zone and named, with a named browser-zone fallback for a zone the browser's database does not have (confirmed to throw a `RangeError` that took the whole card down without the guard). **The calendar:** still browser-local — a fixed zone is 2.77, and needs a zone an organization does not yet carry — but it now says which zone its grid is drawn in. **`time/tzdata`:** `cmd/manager` blank-imports it. Fourteen `LoadLocation` call sites across six packages resolve named zones, and on a host shipping no zoneinfo all fourteen failed at once while blaming the operator's data (`internal/schedules` stops the schedule with "the schedule's timetable is invalid; edit and save it", which no edit can fix). The embedded copy is a fallback — the host's own files still win and still get the host's updates — so what it buys is that a failure now means an unknown zone NAME, which is what those call sites already say. Manager only: the worker and the agent resolve no named zones, and the agent binaries are served to hosts, so the 403 KiB is not theirs. `TestManagerEmbedsTheZoneDatabase` reads the import out of the shipped sources only, since a `_test.go` import would embed nothing |
| 2.89 | More than one vault password per repository | done | Migration 00059 added `git_repo_vault_credentials` and `job_template_vault_credentials` (org_id, parent id, credential_id, label — primary key on the label), beside the old single `git_repos.vault_credential_id` column (kept, see 2.93). `internal/repos.ResolveVaultIdentities` merges a run's repository and template sets by label, the template winning a collision — the same org → group → host → item precedence `internal/rules.Resolve` already gives. The runner (`internal/worker/runner`) writes one 0600 password file per identity and a `vault_identity_list = label@path, ...` line into the generated `ansible.cfg`, replacing `vault_password_file`; `internal/vaultlabel` is the one label-safety rule, checked at write time (`internal/repos`) and again, last, by the runner itself, since nothing stops a future caller of the store from skipping the service layer. The repository's and template's editors (`RepoDialog`/`TemplateDialog`'s shared `VaultIdentitiesField`) edit the labelled set directly, and a run's detail reports the resolved set with `from_template` per entry. `internal/e2e/vault_test.go`'s `TestVaultIdentitiesEndToEnd` runs a repository with two labelled passwords and a repo-backed playbook whose two `vars_files` are each encrypted under a different one, plus a second template adding a third and proving a sibling template never receives it; only CI's real-ansible pass (2.96) actually decrypts, the default fake-runner pass proves the resolution and API plumbing instead. Docs: `user/credentials-and-jump-hosts.md` and `user/playbooks-and-jobs.md` (`NoiXdev/noix-docs`, branch `pulsix`). Left over: 2.91–2.96 |
| 2.90 | Resources the installation owns, usable by every organization | done | v0.2f. The `org_id`-nullable mechanism this row originally sketched does not survive the schema: five tables already tie tenancy to composite foreign keys `(org_id, id)`, which a NULL `org_id` can never match (design doc decision 1). Shipped instead: the installation owns a **definition** (`shared_repos`, `shared_job_templates`, `shared_check_definitions` — no `org_id`, instance-admin only) and each organization gets an ordinary **projection** of it — its own row, its own `org_id`, its own row-level security and composite keys, linked by one nullable `shared_repo_id`/`shared_template_id`/`shared_definition_id`. The split (decision 2): a repository's name/url/branch/subpath, a template's playbook path/limit/extra vars/schema (and the shared repository it resolves against), and a check's key/plugin/params/interval are the installation's; the credential, the scope and SSH credential, and the binding plus enabled flag stay each organization's own. A shared definition therefore carries **no secret by construction** — proved directly by `internal/e2e/shared_resources_test.go`: two organizations (two independent users), each with its own deploy credential against the SAME shared repository, each running a real playbook through it, with every response org A's session can reach searched for org B's credential and host ids, and the reverse, finding neither. Creating a definition projects into every existing organization immediately and into every new one (`internal/app/app.go`'s `orgCreated` hook chain); editing propagates atomically; deleting a definition still in use, or naming it the same as an organization's own resource, is refused to an instance administrator only, naming the organization — never reachable from inside one (`internal/api/*shared*_test.go`'s leak tests, one per kind). Three cascading foreign keys turned up as the open door during review, closed each time: `job_triggers`/`git_repo_vault_credentials` on repositories, all three of `job_templates`' own dependants, and `check_definitions.group_id`/`host_id` — the last needed a database-level guard (migration 00063) because a customer deleting their own group or host must not silently destroy the provider's check, plus a follow-up guard reserving a shared projection's organization-wide slot so the customer cannot lock themselves out of their own delete. GUI: one instance-admin page (`SharedResourcesPage.tsx`, beside instance settings) manages all three kinds; `RepoDialog`, `TemplateDialog` and `CheckDialog` show the shared fields disabled and the bindings editable. Docs: `admin/shared-resources.md`, `user/playbooks-and-jobs.md`, `user/monitoring.md` (`NoiXdev/noix-docs`, branch `pulsix`). See the architecture decision above for why there is no provider entity |
| 2.91 | The run detail recomputes its resolved vault identity set instead of recording what the run received | done | Migration 00064 added nullable `jobs.resolved_vault_identities jsonb`. It is written once, at claim time (`internal/jobs/queue_sql.go`'s `claim`, via the new `RecordVaultIdentities` query), in the SAME SQL transaction as the `ClaimJob` UPDATE that flips the row to running — a claimed job can never be read back without its record already committed alongside it. The resolution itself (`resolveClaimVaultIdentities`) merges the repository's and template's vault identities exactly like the claim payload does, but never opens a secret — that stays strictly after commit, in `buildPayload`, so the row's lock is never held across a credential read. `internal/api/job_handlers.go`'s `runVaultIdentities` now reads the stored record when one exists instead of recomputing, and a new `vault_identities_source` field (`"recorded"` or `"computed"`) on the run detail DTO says which it is — `web/src/features/runs/RunDetailPage.tsx` shows the label beside the Vault identities card. NULL (`"computed"`, a live resolution) covers the three cases nothing was ever recorded for: a run still queued (today's configuration genuinely is the forecast), a run claimed before this migration existed, and a job cancelled before it was ever claimed — all three degrade the same way a deleted template or repository already did, rather than crashing the detail. The acceptance test (`TestRunDetailStillShowsTheClaimedVaultIdentitiesAfterARepoEdit`, `internal/api/job_handlers_test.go`) claims a run, edits the repository's vault identities afterward, and asserts the detail still shows the OLD set with `vault_identities_source: "recorded"` — confirmed to fail (showing the new, wrong set) with the fix reverted. `internal/jobs/queue_sql_test.go`'s `TestQueueClaimRecordsVaultIdentitiesImmuneToALaterRepoEdit` pins the same guarantee at the store level. Claiming a repo-backed run job also needed `internal/api/apitest`'s queue to carry a real `RepoSource`/`VaultResolver` for the first time (production's `internal/app.Build` always had them; the test harness never did, since no api-level test had claimed a repo playbook before). Docs: `user/playbooks-and-jobs.md` (`NoiXdev/noix-docs`, branch `pulsix`) |
| 2.92 | `repos.Service` still accepts `vault_credential_id` on write | done | `legacyVaultIdentities` is removed; `Create`/`Update` now refuse a non-nil `vault_credential_id` outright with `ErrInvalidInput` naming `vault_identities`. The field stays on the request body (so a client sending it gets a 400, not huma silently dropping an undeclared field) and the column stays (dropping it is 2.93, after a shipped release). `RepoDialog.tsx` no longer shows or sends the legacy field. Nothing is lost: every repository already has its link row from the bridge or migration 00059's backfill — proved by a test that builds that exact precondition through the store directly (the service can no longer produce it) and asserts the resolved identities survive a save. Review found `Update` was still assigning `next.VaultCredentialID = in.VaultCredentialID`, which validation now guarantees is `nil` — so the first save from the UI nulled the column 2.93 wants kept intact as evidence until a release has shipped. Fixed: `Update` now leaves `VaultCredentialID` at its current value, confirmed by a test that fails against the un-fixed assignment |
| 2.93 | Drop `git_repos.vault_credential_id` once a release has shipped with nothing reading it | idea | v0.2e final review. Migration 00059 backfilled the column's one repository-vault pairing into `git_repo_vault_credentials` under the label `"default"`, and nothing reads the column itself after that sub-project — the claim path (`internal/jobs`) moved to the labelled set. `repos.Service` still writes it on every create and update for compatibility (2.92 is that bridge). Migrations are additive-only, so the column stays until a release has shipped with 2.92 also gone, then an additive migration drops it |
| 2.94 | `repos.Service.List` and `templates.Service.List` fetch vault identities once per row | done | New `ListOrgRepoVaultIdentities`/`ListOrgVaultCredentials` queries fetch the whole organization's rows in one call each, grouped by repo/template id; both `List` methods call the bulk form once instead of looping. Neither `List` had a dedicated test before this; both now have one at the service layer (fakes) and the store layer (real Postgres, cross-org included) proving each row gets its own identities, not a sibling's |
| 2.95 | A repository's row write and its vault identity write are two separate transactions | done | v0.2e final review. `repos.Service.Create`/`Update` wrote the repository row, then called `ReplaceRepoVaultIdentities` as its own statement — each atomic alone, neither atomic with the other — so a failure between them (a dropped connection, a crashed manager) left a repository stored with none of the vault passwords the request named, while the caller held an error and would reasonably assume nothing was stored. The damage is invisible at save time: it first appears as a decryption failure on the next run, with nothing wrong-looking in the configuration. Both writes now share one transaction through a new `Store.InTx(ctx, orgID, fn)` port — the `setup.Store.InTx` pattern — implemented over `db.WithWriteTx`, which every store call made with the context it hands fn joins. **`internal/templates` had the identical split** from the identical v0.2e work (`Create`/`Update` then `ReplaceVaultCredentials`); this row named only repositories, but fixing one and leaving its twin next door would have made the row read closed while the hazard stayed. Both are fixed the same way. `TestARepositoryAndItsVaultIdentitiesLandTogetherOrNotAtAll` and `TestATemplateAndItsVaultCredentialsLandTogetherOrNotAtAll` run the REAL SQL store with one write made to fail, so the rollback under test is a real one; each was confirmed to fail (one surviving row) with the transaction removed. The fakes' `InTx` runs fn straight through and says so: a map-backed fake can model the service asking for a transaction, not what one does. Noted at both implementations: `InTx` does not nest, because `db.beginOrg` always begins a fresh pool transaction rather than joining one bound to the context (the same finding 2.87 turns on) |
| 2.96 | The Playwright `ansible` journey cannot run; the Go `internal/e2e` Ansible scenario does | idea | Confirmed while building 2.89's own end-to-end scenario. `web/e2e/ansible.spec.ts`'s `ansible` Playwright project skips itself because the worker image carrying the Ansible runtime is absent from the compose test stack (`e2e-ansible-ui` stays commented out in `.github/workflows/ci.yml`, blocked on Task 17 per that job's own notes) — so this sub-project's GUI (the repository's and template's vault identity editors, the run detail's resolved-set card) has no Playwright coverage, only Vitest component tests. The Go scenario is not in the same position: `e2e-ansible` in `ci.yml` runs on every PR, once against the deterministic fake ansible-runner and once with `PULSIX_E2E_REAL_ANSIBLE=1` against the real one, so `internal/e2e/vault_test.go`'s scenario is exercised for real there, decrypting two vault-encrypted files under two different passwords. Only the fake-runner pass runs without `ansible-runner` installed (e.g. local dev); that split is pre-existing and documented (`internal/e2e/ansible_test.go`'s own header comment), not something this sub-project introduced **The Playwright spec for the SCHEDULES page waits on this too (moved here from 2.68, 2026-10-25).** A schedule needs a job template, a template needs a playbook, a playbook needs a synced repository — and syncing is a job, so without the worker image there is nothing for a schedule to point at. The sibling pages were reachable around it: `workers.spec.ts` stops where a real worker would begin, and `triggers.spec.ts` hangs its webhook on a repository rather than a template |
| 2.104 | A cancelled playbook keeps running once `PULSIX_WORKER_PLAY_UID` is set | done | **Closed 2026-10-29: the open half is 2.107, which shipped (spec `docs/superpowers/specs/2026-10-29-cancel-stops-the-play-design.md`).** A cancel now stops the play and everything it started under the play uid, proven in the real worker image: `TestCancellingAPlayUnderThePlayUIDStopsIt` passes with only its `t.Skip` line deleted — the weaker signal, since its wait releases on `ansible-runner` and it can cancel before the `sleep` exists — and `TestACancelledPlayLeavesNoZombie` is the sleep-level proof: it waits for the `sleep` itself, then no play-uid process, live or zombie, remains within grace + 1 s. The text below is the state on 2026-10-28, kept as the record. **Half fixed, half open — this row's own premise turned out too narrow.** The uid-crossing half is fixed and proven, 2026-10-28, spec `docs/superpowers/specs/2026-10-28-cancel-across-the-uid-boundary-design.md`: the setuid helper (`deploy/worker/setuid-exec.c`) gained a `UID --signal SIGNAL PGID` mode that performs the same privilege drop as its exec mode and signals only after it, so a worker running as uid 65532 can now deliver `TERM`/`KILL` to a play running as 65533; `terminateGroup`'s `EPERM`-blind liveness probe — treating ANY `kill(-pid, 0)` error as "already gone" — is also gone, replaced by the worker's own `cmd.Wait()` as the authoritative answer to whether the child exited. `TestCancellingAPlayUnderThePlayUIDReportsCancelled` (`internal/e2e/uidboundary/cancel_test.go`) proves it inside the real worker image: the job now reaches `cancelled` in ~5.1 s, where before this fix it never reported within 30 s at all. **What stays open, and why this row does not close:** the signal reaches the worker's own `exec.Cmd` leader and nothing below it. `ansible-runner` invokes `ansible-playbook` through `pexpect.spawn()`, which pty-forks into a NEW process group as an unconditional property of attaching to a pty; `ansible-core`'s `WorkerProcess` isolates one level deeper, into another new group per host. The worker's `kill(-pgid, …)` — through the helper or direct — reaches only its own leader's group; the two nested groups below it, and everything they forked, are never signalled and run to completion. **This is independent of the uid work**: the identical escape reproduces with a plain `kill -TERM`/`kill -KILL` from the SAME uid, no helper, no `PULSIX_WORKER_PLAY_UID` at all — a pre-existing defect of every cancel that no prior test could see, because the suite's stand-in runner forks no nested group. Tracked as **2.107**, with the installed versions, source locations and observed pgids recorded there for the spec that fixes it. `TestCancellingAPlayUnderThePlayUIDStopsIt` (same file) asserts the play's processes are actually gone after a cancel and is `t.Skip`-ped today, naming 2.107; delete that one line to re-run it once 2.107 ships. An operator who cancels a destructive playbook under a play uid now gets an honest job status — but the play itself, and everything it started, keeps running until 2.107 closes it |
| 2.105 | The work-dir cleanup child inherits `PULSIX_ENROLL_TOKEN` | done | **Gates enabling 2.42.** `removeWorkDir` (`internal/worker/workdir.go`) runs `rm -rf` through the setuid helper as uid 65533 and leaves `cmd.Env` nil, so the child gets the worker's whole environment including every `PULSIX_*` variable. The run spawn strips them (`stripPrivateEnv`, `runner.go`); this path does not. The helper's final `execv` is a non-setuid binary with unchanged credentials, so the process is dumpable and its `/proc/<pid>/environ` is owned by 65533 — readable by anything the play left behind under that uid, and nothing reaps the play's stragglers outside the cancel path (which 2.104 shows does not work). Narrow window, and exactly the secret 2.42's own goals name. Fixed 2026-10-26: `removeAsPlayUID` sets `cmd.Env = stripPrivateEnv(os.Environ())`, the same strip the run spawn uses, and `TestRemoveWorkDirDoesNotHandThePlayUIDTheWorkersSecrets` pins both halves — no `PULSIX_*`/`ANSIBLE_*` reaches the child, and the rest of the environment survives, because a child with no `PATH` at all is a different program than the one the run spawn builds. These two are the only sites that spawn the setuid helper |
| 2.106 | Loose ends the playbook-uid whole-feature review deferred | done | Collected so they survive the plan's scratch directory. **The repo cache stays group-readable for ever** once a repo-backed job has run: `ShareRepoRead` takes the cache root and each org directory to `0750` and nothing reverts them, so every clone is readable to every later play (all plays are one uid). Probably inside the trust model within a zone, but nothing states or pins it — and `loop_test.go` still asserts the cache is `0700`, which is true only before the first repo job. **`~/.ansible/tmp` under `/home/pulsix-play` is job-crossing scratch** that lives for the container's lifetime and is never cleaned; the Dockerfile claims "the run's own scratch still lives in its work dir … and the dir is wiped afterwards" and `workdir.go` lists `~/.ansible/tmp` as a work-dir leftover — the new home is the counter-example to both. **`removeWorkDir` can block `runOne`'s defer for up to 30 s** per job, and therefore shutdown; it used to be a fast `RemoveAll`. **`share.go`'s ancestor loop does not skip symlinks** unlike the `WalkDir` beneath it — unreachable today since only the worker creates those paths, but it is the same hazard that walk explicitly guards against (see 2.68's own symlink finding). **`deploy/compose/.env.example` and `README.md` never mention `PULSIX_WORKER_PLAY_UID`**, so the documented off-switch is invisible to anyone working from the example file. **Closed 2026-10-29; two of the five turned out to be records rather than code.** The *repo cache* opening is permanent and now says so on `ShareRepoRead`, with the reasoning that it is the trust model and not a hole — all plays on one worker are the SAME uid, so reverting per job would buy a window, not a boundary, and the real boundary is a separate zone with its own worker; `loop_test.go`'s `0700` assertion keeps its value but now reads "before any repo-backed job has run" instead of "unchanged". *`~/.ansible/tmp`* stays — pointing `remote_tmp` at the work dir is not available (the same setting applies to real target hosts, where no such directory exists) and clearing it between jobs would delete a CONCURRENT job's scratch; both false comments are corrected, the Dockerfile's "the run's own scratch still lives in its work dir" and `workdir.go`'s listing it as a work-dir leftover, which it never was. *`removeWorkDir`'s 30 s* is now 10 s: the old number contradicted its own comment ("a second is already generous") and, running in `runOne`'s defer, was also how long one job could delay a worker SHUTDOWN. *`share.go`'s ancestor loop* now refuses a symlink instead of chmoding through it — and the hazard measured worse than the row claimed: Linux gives symlinks mode 0777, from which `chmodReadShared` computes **0757** and chmods the TARGET to world-rwx outside the cache, while Darwin's 0755 symlinks compute back to 0755 and the chmod never fires, so the defect is real exactly where the worker runs and invisible on a developer's machine. It refuses rather than skips, unlike the `WalkDir` below it, because an ancestor is part of the cache's own structure and skipping it would fail the run later with a permission error naming nothing. *`.env.example` and `README.md`* now carry the off-switch, what it costs, and that cancelling does not stop the playbook (2.107; since closed by 2.107) |
| 2.107 | A cancel's signal reaches only the worker's own process group; `ansible-runner`'s pty-fork and `ansible-core`'s own worker isolation put the play two groups deeper | done | **Closed 2026-10-29, spec `docs/superpowers/specs/2026-10-29-cancel-stops-the-play-design.md` (branch `fix/2.107-cancel-stops-the-play`).** A cancel now stops the play and everything it started, under a play uid or not, **on Linux workers** (elsewhere `PlaySupervisor` is empty and the direct spawn keeps the old behaviour; production workers are Linux). The worker no longer starts `ansible-runner` directly: it starts a per-run **play supervisor**, a hidden mode of the worker binary itself (`internal/worker/supervisor`; reserved first argument `__pulsix-play-supervisor`, dispatched by `cmd/worker` and by `cmd/manager` for the embedded worker; `worker.Config.PlaySupervisor` is `os.Executable()` on Linux, filled in by `worker.Load`, and empty — today's direct spawn — on every other OS and in hand-built configs). The supervisor marks itself a child subreaper, so every process the play orphans stays in its descendant chain. On `SIGTERM` (the cancel signal the worker already sent to the leader's group) it sends `TERM` to every descendant, repeats for new ones until the grace (5 s, `killGrace()`) runs out, then `KILL`s until none remain, reaps all of it and exits with the runner's own code, or re-raises the runner's death signal, so `summarize`'s mapping is unchanged. Signals go only through `pidfd_open` → re-check descent by the ppid chain → `pidfd_send_signal`, never `kill(pid)` or `kill(-pgid)`, so a recycled pid is never hit; and the walk is by descent, never by uid, so a concurrent job's play under the same uid is never touched (the hazard this row names). When a run ends on its own the supervisor stops any leftover descendants the same way, so a play that backgrounds a process no longer leaves it running into the next job. `stopGroup` (`internal/worker/runner/runner.go`) owns no escalation any more: its own `KILL` is a backstop 10 s after the grace (`defaultSupervisorBackstop`) and logs a warning naming the job. A supervisor that cannot do its job refuses on stderr with the prefix `pulsix-play-supervisor: refused: ` and `summarize` names it; anything it reports after the runner started (a stop that did not drain, a failed signal or reap) keeps the prefix `pulsix-play-supervisor: `, leaves the runner's status in place and is logged by the worker at warn level with `job_id` on every path, a cancel included. **Proof, inside the real worker image** (`internal/e2e/uidboundary`; 11/11 pass, no skip): `TestCancellingAPlayUnderThePlayUIDStopsIt` passes with only its `t.Skip` line deleted (0.26 s) — its wait releases on `ansible-runner`, so it cancels before the `sleep` exists and is the weaker signal. **The sleep-level proof of the nested-group escape** is `TestACancelledPlayLeavesNoZombie` (play uid; waits for the `sleep` itself, then no play-uid `/proc` entry, live or zombie, within grace + 1 s; 0.52 s) and `TestCancellingAPlayWithoutAPlayUIDStopsIt` (no play uid; waits for the `sleep`, which is gone within grace + 1 s; 0.59 s) — with `PlaySupervisor` blanked, both fail with the `sleep` still alive. `TestABackgroundProcessDoesNotOutliveItsRun` (0.70 s) pins the leftover sweep (the job reports `succeeded`, the background process is gone); `TestAFailingPlayStillReportsFailed` (0.41 s) pins that a failed run still reports failed. Unit tests on any OS pin the supervisor's policy (`TERM` to all, repeat, `KILL` after the grace, exit-status passthrough and signal re-raise, self-named failure) and `summarize`'s recognition of it. **The run view went back to the predecessor spec's design**: the cancelled-run warning shows only when a host was interrupted and says the command already started there may still be running on that host; the "no task in flight" pill is neutral, no longer green (`web/src/features/runs/RunDetailPage.tsx`). **What a cancel still cannot stop** is a command already running on a managed host: dropping the SSH connection does not kill the remote command. *Everything from here on is the finding as it stood on 2026-10-28/29, before the fix, and is kept as the record of why the design is what it is.* Found 2026-10-28 proving 2.104's cross-uid fix inside the real worker image (`internal/e2e/uidboundary/cancel_test.go`'s `TestCancellingAPlayUnderThePlayUIDStopsIt`; this row IS the evidence — the versions, source locations and observed pgids below were gathered from that proof run, and the spec `docs/superpowers/specs/2026-10-28-cancel-across-the-uid-boundary-design.md` records the same finding in its "As built" section). `stopGroup` (`internal/worker/runner/runner.go`) signals the process group of the worker's own `exec.Cmd` — the `ansible-runner` leader it started — and that signal never reaches two deeper, independently-created groups below it. **ansible-runner 2.4.3** (`ansible_runner/runner.py:286`, inside `Runner.run()`'s `pexpect` branch — the only branch pulsix's `ansible-runner run <dir> --json --playbook <path> --inventory <path> --ident <id>` invocation ever takes, per `ansible_runner/config/command.py`'s `CommandConfig._set_runner_mode`, ~line 86): `pexpect.spawn()` pty-forks `ansible-playbook` into a NEW session/process group as an unconditional property of attaching to a fresh pty, not configurable through any flag `ansible-runner run` exposes. **ansible-core 2.21.4** (`ansible/executor/process/worker.py`'s `WorkerProcess._detach()`, ~line 155-160): forks one `WorkerProcess` per active host per task, unconditionally, and isolates it into yet another new process group (`os.setpgid(0, 0)`, or `os.setsid()` under `WORKER_SESSION_ISOLATION`). `TaskQueueManager`'s own `SIGTERM` handler (`ansible/executor/task_queue_manager.py`, ~line 216) cascades correctly to each worker's group — it is simply never triggered, because the process holding it is never the one the worker's `kill(-pgid, …)` reaches. Observed nesting from a failing run (`/proc/<pid>/stat`'s pgrp field): worker leader `pid=14 pgrp=14` (`ansible-runner`, what `stopGroup` signals, and does reach — across the uid boundary, inside the 5 s grace); `pid=16 pgrp=16` (`ansible-playbook`, the pexpect split); `pid=23 pgrp=23` (`ansible-playbook`, the `WorkerProcess` split); `pid=39/40/41/42 pgrp=23` (shell/python/`sleep`, the actual play command) — none of pgrp 16 or 23 are members of group 14, so none are signalled. Cross-checked as having nothing to do with uid separation: the identical two-level escape reproduces with a plain `kill -TERM`/`kill -KILL` against a process group started and run as ONE uid, no setuid helper, no `PULSIX_WORKER_PLAY_UID` configured at all — the leader dies, its two descendant groups are reparented to init and keep running their `sleep 300` untouched. **The obvious fix carries a hazard of its own, which is why this needs a spec rather than a patch:** discovering the real process group(s) to signal by the play's **uid** at cancel time would also catch a CONCURRENT job's play running under the same play uid on the same worker — today's one-job-per-worker design does not forbid that from ever changing, and signalling by uid is the wrong primitive on its face regardless. Discovery has to walk by **descendancy** from the worker's own leader pid instead — but a process already reparented to init, exactly what `ansible-playbook` becomes the moment its immediate parent's group is killed (observed above), escapes a descendancy walk too, which is the identical shape of the leftover this defect produces. `TestCancellingAPlayUnderThePlayUIDStopsIt` asserts the play's processes are gone after a cancel, unweakened from the version that first caught this; it is `t.Skip`-ped today naming this row — delete that one line once a fix lands. **That claim is verified as of 2026-10-29**, after 2.108's test half: with the skip line deleted, the in-image suite fails on exactly one assertion — `cancel_test.go`'s "a process with uid 65533 still exists 30s after cancelling" — and on live processes only: a reparented `ansible-playbook` (`PPid: 1`, state `S`) and the `sh`/`sh`/`python3.13`/`sleep 300` chain below it, while the job itself reports `cancelled` and no work dir survives. Before 2.108 the same run would also have failed on the previous test's zombies, whatever a fix achieved |
| 2.108 | The worker is PID 1 in its container and never reaps what a play orphans | done | **Closed 2026-10-29, both halves** (spec `docs/superpowers/specs/2026-10-29-cancel-stops-the-play-design.md`). *Production half:* `deploy/compose/docker-compose.yml`'s `worker` service now has `init: true`, with a comment naming this row, so Docker's init is PID 1, execs the worker as its child and reaps whatever is orphaned into it; the admin docs (`admin/installation/docker-compose.md` and `admin/configuration/workers-and-zones.md` in the docs repo) say to pass `--init` to a plain `docker run`. It is deliberately not baked into the image's `ENTRYPOINT`: the compose file overrides the entrypoint and would bypass it silently. The decision this row left to 2.107's design came out as both, in layers: the per-run supervisor of 2.107 is a subreaper between the worker and `ansible-runner`, so a play's orphans stay findable by descent and are reaped by the supervisor, nothing of a play reaches PID 1 any more; `init: true` catches what still can (a repository sync's `ssh` outliving its context-killed `git`, a supervisor the worker had to `KILL` as its backstop). `TestACancelledPlayLeavesNoZombie` (`internal/e2e/uidboundary`, 0.52 s) asserts no play-uid `/proc` entry, live or zombie, within grace + 1 s of a cancel. *Test half:* fixed the same day it was found (see below). *Finding as it stood, before the fix:* Found 2026-10-29 running `reapPlayUIDLeftovers` (`internal/e2e/uidboundary/cancel_test.go`) for the first time: its `KILL` through the setuid helper worked, and every play process it killed — `ansible-playbook`, two `sh`, `python3.13`, `sleep` — stayed behind as a **zombie** with `PPid: 1`. In that test PID 1 is the test binary (`docker run --entrypoint /uid-boundary.test`, no `--init`), which waits only for children it started itself. **Production has the same shape:** `deploy/compose/docker-compose.yml`'s `worker` service ends its entrypoint in `exec /pulsix-worker` with no `init: true`, and the image's own `ENTRYPOINT ["/pulsix-worker"]` does the same for a plain `docker run`, so the worker binary is PID 1 and inherits every process a play orphans. Go's `os/exec` reaps only the `exec.Cmd` it started (`internal/worker/runner/runner.go`, the single `cmd.Wait()`); nothing in the worker waits for a pid it did not start. Every cancelled run orphans its play (2.107: the pty-forked `ansible-playbook` and the `WorkerProcess` below it are reparented the moment the `ansible-runner` leader dies), and each of those processes becomes a permanent zombie the moment it exits — on the cancel's `KILL` once 2.107 is fixed, or when the play finishes on its own today. A zombie holds no memory or files, only its pid and process-table slot, so this is a slow leak bounded by the container's pid limit rather than an outage, but it grows with every cancel for the container's lifetime and makes `ps` inside the worker lie about what is running. **The test half is fixed** the same day: `playUIDProcessExists` and `playUIDProcessGroups` count only live processes (`isLiveUnderUID` in `internal/e2e/uidboundary/proc_test.go`, which treats state `Z` and `X` as gone), and `reapPlayUIDLeftovers` now fails its test on a live survivor instead of logging it — it had been reporting "survived" for 30 s after a `KILL` that had worked, while the test around it passed. **The production half belongs to 2.107's design**, not a patch of its own: `init: true` in compose (or `tini` in the image) would reap orphans but leave a non-compose deployment unfixed and the play no easier to find; the worker becoming a reaper itself (`PR_SET_CHILD_SUBREAPER` is implied for PID 1; a `SIGCHLD` loop around `wait4(-1)` would race the runner's own `cmd.Wait()`) needs to know which pids are its own; and a per-job subreaper between the worker and `ansible-runner` would both keep a cancelled play's orphans findable by descendancy — the hazard 2.107 names — and reap them. Decide there |
| 2.109 | `TestSetuidHelperSignalModeContract/no_signal_without_the_drop` fails when the runner tests run as root with a C compiler | done | **Fixed 2026-11-02.** Every case whose premise root does not share now skips as root and says why. `no signal without the drop` skips when the effective uid is 0 (root needs no drop to signal). `TestLoadPlayUID/own uid` skips under uid 0, which `zero is root` already covers with its own message. The three read-only-directory cases of renew_writeprobe_test.go call `skipIfStillWritable` after their chmod 0500 and skip when the directory still takes writes. Their old probe ran before the chmod and could never trigger. Checked as root in `golang:1.27.1-alpine` with no network: internal/worker and internal/worker/runner pass, the four cases report SKIP; that image has no gcc, so the setuid case does not run there at all. As an ordinary user nothing changes. Finding: Found 2026-10-29 running the runner package's tests inside `golang:1.27.1-alpine` with `gcc` installed (as root, which is what a container gives you). The case (`internal/worker/runner/setuidhelper_c_test.go`) compiles the setuid helper and runs it as a non-setuid binary expecting the refusal `not setuid-root`; as root, the helper does not refuse, because root needs no privilege drop to signal, so the stated expectation is wrong in that environment. Pre-existing and independent of the play supervisor (it reproduces on `main`); on a developer's machine, and in CI where the tests run as an ordinary user, the case passes. The same root-in-container run also fails `internal/worker`'s `TestLoadPlayUID` (`own_uid`) and the three read-only-directory cases of `renew_writeprobe_test.go`, for the same reason (root ignores the permissions they rely on); all of them pass in the same image under `docker run --user 65534:65534 -e HOME=/tmp -e GOCACHE=/tmp/gocache`, checked 2026-10-30, which is the cheaper way to run the Linux tests until the cases skip as root. **Fix:** skip the case when the test runs as uid 0 and say why in the skip message, or run the helper under an unprivileged uid. Low priority; it only matters to someone running the suite as root in a container |
| 2.110 | `golangci-lint` 2.15.0 reports 26 errcheck findings on `main` that older versions do not | done | **Decided and fixed 2026-11-02: fix the findings, move the pin.** All 26 now handle the received error instead of dropping it. The tests check it (`if err := <-done; err != nil { t.Errorf(...) }`; the two rules ticker tests expect `context.Canceled`, which Ticker.Run returns on purpose). `cmd/manager`'s early-return case puts the error in its failure message. In `internal/agent/loop.go` the scheduler's end is logged as a warning when it carries an error, and the drained receive's error as a debug line. An explicit `_ = <-ch` was tried first and rejected: staticcheck's S1005 flags it, so errcheck and staticcheck agree only on real handling. `golangci-lint run --max-issues-per-linter 0 --max-same-issues 0` reports 0 issues with 2.15.0, and ci.yml's pin moves to v2.15.0 (its stale v2.13.2 comment corrected). Finding: Found 2026-10-29: `golangci-lint` 2.15.0 (the version installed locally; `go1.27.3` toolchain) reports **26** `errcheck` findings on `main` — 13 in `internal/worker/loop_test.go`, 8 in `internal/agent/checks/scheduler_test.go`, 2 in `internal/rules/ticker_test.go`, 1 in `cmd/manager/main_test.go` (mostly `<-done` or `<-errC` on a `chan error`) and 2 non-test ones, `internal/agent/loop.go:239` and `:373` — where older versions reported none. (Run with `--max-issues-per-linter 0 --max-same-issues 0`: the default caps hide all but a few.) CI pins `v2.14.0` (`.github/workflows/ci.yml`; its comment above the step still says v2.13.2), so CI is green and the findings only show to someone running a newer linter locally; the branch `fix/2.107-cancel-stops-the-play` is gated as "no findings beyond these 26". **Needs a decision**, not a quick edit: either fix the 26 (receive into `_` or assert the error in the tests, handle it in `internal/agent/loop.go`) so the repo is clean under both versions, or keep the pin and write down that a newer linter reports them. Whichever is chosen, the day CI's pin moves the findings arrive all at once |
| 2.111 | The embedded worker's container has no init | done | **Closed 2026-10-30:** `deploy/compose/docker-compose.yml` gives the `manager` service `init: true` too, `deploy/compose/README.md`'s embedded worker section says why and asks for `--init` on a plain `docker run`, and the docs' built-in worker section (noix-docs `pulsix` 2d194bc) says the same. Found 2026-10-29 closing 2.108. `deploy/compose/docker-compose.yml` gives the `worker` service `init: true`, but not the `manager` service, which hosts the embedded worker when `PULSIX_EMBEDDED_WORKER=1` (off by default; `internal/app/embedded_worker.go`). There the manager binary is PID 1, so the embedded worker keeps 2.108's residual orphan paths: a repository sync's `ssh` outliving its context-killed `git`, and the tree of a play supervisor the worker had to `KILL` as its backstop. They become zombies for the container's lifetime. A play's own orphans are not affected: the per-run supervisor (2.107) reaps them for the embedded worker too, since `cmd/manager` dispatches the supervisor argument. **Fix:** `init: true` on `manager` (it changes nothing else for the manager) and a line in the admin docs' built-in worker section, or decide the embedded worker is trusted-single-tenant-only enough not to matter. Low priority while the embedded worker stays off by default |
| 2.112 | An unsupported host shows only at the first playbook job, not at worker start | done | **Closed 2026-10-30:** `cmd/worker` calls `supervisor.Probe` right after loading its configuration whenever it will run plays under the supervisor (`Config.PlaySupervisor` set, i.e. on Linux), and refuses to start as a configuration error (exit 2) with the probe's refusal in its log. The probe (`newSystemWith`, `internal/worker/supervisor/system_linux.go`) now checks both calls: it opens a pidfd on itself and sends it signal 0, since a seccomp profile can allow `pidfd_open` and block `pidfd_send_signal`, which the supervisor would otherwise first need at a cancel. Refusing rather than warning follows `worker.Load`'s own rule that a worker which cannot supervise must not look healthy; it costs such a worker its connection tests, which it could still run. Left: the embedded worker is not probed (the manager image has no Ansible runtime, so it runs no plays, and the manager must not refuse to start over it), and nothing reports the host to the manager, so the operator sees an offline worker and its log, not a reason in the GUI. End-user docs: noix-docs `pulsix` ebdf69a. Found 2026-10-29 in the whole-branch review of 2.107. The play supervisor needs pidfds (`pidfd_open`, `pidfd_send_signal`): a kernel 5.3 or newer, and a container runtime whose seccomp profile allows them (Docker's default profile allows them from 20.10). Without them the supervisor refuses, by name, and every playbook job on that worker fails with that refusal as its error. Nothing fails earlier: the worker enrolls, connects, reports itself healthy and claims jobs. **Planned:** probe at worker start (open a pidfd on the worker itself, as `newSystemWith` does in `internal/worker/supervisor/system_linux.go`) and make an unsupported host show at boot, in the worker's log and ideally in what it reports to the manager, so an operator sees it before the first job. The host requirement is in the end-user installation docs from this branch on |
| 2.113 | A version-pinned worker keeps the old Cancel while the run view stops warning | done | **Closed 2026-10-30, as a capability rather than a version comparison:** the worker says on every claim whether a cancel stops the play on it (`ClaimJobRequest.cancel_stops_play`, true exactly when it runs plays under the play supervisor, so a `dev` build or a worker off Linux answers truthfully), and the `ClaimJob` UPDATE records the answer on the job (`jobs.cancel_stops_play`, migration 00072). Recording it at claim time answers the second open point: a worker upgraded after the run cannot change what its run says. NULL (never claimed, claimed in process or before the migration, or cleared by the reaper's and a revocation's requeue, whose first worker's play may still be running, see 2.57) reads as false. The run detail returns it as `cancel_stops_play`, and the run view shows the pre-2.107 warning about every host whenever it is not true, the interrupted-host warning only when it is. A cancelled run that was never claimed therefore also gets the stronger warning, as it did before 2.107; telling that case apart by `started_at` would hide a requeued run whose first play may still run. End-user docs: noix-docs `pulsix` dbc3a65. Found 2026-10-29 in the whole-branch review of 2.107. The Add worker dialog's snippets (`workerSnippets`, `internal/api/worker_snippets.go`) pin `noixdev/pulsix-worker:<manager version at enrollment>`. After a manager upgrade, a worker added that way keeps its old image, and with it the old Cancel: the job reports `cancelled` while the play keeps running. The run view no longer warns about that (2.107 took the warning back to "a host was interrupted"), and the docs say Cancel stops the run. The docs now say Cancel stops the play only on workers at this release or newer, and to update pinned worker images after upgrading; that is a sentence an operator has to find. **Decision to make:** gate the run view's cancelled-run wording on the version the worker already reports to the manager (the worker sends its build version on every claim, and the manager stores it on the worker): a run cancelled on a worker older than the 2.107 release would keep the old, stronger warning. Open points: where the cut-over version lives (one constant beside the run view, or a capability the worker reports instead of a version comparison, which also covers `dev` builds), and whether the job row has to record the worker's version at claim time, since a worker can be upgraded between the run and the moment someone reads it |
| 2.114 | Loose ends the cancel-stops-the-play whole-branch review deferred | done | **Closed 2026-11-02: two fixed, the rest kept as records.** Fixed: `parseArgs` refuses a repeated `--grace` like every other deviation (TestParseArgsRefuses/repeated grace), and the runner package's new TestMain removes the play supervisor its Linux tests build (checked in a Linux container: no `pulsix-play-supervisor-*` dir left). Kept as they are, each a choice rather than a defect: a pid first seen in the last TERM pass gets only the remaining grace before KILL, because the grace bounds the whole stop; the backstop is the point. `raise` stays in `system_*.go` beside the other platform calls. The routing tests' one stderr line and the session-escape fake are noted in the tests. `cancelRun`'s duplication and the hand-mirrored `stopGrace` stay until those tests change for another reason. Acceptance 4's exit-4 and extra-vars cases stay covered by TestSupervisedExitCodesPassThrough and `summarize`'s unit tests. `TestLinuxSystemFindsOrphanedGrandchild`'s waitFor has not flaked in the runs since (watch item). Finding: Collected so they survive the plan's scratch directory (spec `docs/superpowers/specs/2026-10-29-cancel-stops-the-play-design.md`).
