None changes behaviour a user can see. **`parseArgs` accepts a repeated `--grace`** (the last wins) where it refuses every other deviation; the worker is its only caller and never repeats it. **Pids first seen in the last `TERM` pass are `KILL`ed without a full grace**: `stopTree`'s `TERM` phase ends at the deadline, so a process forked moments before it gets only the remaining slice. **`raise` lives in `system_*.go`** rather than a `raise_*.go` of its own. **`TestLinuxSystemFindsOrphanedGrandchild`'s `waitFor` closure can `t.Fatal`** if the `sleep` exits mid-poll; watch it for flakes. **The routing tests print one supervisor refusal line to real stderr.** **The escape fake is a session escape, not a double fork** (`writeFakeRunner`'s ESCAPE and DAEMON modes); the in-image tests cover the real nested groups. **The Linux runner tests leave the built supervisor binary in `os.MkdirTemp`** (`playSupervisor`, `supervised_linux_test.go`; the package has no `TestMain` to clean up). **`cancelRun` duplicates the harness inlined in `TestCancellingAPlayUnderThePlayUIDReportsCancelled` and `...StopsIt`**, and the in-image `stopGrace` mirrors `defaultKillGrace` by hand. **Acceptance 4's exit-4 and extra-vars cases have no in-image test** (they are covered by `TestSupervisedExitCodesPassThrough` and `summarize`'s unit tests) |
| 2.115 | The worker logs `skipping malformed runner event … invalid character 'W'` in most in-image runs | done | **Fixed 2026-11-02.** The warning now carries a bounded, redacted prefix of the line (`line_prefix`, at most 120 bytes; redacted before the cut so a secret cannot straddle it). That named the line at once: ansible-core's `[WARNING]: Failed to create the directory PosixPath('<work dir>/home/.ansible'): [Errno 2] No such file or directory`, printed to stdout. The worker points HOME at `<work dir>/home`, but only repository-backed runs ever created that directory (for the fetch's ssh_config). `materializeRunnerConfig` now creates it for every run, 0700 like the rest of the work dir until ShareWorkDir opens it to the play's group. In the worker image the in-image suite went from 12 such warnings to none. Tests: TestMalformedRunnerLineIsNamedRedacted, TestRunnerConfigCreatesTheRunsHome, and TestLocalhostPlayRunsUnderThePlayUID now fails if a run prints a line that is not an event. Finding: Found 2026-10-29 building 2.107, and pre-existing: it appeared in the first in-image run on `main` (`b19c794`) before any of that work. `streamEvents` (`internal/worker/runner/runner.go`) warns once per stdout line of `ansible-runner` that is not a JSON event; the offending line starts with `W`, and what writes it has not been looked at (a warning printed to stdout instead of stderr is the likely shape). It changes no status and no assertion, but a warning in every run teaches an operator to ignore the worker's warnings. **To do:** capture the line itself (log a bounded, redacted prefix of it instead of only the decode error), find what writes it, and either keep it off stdout or recognise and drop it quietly |
| 2.97 | Go and the browser reject the same vault labels but for different reasons on U+0085 and U+FEFF | done | `vaultlabel.Invalid` and `invalidVaultLabel` now share one merged message for both the trim check and the Cc/Cf check (a tagless-switch multi-value case in Go, an `||` in TypeScript), so the report depends only on whether a label is rejected, never on which half of the merged check — or which language's trim() — did the rejecting. A new test in each pins that NEL and the BOM, at every position, produce byte-for-byte the same message |
| 2.98 | `proto/buf.yaml`'s `breaking: use: WIRE_JSON` drops source-level checks for every other message in the package | done | Revisited and kept: recorded directly in `buf.yaml`'s own comment, next to the original decision, so the next reader finds the reasoning without a backlog row. The proto is still one service and a dozen messages a reviewer reads in full for any change; worth a second look once that stops being true, not before. No code change |
| 2.99 | `alert_routes.sql`'s replace comment says the DELETE commits before the INSERT, inside a transaction where it does not | done | Both comments (`DeleteRouteSeverities` and `InsertRouteSeverities`) now say what the design actually guarantees: the DELETE is *ordered* before the INSERT (why two statements, not one data-modifying `WITH`), but the two commit together when `replaceSelections`' own transaction does — not that either commits first. Comment-only; no behavior change, no test |
| 2.100 | A shared check definition's params editor only covers the `service` plugin's unit list | superseded | Superseded by 2.101, which names this exact gap (a shared `disk` check's `mounts` editor) as one of the two editors it covers, plus the `extra_vars`/`extra_vars_schema` editor this row did not mention. Closed here rather than tracked in two rows for one piece of work |
| 2.101 | `SharedResourcesPage.tsx`'s dialogs have no editor for a shared template's extra vars or a shared `disk` check's mounts | done | v0.2f whole-branch review, and 2.100 folded in. A prior fix made both dialogs ROUND-TRIP the stored value through an edit that does not touch it, closing the silent-data-loss bug; neither was EDITABLE, so an instance admin had to set these through the API and then never open that definition in the GUI again without re-sending them by hand. The docs already described both as part of what a shared definition carries — the GUI was the thing behind. **Templates:** `extra_vars_schema` through `SchemaBuilder` (nested, the compact form `TemplateDialog` already uses) and `extra_vars` through `SchemaForm`, seeded from the stored values. `SchemaBuilder` gained an optional `types` prop so the shared dialog can withhold `secret`: the server refuses a secret-typed field on a shared definition by construction (`schemaHasSecretField` — the values are written into every organization's own column unsealed), and offering a type whose save can never succeed is worse than not offering it. **Checks:** a `mounts` textarea for the `disk` plugin, same shape as the existing `service` units list, with `CheckDialog`'s own contract that an empty list means every mount. **A finding beyond the row:** `validateTemplateDefinitionInput` ran only `ansiblevars.ForbiddenIn`, which looks at top-level names — the same gap 2.71 closed for an organization's own templates, and worse here, because a shared definition's extra vars are written straight into EVERY organization's `job_templates.extra_vars`: one malformed `__ansible` marker would break a run in each of them with a parse error in a document none of their operators wrote. `CheckMarkers` now runs there too. Docs: `admin/shared-resources.md` gained the secret-field rule, which was never stated |
| 2.102 | `ListCredentialUsage` never counted the labelled vault identity link tables | done | Found during the 2.76/2.92/2.94/2.97/2.99 triage, not named by an existing row. The query counted `git_repos.credential_id`/`.vault_credential_id` but never `git_repo_vault_credentials` or `job_template_vault_credentials` (migration 00059) — the exact class of gap the query's own comment already describes closing, reopened one layer down by v0.2e's labelled identities. A vault credential used only through a labelled identity read as unused in the GUI and then refused to delete on the link tables' own foreign keys (`store_sql.go` already mapped that violation to `ErrInUse` — the count was what lied, not the delete). `Repos` now folds in `git_repo_vault_credentials`, deduplicated by repository (a repository carrying both the legacy slot and a matching labelled identity for the same credential — the normal shape for anything saved before 2.92 — counts once); a new `Templates` field counts `job_template_vault_credentials` separately, since a template-only reference is not a repository one |
| 2.103 | `agent.LoadIdentity` reports "absent" and "corrupt" identically, and `serialOf` prints the result as `<nil>` | done | Found 2026-10-22 triaging an `internal/e2e` failure; closed 2026-10-23, and the underlying race the row listed as unestablished turned out to be the actual cause. **(a) and (b):** `LoadIdentity` keeps collapsing "the files are not there" and "they are there and do not parse" into `(nil, nil)` — the enrollment path depends on a corrupt identity re-enrolling rather than wedging the agent — and `LoadIdentityDetail` now returns the same thing plus a reason for a human, `""` when the identity is usable. The reason is explicitly not for branching on. **The product side, which the row did not name:** `Loop.Run` logged `"no valid agent identity; enrollment path"` identically on a first start and on a truncated certificate file, so an operator watching an agent re-enrol repeatedly had no signal at all; it now carries the reason, including the third case `LoadIdentityDetail` does not speak to (an identity that loaded and is simply no longer valid). **The race, now ESTABLISHED:** `agentapi`'s `Service.Enroll` calls `Agents.Register` INSIDE the RPC, before it returns the signed certificate, so the manager lists the agent strictly before the agent has the response — let alone before `SaveEnrollment` has written its four files. `waitForAgent` was therefore satisfied while the data dir was still empty, and `serialOf`'s single read found nothing. That is exactly how `TestAgentEnrollmentGate` failed under the parallel group and passed alone. `serialOf` now waits, bounded, and reports the last reason if the directory never fills. The four-writes-not-atomic-together observation stands but was not needed: the window that bit was the one BEFORE the first write. The renewal path remains ruled out |

## v0.3 · Agent & monitoring

| # | Item | Status | Notes |
|---|------|--------|-------|
| 3.1 | Agent binary: Linux amd64/arm64 (systemd) | done | v0.3a: `cmd/agent` + `internal/agent` — first-start enrollment, one mTLS stream, heartbeat 30 s, facts at connect and every 6 h, 1 s→60 s reconnect backoff; `make agent-binaries` cross-compiles both arches into the manager's embedded dist |
| 3.2 | Agent binary: Windows service | planned | |
| 3.3 | Agent binary: macOS launchd | planned | |
| 3.4 | Enrollment: one-time token, install script, mTLS cert issued by manager CA | done | v0.3a: `pxa1` tokens (bound to a host or creating one, 1–100 uses, expiry), `GET /install.sh` templated with the manager's public URL + `GET /agents/bin/{os}/{arch}` with a sha256 beside it; the token carries the CA fingerprint the agent pins its first connection against; 90-day certificate renewed at 2/3 lifetime. Agents dial the MANAGER directly, not a zone worker — the relay is v0.6 (spec §8 delta) |
| 3.4a | E2E: agent enrollment, monitoring pipeline (threshold → alert → notify → resolve), remote checks | in progress | milestone gate — three of four parts done. Enrollment in v0.3a (`internal/e2e/agent_test.go`: both token flows, facts + auto tags, renewal, revocation; `web/e2e/agent.spec.ts`: the operator journey). The **measurement** pipeline in v0.3b-1 (`internal/e2e/checks_test.go`: the seed set reaches an enrolled agent, an edited interval re-times the running agent fast enough that its enrolment-time configuration could not have produced the readings observed, those readings appear as current values and as graph points, and a monitoring group's definition beats the organization's for a tagged host; it asserts on `cpu` only, never on `service`, because the container provides no systemd). The **threshold → state → resolve** half in v0.3b-2 (`internal/e2e/rules_test.go`: a host-bound check of its own reaches `warn` the instant its threshold is crossed, `crit` only once the deterioration has held for the rule's own `for_duration`, `ok` again the moment a recovering value applies — no ripening on the way down — and the agent falling silent altogether reaches `unknown` once three of its check's own intervals have passed; `web/e2e/fleet.spec.ts` puts one host in each of the four states on the fleet page and filters down to just them). What "alert" meant when this row's title was written is now `warn`/`crit` — a judged state, not yet a notification. Still missing: **notify**, which needs v0.4's channels, and remote checks |
| 3.5 | Agent local config `agent.yaml`: secrets by name, static local checks | planned | secrets never sent to manager; untouched by v0.3b-1 — the agent takes its whole check configuration from the manager's stream and reads no local check file |
| 3.6 | Check definitions pushed from GUI to agent, secret references by name | in progress | v0.3b-1 shipped the push half: an operator's edit in the GUI resolves that host's set, stamps it with a version and pushes it over the open agent stream, and every heartbeat carries the version the agent is running so a missed push heals on the next one (`internal/checks` resolver, `internal/agentapi` delivery, `internal/agent/checks` scheduler). The secret half is untouched — no check parameter references a secret by name yet (needs 3.5) |
| 3.6a | Check engine: plugins in code with declared parameter schema | planned | cpu, memory, disk, service, process, smart, docker, db, web, http, tcp, icmp, tls, dns, snmp, script. Four of these exist as agent code after v0.3b-1 (see 3.7/3.8), but the row's actual subject — a plugin that *declares* its parameter schema, its metrics and its location, so the GUI and the validator read one source — does not: v0.3b-1 validates parameters with a hand-written per-plugin function in `internal/checks` and the SPA's form knows the four shapes by hand |
| 3.6b | Check definitions as data: plugin + params + interval + binding (host / monitoring group / org) | in progress | v0.3b-1: `check_definitions` (migration 00043) with key, plugin, params, interval, enabled, origin and a binding to the organization, a monitoring group (`monitoring_groups`, migration 00042 — a named, saved host-tag selector with a priority) or a host; resolution is per key with host > group > org, group priority then age breaking ties, and the host page names the winning scope. Both the definitions and the groups are editable in the GUI. What is missing: only **Linux base** is seeded (four rows: cpu, memory, disk, service) — "PostgreSQL", "Windows base", "macOS base", "Web server", "Docker host", "Endpoint" need their plugins first |
| 3.6c | Discovery of items (mounts, services, interfaces, containers) with per-item overrides | done | v0.3b-3 (`internal/items`, migration 00048): discovered items live in `check_items` — one row per (host, check_key, item) the evaluation ticker's own `Load` inserts on every tick (`ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING`, `first_seen` never moves), covering mounts, service units and anything else a plugin reports with a non-empty item name; a row is never deleted, so a mount gone for two weeks and back is still the same item. An operator can decide one item — or a `*` pattern of them — should not be judged: a decision (`check_item_decisions`) binds at the organization or a single host, names an exact item or a glob, says `ignore` or `watch`, and a host decision always outranks an organization one (`host` beats `org`, then `exact` beats `glob`, then the older `created_at` — the same strict total order `rules.Resolve` already uses). Ignoring drops the tuple's stored state so a `crit` does not sit unrefreshed, but never touches what the agent is asked to measure — the reading keeps arriving, only the verdict stops. The Items tab (`web/src/features/checks/ItemsPage.tsx`) lists every decision with a pattern editor that previews its match count before anything is saved; the host page's own monitoring card offers a one-click ignore/watch per row. `internal/e2e/items_test.go` and `web/e2e/items.spec.ts` prove the arc end to end: a mount crossing a threshold reaches `crit`, ignoring it returns the host to `ok` with the tuple carrying no state, watching it again returns it to `crit`. **Half of this row's own text had already shipped in v0.3b-2, unmarked at the time**: a rule has bound to `(host, check_key, item)` since 3.6d (`rules.ScopeItem`), item already beat host beat group beat organization there too, and "new items inherit rules" — this row's own original note — was never a feature v0.3b-3 needed to build: `rules.Resolve` judges a tuple by its `(check, item)` regardless of whether anything had "discovered" that item yet, so an item nobody had decided anything about was always implicitly watched under whatever rule already governed it (the design spec's own "What is already built" section says so in as many words). What this row's title actually named and v0.3b-3 is what built it: a record of which items exist at all, and the operator's ability to say one should *not* be judged |
| 3.6d | Rule engine: metric, operator, value, duration → state; inheritance plugin → org → group → host → item | done | v0.3b-2: `internal/rules`' AST (`ast.go`) and metric catalogue (`catalog.go`, eleven entries — see the design doc's As built section for the three `cpu.load*` ones the original spec table omitted), a recursive-descent parser (`parse.go`) and a pure evaluator (`evaluate.go`, `states.go`) turn one host's measured tuples and its effective rules into a state per tuple (`ok`/`warn`/`crit`/`unknown`) and a per-host roll-up. Precedence resolves per (tuple, severity) independently, narrowest scope wins (item > host > group > org, `resolve.go`), and the effective view (`GET /hosts/{id}/effective-rules`) names every rule a winner shadowed. A 15 s ticker (`ticker.go`) claims dirty-or-due hosts `FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED`, so several managers stay safe with no claim protocol of their own |
| 3.6e | Rule editor: form mode + expression mode (same rule model) | done | v0.3b-2: `web/src/features/checks/RuleDialog.tsx`, `RuleForm.tsx` and `RuleExpression.tsx`. A form-mode threshold compiles through `rules.Form.Compile`; an expression compiles through `rules.Parse`; both produce the same `Node` tree the evaluator reads, so neither editor is a second implementation of the rule language. Switching form → expression is one-way, except that the button back to form mode stays offered as long as the edited expression still fits the form's own subset (`AsForm`). A seeded rule's metric, scope and severity are fixed (copy it to change them); its threshold, clear condition, duration and enabled flag are not. `POST /rules/validate` — a route beyond the plan's own table, see the design doc's As built section — backs the editor's live validation without persisting anything, proven by a test that a validate call leaves the rule list untouched |
| 3.6f | Hysteresis / flap protection, unknown state on missing data | done | v0.3b-2: hysteresis is a rule's own clear condition (`Rule.Clear`) — a tuple standing at warn or crit holds that state until the clear predicate is true, not merely until the trigger predicate is false, and only against an improvement, never against an escalation (`evaluate.go`'s `decide`, step 3). Flapping is a per-tuple flag set at five state changes inside a rolling 30-minute window and cleared below three, counting the change the current evaluation is about to write so a tuple's fifth change flags on the very tick that produced it. `unknown` is reached three of a check's own resolved intervals after its last reading (`staleAt`), outranks nothing but a genuinely judged warn or crit (`unknown` ranks below `warn` on purpose — a known problem elsewhere on the host must not be masked by one stale mount), and is asserted through a real agent falling silent by `internal/e2e/rules_test.go` |
| 3.6g | Custom script checks: Nagios plugin format (exit code + perfdata) and JSON format | planned | reuse existing check_* plugins |
| 3.6h | HTTP check with JSONPath extraction, SNMP OID checks | planned | |
| 3.7 | System checks: CPU, load, memory, swap, disk usage, inodes, network, uptime | in progress | v0.3b-1 ships six of the eight as agent plugins over gopsutil: `cpu.load1/5/15` + `cpu.used_pct`, `mem.used_pct` + `mem.available_bytes`, `swap.used_pct`, and per mount `disk.used_pct` + `disk.free_bytes` + `disk.inodes_used_pct` (an optional mount list, otherwise every real filesystem). Network and uptime have no plugin |
| 3.8 | Service checks: systemd / Windows services / launchd | in progress | v0.3b-1: the `service` plugin reports `service.state` per named unit over systemd's D-Bus API (`active` / `inactive` / `failed` / `activating`, and `unknown` for everything else — including systemd's own `deactivating`, which the plugin does not pass through, so a unit shutting down reads as unknown) — an observation, not a verdict; nothing evaluates it yet. Non-Linux builds answer every unit with "not supported on this platform" rather than guessing, so Windows and launchd remain to be written |

| 3.10 | Database checks: PostgreSQL, MySQL/MariaDB, Redis | planned | |
| 3.11 | Web server checks: nginx, Apache, Caddy status endpoints, PHP-FPM | planned | |
| 3.12 | Container checks: Docker containers and their state | planned | |
| 3.13 | Synthetic checks: ICMP, TCP (with banner match), HTTP(S), TLS, DNS, SNMP | planned | for hosts with or without agent |
| 3.13a | Probes per synthetic check: manager, zone workers, agent groups as vantage points; per-probe results and metrics; fail policy any/majority/all with second-probe confirmation | planned | probe ships in every agent binary, no extra install |
| 3.13b | Web assertions: status, response time with DNS/TCP/TLS/TTFB/transfer breakdown, body contains and not contains, regex, HTML selector, JSON path, header, redirect count, content size, TLS expiry and issuer; severity per assertion; failed assertions shown on the alert | planned | |
| 3.13c | Agent config pull every 60 s in addition to push, config version per agent shown in the GUI | in progress | v0.3b-1 ships the self-healing half in a cheaper shape than the row assumes: instead of re-fetching a whole configuration, the agent re-asserts the version it is running every `PULSIX_CONFIG_INTERVAL` (default 60 s, 30–600 s) and on every heartbeat, and the manager answers only when that version differs from what it resolves. A missed push therefore heals within one interval. Not done: nothing in the GUI shows which agents are on the current version |
| 3.14 | Time series ingest (gRPC stream), TimescaleDB hypertables, downsampling | in progress | v0.3b-1: agents ship readings over the existing mTLS stream (batched, each carrying the agent's own timestamp, replayed from a bounded 15-minute ring after a disconnect, with a plausibility window on arrival), into `check_samples` — a hypertable on one-day chunks (migration 00044) — plus a `check_current` row per host/check/item/metric for the current-value view. Downsampling is `time_bucket` at query time into a fixed bucket count per range. Missing: continuous aggregates, chunk compression, and the worker-side batching with `COPY` that spec §10's ingest target assumes |
| 3.15 | Retention per org (30/90/365 d) | planned | v0.3b-1 set an instance-wide 90-day retention policy on `check_samples` (migration 00044) so the hypertable cannot grow without bound; per-organization retention and the 5 m / 1 h aggregate tiers are still this row |
| 3.16 | Graphs per host and per check (uPlot), fleet overview | in progress | v0.3b-1: the host page's monitoring card lists every check that applies to the host with its current values (and the scope it came from), and opens a uPlot graph per numeric metric over 1 h / 24 h / 7 d, loaded on demand as its own chunk. v0.3b-2 closes the roll-up half: `GET /orgs/{org}/fleet` and `web/src/features/fleet/FleetPage.tsx` list every host worst-state-first with its per-severity tuple counts, filterable by zone, monitoring group or a single tag, with totals scoped to the filtered set rather than the whole organization. What remains is the other half of the row's own title — no graph anywhere outside a single host, e.g. an aggregate or multi-host chart on the fleet page itself |
| 3.17 | Facts collection (OS, kernel, CPU, memory, IPs), auto tags | done | v0.3a: gopsutil v4 behind narrow provider interfaces, failure-tolerant (a probe that fails does not blind the rest); `os`/`arch`/`virt` upsert as `source='fact'` tags beneath manual ones — a manual tag of the same key always wins, and removing it lets the next push re-apply the fact |
| 3.18 | Agent auto-update from manager | idea | |

**v0.3a shipped (2026-09-24)** — the agent exists and reports: enrollment, identity,
heartbeat, facts and their automatic tags, the Agents page and the host Agent card,
with the three v0.1 release blockers (1.20–1.22) cleared on the way. What it
deliberately does not do yet: **v0.3b** is the check engine and everything that needs
it (3.5–3.13c, 3.14–3.16 — local config, pushed check definitions, discovery, the rule
engine, time series and graphs), **v0.3c** the remaining platforms and the polish
around them (3.2, 3.3, 3.9–3.12, 3.18). The agent binary parses no check today; the
monitoring half of gate 3.4a activates with v0.3b.

**v0.3b-1 shipped (2026-09-28)** — the *check pipeline*, the first of three cuts of
v0.3b (spec `docs/superpowers/specs/2026-09-27-v0.3b-checks-design.md`, plan
`docs/superpowers/plans/2026-09-27-v0.3b-checks.md`). An operator now sees, per
monitored host with an agent, its processor, memory and disk usage and the state of
the units that matter — as a current value and as a graph over 1 h / 24 h / 7 d — and
configures those checks: interval, parameters, enable/disable, and which hosts they
apply to, bound to the organization, a monitoring group or one host. What it
deliberately does **not** do: **nothing evaluates a reading.** There are no rules, no
thresholds, no states, no severities, no hysteresis and no alerts — a `failed` unit is
a value on a page, not an incident, and nothing notifies anyone. That is cut 2 (rules
and states, 3.6d–3.6f); cut 3 is discovery and per-item overrides (3.6c). Rows
partially closed here say which part in their own notes: 3.6, 3.6b, 3.7, 3.8, 3.13c,
3.14, 3.16, and the measurement half of gate 3.4a.

## v0.3b-1 follow-ups

Deferred decisions and limits recorded while building the check pipeline (2026-09-28).
Each was a conscious "not in this milestone", not an oversight.

| # | Item | Status | Notes |
|---|------|--------|-------|
| 3.19 | The manager resolves the whole organization on **every** heartbeat, not only on a stale one | idea | v0.3b-1 Task 6 ruling: `processHeartbeat` (`internal/agentapi/stream.go`) calls `ResolveHost` — which loads the organization's monitored hosts, its definitions, its monitoring groups and every group's selector expansion — for one host, on every heartbeat, **before** it compares versions. Reporting the version saves the push (the encode and the send), never the resolve; the branch's own `internal/agentapi/checks_test.go` asserts three resolves for three heartbeats, two of which reported the converged version. So there is no cheap path for a converged fleet, and the earlier wording here claiming one was wrong. The numbers at the defaults: the version rides every heartbeat and the configuration re-assert ticker does not run when the heartbeat is at least as frequent (30 s heartbeat, 60 s configuration), so **two resolves per agent per minute — 33 a second for a 1 000-host organization, measured in the final branch review at 28.3 ms per resolve with five monitoring groups, i.e. about one core saturated by a fleet that is not changing at all**. It was kept because per-heartbeat comparison is what the design asks for and the statelessness is exactly what makes convergence self-healing: nothing records what was pushed, so an unrecognised or stale version corrects itself in one round trip. Fleet scale is v0.6's subject. The named mitigation stands — a **short-TTL per-organization bundle cache** in front of the resolve, which caches the inputs and not the push history, so the property convergence needs survives it |
| 3.20 | `internal/agentapi`'s stream tests wait on a bare `Recv()` with no deadline | done | v0.3b-1 follow-ups: all 41 call sites in `checks_test.go` and `stream_test.go` read through `recvWithin(t, stream, recvTimeout)` (`recv_test.go`, 5 s). It takes a one-method `receiver` rather than the generated `grpc.BidiStreamingClient`, so its own two tests can stub a stream that never answers without implementing the whole `grpc.ClientStream` surface; a receiver's own error is returned rather than reported, because refusing a stream is what several of these tests pin. Verified against an injected regression — `pushResolved` with its `PushToHost` removed — which now fails `TestPushHostDeliversToConnectedAgent` and `TestPushHostsResolvesOnceForTheBatch` by name in ten seconds instead of hanging to the package timeout with a goroutine dump
| 3.21 | The `-race` gate's margin on `internal/api` is thinner than it looks | done | v0.3b-1 follow-ups, taking the row's second option: a per-package timeout instead of one number for the module. `scripts/test-groups.sh` is now the single definition of the race suite's groups and their timeouts — `internal/api` alone at 3600 s (about 3x its worst measurement of 1 174.5 s, against the 1.5x the old 1 800 s gave it), `internal/e2e` alone at 1 800 s for the reason 2.83 gives, everything else at 900 s, six times the slowest of them measured at the time (147 s) — **stale as of 3.46**: `internal/jobs` had grown to 454-595 s before that row's fix, a ~3-4x margin rather than 6x, which is what let five packages (`auth`, `checks`, `db`, `hosts`, `jobs`) time out together on one gate run from contention alone; 3.46's fix to `internal/db/testdb` brought the known worst case back down to ~20 s, restoring the margin for that one package, though nobody has re-surveyed the rest of the `rest` group for today's actual slowest (see 3.46a). `go test`'s `-timeout` applies to each package's own binary, so the old single number was sized for the slowest package and was then also what a hung five-second package had to wait out; splitting it widens the margin where it is thin and tightens it everywhere else. **Both numbers re-measured 2026-10-26 (3.46a), and the api one was badly stale:** `internal/api` runs **153.6 s** standalone and **162.2 s** inside a `make test` run, against the 1005-1175 s this row sized 3600 s off — backlog 3.46's cut to `internal/db/testdb`'s per-test cost reached the package this whole grouping exists for, roughly 7x. The timeout came down with it, **3600 s to 1800 s**: about 11x today's measurement, and deliberately still ABOVE the old worst case, so a regression all the way back to the pre-3.46 runtime surfaces as a slow suite somebody looks at rather than as a timeout, while a hung test names itself in half an hour instead of an hour. The GROUP stays — what justifies running this package on its own invocation is contention (2.83), not its runtime — and `rest` stays at 900 s, now ~25x its slowest (`internal/jobs`, 36.3 s). Every number here is from a developer machine; CI's own timings have never been measured, which is the one thing that would justify tightening either further. The Makefile's `test` target, `scripts/coverage-gate.sh` and CI all read the groups from that one file, and it refuses to print a grouping that does not partition `go list ./...` — a hand-written path that stops existing would otherwise demote the slow package back into the 900 s group with nothing said. Not done, and not needed for this: splitting `internal/api`'s test files into two packages
| 3.22 | The SPA has no i18n infrastructure | superseded | Superseded by 1.41, which shipped the i18n: English and German through a typed catalogue, an instance default chosen at install and a per-user choice. Closed here rather than tracked in two rows. The original finding: Repo-wide and older than this milestone: `web/package.json` carries no i18n library, there are no locale files and no call sites — every page, label, empty state and error message is hard-coded English, and dates are formatted in the browser's locale by accident rather than by choice (see 2.88). Nothing to fix in one feature; the decision to make is whether Pulsix ships translated at all before v1.0, and if so, picking the library before the string count grows again |
| 3.23 | Data fetching inside the presentational layer | done | v0.3b-1 follow-ups. `SettingsTabs` moved to `web/src/features/settings/` — deciding which tabs a role may see means asking who is signed in, which is a feature component's job, and `features/checks/ChecksTabs.tsx` was already the same strip one section over. `useResetSession` was not lifted into a prop as this row proposed: `useQueryGuard` sits between `QueryError` and its 42 callers, so a threaded callback would have reached all of them. `session.ts` moved from `src/api` to `src/lib` instead, beside `sessionState.ts` — it fetches nothing, declares no query key and calls no endpoint, it clears a cache, so `src/api` was a misfiling and the layer edge it forced on `ui/QueryState` was never data fetching. The rule is now enforced rather than documented: `@typescript-eslint/no-restricted-imports` fails a value import from `src/api` anywhere under `src/ui`, with type-only imports of the generated schema types allowed (a component taking `MetricPoint[]` as a prop is presentational precisely because it names the type and fetches nothing)
| 3.24 | Nothing stops the metric chart from re-entering the entry bundle | done | v0.3b-1 follow-ups: `web/eslint.config.js` carries a `no-restricted-syntax` entry matching an `ImportDeclaration` whose source names `ui/MetricChart`, with a message saying why. It is deliberately a declaration selector and not `no-restricted-imports`, so the `import()` expression `React.lazy` needs stays legal while a static import anywhere in `src/` fails lint; test files are exempt because they are not bundled. The alternative guard — asserting the entry bundle's gzip size in CI — was not added: one guard is enough and this one names the cause at the call site
| 3.25 | `.golangci.yml` enables no formatting linter | done | v0.3b-1 follow-ups: `.golangci.yml` gained a `formatters: enable: [gofmt]` block — golangci-lint v2 moved formatters out of `linters`, which is why the previous config had none in either half and a green `golangci-lint run` said nothing about formatting. One local command now tells the whole truth. The linter's gofmt simplifies, which is stricter than the plain `gofmt -l .` CI ran for 2.59: it immediately found an empty `import ()` in `internal/e2e/templates_steps_test.go` that the CI gate had waved through for the whole milestone. That file is fixed and the CI step now runs `gofmt -s -l .`, so the two agree; the step stays because it names the file in seconds, ahead of the ten-second full lint
| 3.26 | The metric chart's chunk waits for the data, and never re-measures without a window resize | done | v0.3b-1 follow-ups, both halves. (a) The Graph button's `onClick` primes the same `import('../../ui/MetricChart.tsx')` React.lazy awaits, so the chunk and the series request overlap instead of running one after the other; pinned by `HostChecksCard.prefetch.test.tsx`, where the series 500s and the chart therefore never renders — an import on that path can only have come from the click. (b) `MetricChart` measures its container through a `ResizeObserver` instead of a `window.resize` listener, so a sidebar collapsing or a panel opening beside it re-sizes the chart; re-measuring to the width it already has sets the same state and React drops the render, so the observer's extra notifications cannot tear the plot down under a hovering cursor. jsdom ships no ResizeObserver, so `src/test/resizeObserver.ts` stubs it for the whole suite (the fourth such stub in `src/test/setup.ts`) and gives tests a `resizeTo` that throws when nothing observes the element
| 3.27 | "Counted" is a log line nobody aggregates, and half of it is on the wrong machine | idea | The v0.3b-checks spec promises counting twice ("rejected and counted"; "That, too, is counted: a gap in a graph should have a cause somebody can read"), and there is no metrics infrastructure in this repository at all — no `prometheus`, `promhttp`, `expvar` and no `/metrics` route anywhere (the only `metrics` in the API is `GET /orgs/{org}/hosts/{id}/checks`'s sibling graph route). What exists after this milestone's fix wave: the manager's ingest rejections are one `slog.Warn` per batch in `internal/agentapi/stream.go`'s `recordCheckResults`, split by cause (`rejected_implausible_at` = the host's clock, `rejected_malformed` = a broken agent build, `rejected_unknown_host` = a stale enrolment) — readable, but only by whoever tails the manager. The agent's own two drop paths (`internal/agent/session.go`: the ring overflowing in `handleResults`, and the put-back after a failed reconnect replay in `activateSender`) log through the agent's `slog`, which under `deploy/agent/systemd/pulsix-agent.service` goes to **the monitored host's journal**. So the gap in the graph has a cause that is written down on the machine the operator is looking at the graph *about*, and nowhere in Pulsix. Closing it properly is a milestone of its own (an instance-wide metrics endpoint, v0.6's subject); the cheap half is an agent-side drop counter carried on the heartbeat it already sends every 30 s, which would make the ring's losses visible on the host page beside the samples they cost |
| 3.28 | A `check_current` row outlives the thing it measured, and the host card cannot tell | done | v0.3b-1 follow-ups, as a read-side filter and not a delete: `checks.Definition.AsksForItem` says whether a definition still asks the host for an item, and `stillAsked` in `internal/api/host_check_handlers.go` drops the current values it does not. Both details the row named are pinned by tests — a disk check with no `mounts` list means every filesystem the host finds, so it bounds nothing (as do cpu and memory, which have no item at all), and `GET /hosts/{id}/metrics` still serves a dropped item's samples, because a graph of a mount dropped last week is a real graph. Nothing deletes a `check_current` row, unchanged: no event says a mount is gone, and `Current`'s doc comment and migration 00044 both still forbid inventing one
| 3.29 | `internal/agent`'s coverage floats across its own 90 % floor, so the gate is a coin toss | done | Recorded and fixed on 2026-09-28. The measurement stands: six runs gave 90.0, 89.3, 89.3, 89.5, 89.5 and 89.5 %, so the package failed its floor more often than it met it and one full `scripts/coverage-gate.sh` run duly reported `FAIL … 89.5 % (floor 90 %)` with no test failing. The two statements that flip are `loop.go:261` and `session.go:266-268`, opposite sides of one select race the code's own comment already describes: `streamCtx` derives from `ctx`, so on shutdown both `<-ctx.Done()` and `<-recvErr` are ready and Go picks pseudo-randomly. **That race is not fixed and does not need to be** — the code is right, both paths reach the same outcome, and forcing the loser would need a context that lies about its own `Err()`. What was wrong was the margin: the floor was being met by 0.0 to 0.5 points, so a ±0.35 flutter decided it. Eight new tests over paths that had handling but no test — an unreadable key file (`LoadIdentity` must not read an I/O error as "not enrolled" and re-enrol over a live identity), a data dir that is a file, which file `SaveEnrollment` and `Identity.Replace` stopped at and that a failed `Replace` leaves the in-memory certificate on the OLD one, `Valid` without a leaf, a PEM block holding something that is not an EC key, and the two opening sends of a session (facts, first heartbeat) meeting a transport that died before the first byte. Each was mutation-verified against a deliberate break of the behaviour it pins. Coverage is now 92.0-92.5 % over eight consecutive runs: a two-point margin the race's half-point cannot cross. **A second, unrelated flake in the same package surfaced in the verifying gate run and is fixed too:** five tests (`TestLoopEnrollsAndStreams`, `TestStreamSendFailureReconnects`, `TestFactsTickSendFailureReconnects`, `TestLoopRenewalDueDuringStreamEndsSession`, `TestLoopFactsPushErrorIsAdvisory`) waited on a sent message and then read the session's tickers, but a session pushes facts and its first heartbeat before it builds any ticker — so the first send says nothing about whether the tickers exist yet. On an idle machine the gap is microseconds and the assertion won; under the gate's load it did not, and the run failed with `ticker intervals = []`. Reproduced deterministically by delaying the session at exactly that point (all five red), fixed with a `waitForTickers` helper that blocks on what is actually being asserted (all five green under the same delay), which three tests in `checks_session_test.go` were already doing by hand |

**v0.3b-2 shipped (2026-09-30)** — *rules and states*, the second of three cuts of
v0.3b (spec `docs/superpowers/specs/2026-09-29-v0.3b-2-rules-design.md`, plan
`docs/superpowers/plans/2026-09-29-v0.3b-2-rules.md`). A measured reading now means
something: a rule (`internal/rules`) turns a metric, an operator, a value and an
optional duration into a state per tuple — `ok`, `warn`, `crit` or `unknown` — with
precedence over org/group/host/item scopes, hysteresis via a clear condition, flap
detection, and staleness reached three of a check's own intervals after its last
reading. The Rules tab (`web/src/features/checks/RulesPage.tsx`) edits both the form
and the expression form of the same rule model; the host page's monitoring card shows
each tuple's own verdict beside its value, and a new fleet overview
(`web/src/features/fleet/FleetPage.tsx`) lists every host worst-state-first, filterable
by zone, monitoring group or tag. `internal/e2e/rules_test.go` and
`web/e2e/fleet.spec.ts` prove the whole pipeline end to end: a real agent's real,
unfaked reading crossing a real threshold, warn, crit once `for_duration` ripens, ok
the instant a recovering value applies, and unknown once the agent goes silent for
three intervals — closing 3.6d, 3.6e and 3.6f, the roll-up half of 3.16, and the
threshold-to-resolve half of gate 3.4a. What it deliberately does **not** do:
**nothing notifies anyone.** A `warn` or `crit` state is still only a colour on a
page; routes, escalation, acknowledge, snooze, silence and every channel are v0.4.
Discovery of items and per-item overrides (3.6c) and synthetic/remote checks are
likewise untouched.

## v0.3b-2 follow-ups

Deferred decisions and limits recorded while building rules and states (2026-09-30).
Each was found during implementation and is a conscious "not in this milestone", not
an oversight left in a commit message.

| # | Item | Status | Notes |
|---|------|--------|-------|
| 3.30 | `RuleDTO` names no host for a host- or item-scoped rule | done | Added `host_name` and `group_name` to `ruleDTO` (`internal/api/rule_handlers.go`), resolved exactly the way `checkDefinitionDTO` already does: `ruleHostNames`/`ruleGroupNames` batch-resolve the whole page for `GET /rules` (one `ListHostsByIDs` call scoped to the ids on the page, one `ListGroups` call for the organization's own bounded group list — never one lookup per rule), and `ruleHostName`/`ruleGroupName` do the single-rule equivalent for Get/Create/Update. `RulesPage.tsx`'s `RuleTarget` now reads `rule.host_name`/`rule.group_name` directly, falling back to the shortened id exactly as before when a name cannot be resolved. The "degrades, does not error" half of the brief has no reachable fixture: both FKs on `check_rules` are `ON DELETE CASCADE`, so deleting a group or host takes every rule bound to it down with it — there is no surviving rule left to observe a blank name on, the same shape `checkDefinitionDTO`'s own `HostName` is in (no test exists for that path either). Pinned by a new subtest in `TestRuleRoutes` (`internal/api/rule_handlers_test.go`) and a new RulesPage test (`web/src/features/checks/RulesPage.test.tsx`); both verified to fail without their half of the change |
| 3.31 | `fleetStateRank` duplicates `rules.stateRank`'s own order with no compiler link | done | Exported `rules.stateRank` as `rules.StateRank` (`internal/rules/states.go`); `Worse` now calls the exported name and is otherwise untouched. `internal/api/fleet_handlers.go`'s `fleetStateRank` no longer restates the order — it is `3 - rules.StateRank(...)` (its own worst-first sort runs the opposite direction from `StateRank`'s ascending-severity one), with nil ("no data") still trailing every judged state. Pinned by `TestStateRank` (`internal/rules/states_store_test.go`), the same full 4×4 pairwise table `TestWorse` already carries, so the exported surface is pinned at least as well as the function it replaces a duplicate of |
| 3.32 | The evaluation ticker's `Load` is per host; the resolve bundle and rule list are per organization | idea | Carried from Task 9's own review (plan `progress.md`): each host's evaluation calls into the check pipeline for its own monitored-host list, resolve bundle and group bundle, plus a full `ListRules`, so a 15 s tick over the default 200-host batch costs roughly 200x (monitored-host list + resolve bundle + group bundle + full `ListRules`) rather than the shape a per-organization batch would cost. `checks.Service.ResolveHosts` already exists for that batch shape; not implemented because no benchmark in this milestone's own test load showed it was needed yet |
| 3.33 | `MembersPage.test.tsx` selects a row by a CSS-class substring | done | Replaced the `.closest('div[class*="grid items-center"]')` lookup with a structural one, the same treatment `FleetPage.test.tsx` already carries: `MembersPage` renders the name/e-mail pair as `GridRow`'s direct child, with the name itself one span below that, so `saraName.parentElement?.parentElement` reaches the row in two hops with no class name involved. No production code changed; this is the test fix the brief describes |
| 3.34 | The fleet roster's "then by name" tie-break has no test that can actually exercise it | done | Extracted the inline `sort.SliceStable` call into `sortFleetHosts` (`internal/api/fleet_handlers.go`) and gave it an explicit secondary key — alphabetical by name within a tied `fleetStateRank` — rather than relying on `sort.SliceStable` merely preserving whatever order `QueryHosts` happened to hand it; host names are unique per organization (00010_hosts.sql), so `(rank, name)` is a strict total order and `sort.Slice` needs no stability to get it right. Pinned by `TestSortFleetHosts` and `TestSortFleetHostsIsStableUnderRepetition` (new file `internal/api/fleet_sort_internal_test.go`, `package api`, the shape `check_push_internal_test.go` already uses), both built on deliberately unsorted input. Verified by actually mutating the code twice and watching both tests fail: (1) reverting to a rank-only comparator (the pre-fix shape) — both tests failed, ties came out in input order rather than alphabetical; (2) inverting the name comparison to descending — both tests failed on the expected ascending order. Restored the fix after each; `go build` and the two tests pass clean afterwards |
| 3.35 | The chart's hover-prefetch could leak an unhandled promise rejection | done | Found chasing the SPA coverage gate (Task 14): `HostChecksCard.tsx`'s Graph button primes the chart chunk's import with a bare `void importMetricChart()` (3.26's own prefetch) — fire-and-forget, no `.catch`. Vitest's own module mocker does not memoize an in-flight promise, only an eventually-resolved one, so `HostChecksCard.chunk.test.tsx`'s permanently-failing mocked import produced two independent rejections: React.lazy's own, properly awaited and caught by the error boundary (every one of the suite's 614 tests passed), and this prefetch's, which nothing was ever going to await — an "Unhandled Rejection" `@vitest/mocker` raises, invisible in the test output and visible only in the process's own red exit code. The same shape a real visitor hits if their network drops the chunk between a hover and the click that opens it, not only a test artefact. Fixed the same way 3.26 shaped the prefetch in the first place: `importMetricChart().catch(() => {})`, so a failed prefetch is silently swallowed and the real handling stays exactly where it already was, in React.lazy's own await two lines below. `npm run test` now exits 0 |
| 3.36 | A tuple carries no `evaluated_at`, so a stopped evaluator is indistinguishable from a healthy host | done | Added `evaluated_at` and `state_stale` to `hostCheckValueDTO` (`internal/api/host_check_handlers.go`), both absent exactly when `state` is null — the same shape `since`/`flapping` already have. `state_stale` mirrors the reading's own `stale` in name and computation: `now.Sub(evaluatedAt) > staleIntervals * d.Interval`, computed server-side because only the server knows the check's resolved interval. `HostChecksCard.tsx` shows `evaluated_at` in the state pill's own tooltip ("judged 4 minutes ago", beside "in this state since …") and renders a `state_stale` tuple with its own Pill ("stale verdict") — text, not colour, the same bar `stale` already clears. Pinned by a new subtest in `TestHostChecksReportsTupleState` (`internal/api/host_check_handlers_test.go`, a tuple with a fresh reading but an evaluation 20 minutes old against a 5-minute check interval) and a new HostChecksCard test (`web/src/features/hosts/HostChecksCard.test.tsx`); both verified to fail without their half of the change |
| 3.37 | A permanently silent host rewrites every state row every 15 s and sorts to the front of the claim | done | 2026-10-26. `nextDue`'s fallback is `silentHeartbeat` (5 min) instead of `heartbeat` (15 s) once a host has arrived where that journey ends: at least one tuple, every one already `unknown`, and nothing changed this pass. The boundary matters and is pinned by the case beside it — a host GOING silent still produced news (ok → unknown) and keeps the short interval; only the pass after that backs off. A host with no states at all is deliberately not silent either: it writes no roll-up row, so there is nothing to back off from, and a host whose checks are being set up must not wait five minutes for its first verdict. **Why it costs nothing:** the heartbeat exists to bring a silent host INTO unknown, and once there each pass re-derives the same answer from the same absence of data. It cannot delay a recovery — a sample marks the host dirty and `ClaimDueEvalCursors` takes `dirty_at > evaluated_at OR next_due_at <= now()`, so the due time is not what brings a returning agent back. The claim's `ORDER BY next_due_at` then puts the silent hosts at the BACK instead of the front, which was the half of the defect that cost live hosts their place in the batch |

**v0.3b-3 shipped (2026-10-01)** — *discovery and item decisions*, the last of
three cuts of v0.3b (spec
`docs/superpowers/specs/2026-10-01-v0.3b-3-discovery-design.md`, plan
`docs/superpowers/plans/2026-10-01-v0.3b-3-discovery.md`). What a check
measures is now a thing the product knows about, not a string an operator
retypes from memory: `internal/items` (migration 00048) records every
(host, check_key, item) the evaluation ticker's own `Load` sees, and an
operator can decide one item — or a `*` pattern of them — should stop being
judged, at the organization or at one host, with a host decision always
outranking an organization one. Ignoring never stops measurement, only the
verdict: `internal/rules`' own `Apply` deletes the stored state of a
newly-ignored tuple so a `crit` does not sit unrefreshed, and a decision's
own write marks the affected host(s) due again so it takes effect on the
next tick rather than at their own staleness deadline. The Items tab
(`web/src/features/checks/ItemsPage.tsx`) lists every decision with a
pattern editor that previews its match count before anything is saved; the
host page's monitoring card offers a one-click ignore/watch per row and
says where an ignore came from. `internal/e2e/items_test.go` and
`web/e2e/items.spec.ts` prove the whole arc on a real agent: a mount
crossing a threshold reaches `crit`, ignoring it returns the host to `ok`
with the tuple itself carrying no state, watching it again returns the host
to `crit`. Closes 3.6c — which, as its own corrected note says, was already
half done before this milestone started. **v0.3b itself is now complete:**
measurement (v0.3b-1), judgement (v0.3b-2) and discovery (v0.3b-3).

## v0.3b-3 follow-ups

Deferred decisions and limits recorded while building discovery and item
decisions (2026-10-01). Each was found during implementation and is a
conscious "not in this milestone", not an oversight left in a commit
message.

| # | Item | Status | Notes |
|---|------|--------|-------|
| 3.38 | The evaluation ticker's discovery and decision reads run on the pool, not inside the per-host claim transaction | idea | `internal/rules/load_sql.go`'s `Load` calls `items.SQLStore`'s `Discover`/`Decisions` straight through the `items.Store` interface; that store's statements run on `gen.New(d)` against `internal/db`'s own `*db.DB` wrapper, which looks for a transaction bound under `db.WithOrgScope`'s context key (`boundTxKey`, `internal/db/scope.go`), while the claim binds the host's transaction under a different, package-private key (`evalTxKey`, `internal/rules/due_sql.go`) — the two context-binding schemes were never bridged. Judged harmless and accepted as is: `Discover` is `ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING` (a crash before the claim's own commit just re-does a no-op insert next tick), the design already decouples items and decisions from the evaluation outcome they only feed into (`Load`'s read, not `Apply`'s write), and under Postgres' default READ COMMITTED a statement inside the transaction would take its own snapshot at that statement's own start regardless — the transaction boundary buys atomicity with the write-back, which these two reads do not need. Bridging the schemes means teaching `internal/db` or `internal/items` about the other's transaction key, a larger change than this milestone's own scope |
| 3.39 | `WindowRequests` is not ignore-aware | done | `internal/rules/evaluate.go`'s `WindowRequests` now skips a tuple whose item is in `s.Ignored`, the same check `Evaluate` itself applies, so an ignored item's window is never fetched from the store at all instead of being fetched and discarded every tick. `TestWindowRequestsIgnored` pins it. Original note: a rule with a windowed condition (`avg`, `max`, …) on an item an operator has ignored still had its aggregate fetched every tick and discarded, one more round-trip cost per tick for a verdict nobody reads |
| 3.40 | Org-scope `Preview` fetches the organization's every item and filters in Go | done | `items.Service.Preview`'s org-scope branch now calls `ItemsForOrg` with `Filter{CheckKey: d.CheckKey, ItemGlob: d.Pattern}` instead of the zero `Filter`, so the store's own `LIKE` translation does the filtering and `Total` is `len(views)` directly. `TestPreviewOrgScopePushesFilterIntoQuery` records the `Filter` argument to prove the push-down actually happened (the existing `Total`/`Capped`/`len(Matches)` tests pass either way, since the fake's own `ItemsForOrg` already honoured `Filter` before this change); `TestPreviewHostScopeDoesNotCallItemsForOrg` pins that the host-scope branch, left filtering in Go over `ItemsForHost`'s already-bounded fetch, never reaches the org-wide listing. Original note: the pattern that most needs a fast, cheap count — `*`, matching everything — was the one that meant materialising the organization's entire item table into Go to count it |
| 3.41 | Nothing pins "`Decisions` is read once per host" | done | `fakeItemStore` (`internal/rules/due_sql_test.go`) now has a `decisionsCalls` counter, incremented in `Decisions`; `TestLoadSeenItemsDropsEmptyAndDedupesDuplicates` was extended with a second item (`/opt` alongside `/var`) and asserts the count is exactly 1, so "once per host" and "once per item" are no longer the same number for this fixture to tell apart. Original note: a regression that moved the call into a per-item loop would have passed every existing test green |
| 3.42 | `HostChecksCard.tsx` and `RuleDialog.tsx` are past the file-size split signal, and `RuleItemPicker`'s `<datalist>` id is a singleton assumption | done | The `<datalist>` half shipped earlier: `RuleItemPicker` generates its id with `useId` and shares it between the `Input`'s `list` attribute and the `datalist`, so two mounted pickers can no longer collide. **File sizes, 2026-10-26.** `HostChecksCard.tsx` 522 → 158, splitting out `HostCheckValueRow.tsx` (276 — the part that actually grew; the card around it is a header and a list), `MetricGraphPanel.tsx` (92) and `metricChartImport.ts` (15). The last is a file of its own for a lint reason worth knowing: two files need the lazy chart thunk — the panel renders it, the row warms the chunk on hover — and a component file that also exports a plain function trips `react-refresh/only-export-components`. `TupleKey` moved to `hostChecks.ts`, which both halves already import. `RuleDialog.tsx` 478 → 319, splitting out `RulePickers.tsx` (129) and `ruleDialogText.ts` (50): the two confirmation strings are long on purpose — they explain a one-way door and a deletion — and nothing in that file renders. **A trap for whoever does the next one: this repo has NO prettier config.** `npx prettier --write` therefore applies its own defaults (double quotes, a different print width) and silently reformats whatever it is pointed at — 53 files, in one run here, before it was noticed and reverted. ESLint plus `tsc -b` are the formatting gate; there is nothing else to run |
| 3.43 | A host-scoped `watch` overruling an organization `ignore` renders no disclosure until item discovery catches up | done | Closed by the v0.3b-3 review wave: `hostCheckValueDTO` now carries `decided_by` beside `ignored`, both out of one server-side `items.Decide` call on the same value, so `HostChecksCard.tsx`'s `ValueRow` no longer depends on `check_items` having a row for the item. Original note: `ValueRow` only knew *why* a tuple is ignored (or watched over an ignore) from `GET /hosts/{id}/items`' own `decided_by` field — itself dependent on `check_items` already carrying a row for that item, which it does not until the evaluation ticker's own discovery pass has run at least once. Until then the row shows "ignored" (from `GET /hosts/{id}/checks`' own `ignored` flag, resolved independently and immediately) or the bare, un-attributed fallback text ("Origin not yet known") with no way to say the watch is what is currently winning. Not a regression — a plain ignored row with no `decided_by` yet has always rendered the same placeholder — but the asymmetry (review focus 3's own "the host wins, and the view says why") is worth recording: the view says why only once discovery has had one tick to catch up with the decision |
| 3.44 | A host whose every item is ignored renders as "no data" on the fleet page, exactly like a host nothing measures | done | Solved on the read side, exactly as the milestone's own "ignore stays out of the state enum" ruling demands: `rules.State` and `rules.HostState` are both untouched. `fleet_handlers.go`'s `GET /fleet` now reads `deps.Items.ItemsForOrg` and `deps.Items.ListDecisions` once each per request — not once per host — resolves every discovered item through `items.Decide` in memory (`allIgnoredHosts`, the same resolution `internal/rules/load_sql.go`'s `resolveIgnored` runs for evaluation), and adds one field, `fleetHostDTO.AllIgnored` (plus a matching `fleetTotalsDTO.AllIgnored` bucket, carved out of what `NoData` used to count), true only when a host has no roll-up row *and* every item it discovered currently resolves to ignored. A host with even one judged tuple keeps its real state regardless of what else is ignored; a host with nothing discovered at all is still plain "no data" — both pinned in `fleet_handlers_test.go` alongside the core case. `web/src/features/fleet/fleetHost.ts`'s `fleetStateLabel` takes the new flag and renders "all ignored" in the `accent` tone (not `unknown`'s "no data", not a fifth colour), so the distinction is readable without relying on colour alone. The e2e gate (`internal/e2e/items_test.go`) no longer only routes around the zero-judged-tuple case to keep its own "ok" claim clean — it now goes on to ignore every one of a host's three tuples (disk's own mount, plus cpu and memory each through a host-scoped glob naming no item, since neither discovers one to pick) and asserts the fleet overview reads the host as "all ignored", not "no data", while every tuple's own value keeps arriving throughout. Original note: ignoring an item drops its verdict and deletes its `check_states` row (`internal/rules`' own `Apply`), which is the milestone's whole thesis: "ignored ≠ not evaluated", measurement continues, only the judgement goes. One level up that distinction disappeared. A host with no remaining judged tuple contributed no roll-up state, so the fleet page showed it the same way it showed a host nobody has ever measured — and the operator who deliberately silenced the last item could not tell their own decision apart from a broken agent. The e2e gate worked around it explicitly rather than asserting through it, which was itself the evidence the case was real |
| 3.45 | `GET /hosts/{id}/checks`'s `ignored` flag under-reports for a host-scoped glob over an itemless check (cpu, memory), and its own doc comment is wrong | done | 2026-10-26. Two paths answered the same question and disagreed for a metric with no item; the read path skipped those tuples outright on a doc comment asserting they had "nothing to ignore", which the code beside it already contradicted — `items.Match("*", "")` is true, and `resolveIgnored` runs Decide over "the empty-item ones" for exactly that reason. **It was TWO filters, not one:** `hostIgnoredItems` never looked the tuple up, and `toHostCheckDTO` would have dropped the answer anyway, so removing either alone changes nothing — the first fix measured as a no-op and the test caught it. Both gone, and the three doc comments that stated the false rule (the function's, `Ignored`'s and `DecidedBy`'s) corrected rather than left as "description". **The SPA needed the same correction and it is the sharper half:** `HostChecksCard` gated both the origin and the "Why ignored" disclosure on `item !== ''`, so the server's new truth would have produced a bare `ignored` badge with nothing to ask — on the one row whose silencing is hardest to trace, since there is no item under it to look up on the Items tab. The row ACTION stays item-only: a glob is not something that row can flip. `internal/e2e`'s claim 4 no longer routes around the case it was most about — it asserts the flag for all three tuples now, through a real agent, and passes |
| 3.46 | `internal/db/testdb`'s per-test overhead dominated every database-backed package's runtime | done | `internal/jobs` under `-race -count=1 -v` was observed at 453-595s across several runs before this fix — the range itself from machine-load drift between runs, not from any code change — against 76 database-backed tests and 113 pure unit tests that cost effectively nothing; taking the lower end, that is an average of ~6.0s per database-backed test for work that typically inserts a few rows and runs one query, over 99% fixed per-test cost rather than the test's own work. `testdb.Open`, called 41 times from that package alone, paid three costs on every single call: a full goose `Migrate` check even though nothing changes the schema between one test and the next, a fresh bootstrap-superuser `pgxpool` opened and closed purely to run the post-test truncate (`pulsix_app` is deliberately not granted TRUNCATE), and `truncateAll`'s loop issuing one `TRUNCATE ... CASCADE` per table — 57 `CREATE TABLE` statements across the schema's migrations, several of them TimescaleDB hypertables whose truncation reaches every chunk. Three changes, each committed and measured on its own via a microbenchmark (`TestOpenReturnsMigratedDB` repeated `-count=N`, back-to-back via `git stash` so before/after share one machine state): (1) `truncateAll` now builds the table list with `string_agg` inside one `DO` block and issues a single multi-table `TRUNCATE` instead of looping — `-count=25`: 122.7s before (4.9s/call) vs 12.3s after (0.49s/call), roughly 10x and the change that did almost all of the work. The mechanism is not pinned down: both forms run inside one transaction, so taking every lock together rather than one at a time cannot explain a tenfold gap — the likelier causes are `CASCADE` re-walking FK-reachable tables once per statement across the old loop's 57 statements, and per-statement plpgsql `EXECUTE` parse/plan, but this was not isolated and should not be read as confirmed; (2) `Migrate` now runs once per test binary, inside the same `sync.Once` that starts the container, instead of once per `Open`/`URL` call — two `-count=60` pairs ~9-12% faster per call on top of (1); (3) the bootstrap-superuser pool is opened once in that same `sync.Once` and reused by every test's cleanup instead of opened and closed per test — another ~10-12% across two more `-count=60` pairs. The full-package number is a separate, authoritative pair, run back-to-back on one machine state specifically to avoid comparing runs taken under different load (plain `-race -count=1`, no `-v`, unlike the 453-595s range above): `internal/jobs` went from 407.994s to 19.855s, roughly 20x, averaging ~5.4s before and ~0.26s after per database-backed test. The property the whole change rests on is unchanged in kind: every table still empties between tests, with the list still built from `pg_tables` at truncate time rather than a hand-maintained one that would rot the first time a migration adds a table — pinned by `TestTruncateCoversTablesNotInAnyHandWrittenList`, which creates a table named nowhere in `testdb.go` and no migration, and asserts it still comes back empty after the next test's own cleanup. 3.21's own margin claim ("six times the slowest measured, 147s") had gone stale before this fix — `internal/jobs` alone had grown past it to 454-595s, which is why `auth`, `checks`, `db`, `hosts` and `jobs` timed out together on a recent gate run from contention alone, nothing broken in any one of them; the correction is now recorded directly on 3.21. This fix removes `internal/jobs` as the package threatening that margin; whether `rest`'s slowest is now something else again has not been re-surveyed — that is 3.46a |
| 3.46a | Re-survey `rest` for today's slowest package now that 3.46 fixed `internal/jobs` | done | 3.21 sized `scripts/test-groups.sh`'s 900s `rest` timeout off the slowest package it had measured at the time (147s); 3.46 fixed the package that had since grown past that (`internal/jobs`, 454-595s) back down to ~20s, but fixing one package is not the same as re-timing the group — a single gate run finishing inside 900s only proves every package fit, not by how much, and order-dependent cost in a package nobody has looked at recently would not show up as a failure here either. Whoever picks this up should run each `rest` package under `-race` individually, one at a time (not concurrently — see 2.83 and the contention note 3.21 now carries), and update 3.21's own number with whatever is slowest today. **Done 2026-10-26, exactly that way.** Today's slowest is `internal/jobs` at **36.3 s**, then `notify` 32.5 s, `auth` 31.0 s, `app` 23.3 s, `worker/runner` 22.1 s, `alertroutes` 21.5 s; nothing else reaches 21 s and `internal/checks` — the 147 s that sized the number in the first place — is at 12.2 s. So the 900 s is about **25x** the worst case rather than 6x. Left at 900 s anyway and the reasoning written into `scripts/test-groups.sh`: a generous margin is not a wrong one, and the figure the timeout actually has to survive is a cold CI runner, which nobody has ever measured — every number in 3.21 and here is from a developer machine. The numbers include compilation and the test container's own startup, and were taken with `-race` but without `-cover` (the gate uses both), so they run slightly high rather than low. The survey also turned up the bigger finding, which belongs to 3.21: `internal/api` is down to 153.6 s |

## v0.4 · Alerting

| # | Item | Status | Notes |
|---|------|--------|-------|
| 4.1 | Monitoring groups: tag selector (`role=db AND env=prod`) + manual members + exclusions | in progress | The selector half shipped in v0.3b-2 (`MonitoringGroupsPage`, `internal/checks/groups.go`'s `HostMatcher` — "which hosts does this selector match" — and `RuleDialog`'s own group-scoped rules), carried along with the rule engine rather than announced as its own row. Manual members and exclusions are genuinely outstanding: group membership today is a tag selector evaluated per request with no reverse index and no override table, which is also why v0.4a could not resolve a monitoring-group silence (4.13) |
| 4.1a | Nested monitoring groups: optional parent, membership mode within-parent or independent, priority; hosts stay many-to-many | planned | |
| 4.1b | Inheritance semantics: rules override (deepest wins, priority breaks ties, ambiguous ties flagged), check templates / routes / maintenance add up; effective-rules and route-union view per host | planned | This row's own ruling that routes add up, and the route-union view per host it names, shipped in v0.4b — see 4.1c. What is left here is specific to nested monitoring groups (4.1a, still `planned`): rules' own deepest-wins precedence and check templates adding up once groups can nest at all |
| 4.1c | Alert routes: which notification channels hear which alerts — severity and lifecycle-event selections, org / monitoring-group / host scope, routes add up rather than override (4.1b's own ruling) | done | v0.4b. `internal/alertroutes` (`Route`, `Validate`, `Service`, `MatchRoutes`/`Channels`, the SQL store) plus the follower (`Follower.Tick`, one coalesced message per channel per tick — decision 5 — on top of `internal/notify`'s outbox) and the suppression it reuses unchanged from `alerts.Mute` (maintenance, snooze — nothing reimplemented). API: six routes (`alert_route_handlers.go`), CRUD over `/orgs/{org}/alert-routes` plus the per-host union view `GET /hosts/{id}/alert-routes` 4.1b itself named. GUI: Settings → Alert routes (`AlertRoutesPage`/`RouteDialog`) and the host page's own `HostAlertRoutesCard`, naming every route that covers the host, showing what each one fires on, and saying so explicitly when none do. Decision 8's "reported ineffective" is there too: a route whose last channel was deleted survives (the link cascades, not the route) and both views say "Reaches nobody — no channel" rather than leaving an empty cell. That last part did **not** ship with the rest of v0.4b — the whole-branch review found the decision promised twice in the spec and built nowhere, and it landed in the review's own fix wave. `TestAlertRoutesEndToEnd` (`internal/e2e`) drives a host's cpu and memory tuples into crit and asserts one delivery row for the channel naming both alerts |
| 4.2 | Check templates attached to monitoring groups | idea | Probably redundant, not merely unstarted: v0.3b-2's rule engine already binds rules to a monitoring group through its own precedence resolution (org → group → host → item), which is what a "check template attached to a group" would exist to do. A second template mechanism on top would duplicate that rather than add to it. Left as `idea` rather than `planned` until someone identifies a concrete gap the rule engine's own precedence does not already cover; if none turns up, this row should be closed as redundant rather than ever marked `done` |
| 4.3 | Alert rules: thresholds, duration, severity, per host / group | done | Shipped in v0.3b-2, not v0.4 — this row was stale (`planned`) through two milestones after the fact. Evidence: `internal/rules` (`for_duration`, severity, org/group/host/item precedence, the expression and form-mode editors, the fleet/host pages). The seeded `service` check watching `sshd` **must not ship an alerting rule enabled by default**. The seed stands because every host Pulsix manages is reachable over SSH, so the unit exists on the overwhelming majority — and on a host where it does not, v0.3b-1 shows one visibly odd entry and nothing else, because this cut has no alerting at all. The moment a default rule exists, that same harmless entry becomes a page at 3 a.m. for a host that is working perfectly |
| 4.4 | Notification schedules: time windows, weekdays, on-call rotation | done | v0.4c (design doc `docs/superpowers/specs/2026-10-13-v0.4c-notification-schedules-design.md`, including its own "As built"). Two separate halves, both on the route: the **delivery window** — `window_cron`/`window_duration`/`window_timezone` on `alert_routes` plus a breakthrough severity set that crosses a shut one, evaluated per route in `resolution.windowOpen` (`internal/alertroutes/follow_resolve.go`) against `maintenance.ActiveAt`, held as the ledger's new `deferred` state and caught up once the window opens (or never, if another route already delivered the same event — the window gate is per route, the ledger one row per event, see the spec's "As built"); and the **on-call rotation** — the new `internal/oncall` package (`Rotation`, `Exception`, `ChannelAt`, `Shifts`), a cycle of channels plus stand-ins, resolved at delivery time rather than alert-open time (decision 8) and usable as a route's channel source instead of its fixed list (`rotation_id`, `channelsOf`). Migration `00054`. API: nine routes for rotations and their stand-ins/roster (`oncall_handlers.go`), plus the window and rotation fields riding the existing `alert-routes` routes. GUI: `RotationsPage`/`RotationDialog` with a roster strip, and `RouteDialog`'s own delivery-window and channel-source sections. `TestOncallEndToEnd` (`internal/e2e`) drives a real agent's alert through a shut window, advances the follower's own clock past both the window opening and a rotation handover, and asserts delivery to whoever is on call at that instant, not whoever was when the alert opened |
| 4.5 | Channels: email (SMTP) | done | Shipped in v0.2d (`notify.KindEmail`, `internal/mail`), two milestones before this row was corrected — v0.4a's own design doc caught the drift while surveying what "alerting" already contained |
| 4.6 | Channels: generic webhook with template | done | Shipped in v0.2d (`notify.KindWebhook`) — covers n8n, Discord, ...; same stale-row correction as 4.5 |
| 4.7 | Channels: Telegram, Slack, Mattermost native | spec pending | **Design approved 2026-10-19, not yet written as a spec — these decisions live only here.** Finding that shaped it: all three are HTTP POSTs, and the existing `webhook` kind already does method, sealed URL, sealed header values, a body template and HMAC signing — so a user can reach Slack today by hand-writing a template. What "native" buys is the absence of that handwriting, not a transport. **Decision 1: presets on the webhook kind, not three new kinds.** A `preset` column (slack / mattermost / telegram, empty = today's raw mode), chosen over first-class kinds because the latter would touch the CHECK, `View()`, `Send()`, the GUI kind picker and every channel picker in routes, rotations and escalation steps — and again for each of 4.8's four services. Precedent: inbound webhook triggers already carry `preset` (github, gitlab) on one kind. **Decision 2: a preset owns the body**, so the template field disappears when one is set; two sources for one body is the duplication that has bitten this repo twice. **Decision 3: rich per-service rendering**, chosen over reusing `renderEmail`'s plain text — Slack blocks with an attachment colour by severity and a button to `Event.URL`, Mattermost sharing Slack's attachment format but getting its own tests because "compatible" is a claim until proven, Telegram Markdown with `chat_id` as a non-secret config field and the bot token inside the sealed URL. **Decision 4:** where one event batches alerts of mixed severity, the highest decides the colour — the convention the alert list already rolls up by. Unchanged by all of it: delivery, outbox, retries, signing, and every channel picker, because the kind stays `webhook`. The scope is deliberately shaped so each of 4.8's services is one renderer plus one line of validation |
| 4.8 | Channels: ntfy, Pushover, Twilio SMS, PagerDuty | planned | Additive on the existing `notify.Kind` interface; one bounded task each, no design needed |
| 4.9 | Escalation chains (after N min unacknowledged → next channel) | done | Shipped in v0.4d, tasks 1-9. Each route carries an ordered ladder of steps beyond its own immediate delivery (`alert_route_steps`, `alert_route_step_channels`), each naming a delay and either a fixed channel list or an on-call rotation (`alertroutes.Step`, `ValidateSteps`). The follower's `Tick` gains a second, time-driven pass (`alert_escalations`, an anti-join ledger, the same shape v0.4b's delivery ledger uses) that fires the next unfired step once its delay has elapsed since the previous step's own `fired_at` — never from when the alert opened (design decision 3). Acknowledging, closing, the severity leaving the route's set, disabling the route, deleting the route, and every mute reason (snooze, silence, maintenance) each stop or pause the chain per decision 4's table, with resuming giving the next step its full delay again (decision 5, amended by decision 8 so a severity re-entry does not get a fresh delay after a fall that stays within the route's set). A new `escalated` `alert_events` kind makes each rung visible on the alert's own timeline, not only in the channel it reached. API: the route DTO's `steps` field, validated the same way the route's own four selections already are. GUI: the route editor's ladder (add/remove/reorder a rung, pick its channel source) and the host card's escalation display. End to end: `TestEscalationEndToEnd` (`internal/e2e/escalation_test.go`) drives a real agent's crit alert through a base delivery and an escalated rung, proves the rung fires exactly once and the base channel is not re-notified, then proves an acknowledgement stops the chain before its next rung's delay elapses. This sub-project also settled 4.36 — see that row. One known limitation recorded as 4.41: the escalation clock samples a maintenance occurrence at two instants rather than over an interval, which can fire a rung slightly early after a short maintenance window, never late and never skipped entirely |
| 4.10 | Acknowledge, snooze, silence | done | v0.4a. Acknowledge and unacknowledge (`alerts.Service.Acknowledge`/`Unacknowledge`, voided by a worsening severity — decision 2); snooze until an instant or until the state next changes (`Service.Snooze`/`Unsnooze`); silence as a maintenance window with `reason = 'silence'` (migration 00050, `Mute`'s total suppression order over snooze/silence/maintenance). GUI: the Alerts page's row actions and dialogs (`AcknowledgeDialog`, `SnoozeDialog`). Silencing a monitoring group specifically is not included — see 4.13 |
| 4.11 | Maintenance windows on host / group / org + central list, visibility internal or public on selected status pages | in progress | The core (windows on host / inventory group / org, a central list) shipped in v0.2d (`internal/maintenance`); v0.4a added silencing on top (`reason = 'silence'`, 4.10) and the `monitoring_group` target kind at the schema level (not yet reachable — see 4.13).
