Still outstanding: "visibility internal or public on selected status pages" waits on v0.5's status pages existing at all |
| 4.12 | Alert history and timeline per host | done | v0.4a. `GET /orgs/{org}/alerts/{id}` returns one alert's timeline — its own `alert_events` interleaved with the `check_state_changes` transitions in `[opened_at, closed_at]` — rendered by `AlertTimeline`/`HostAlertsCard`'s per-alert disclosure on the host page. Scoped to one alert's own episode, by design decision ("Explicitly not in this sub-project"): a host-level timeline spanning every alert a host has ever had is 4.15, not this row |
| 4.13 | Monitoring-group silence resolution | idea | v0.4a widened `maintenance_windows.target_kind` to accept `monitoring_group` (migration 00050) and `alerts.Mute` accepts the kind in its own Go types, but nothing resolves a monitoring group's *membership* to a set of hosts for suppression purposes — groups are a tag selector evaluated per request (`checks.HostMatcher`, "which hosts match this selector", with no reverse direction), not a materialised membership table. Resolving it per alert means one selector evaluation per candidate group per `Mute` call, with a caching question attached. The API's own huma enum (`maintenance_handlers.go`, `calendar_handlers.go`) deliberately stays `enum:"host,inventory_group,org"` until this lands, so no window of this kind can be created today — an accepted gap, not a silent lie: an operator who wants to silence a monitoring group silences its hosts or its inventory group instead |
| 4.14 | Bulk acknowledge | idea | Deliberately not built in v0.4a (design doc, "GUI": "Deliberately not built"). Decision 4 already took the peak off an alert storm — `unknown` opens one host-scope alert, never one per tuple — so a storm of individually-crit tuples is the remaining case this would help with. A backlog row, not a rebuild, if it turns out to be missing in practice |
| 4.15 | Host-level state history (an aggregate timeline across a host's own alerts) | idea | Deliberately not built in v0.4a (design doc, "Explicitly not in this sub-project": "A history table for host-level state, so a host alert's timeline consists of its own events only"). Today a host's own alert history is reachable (`HostAlertsCard`'s "Full history, including closed alerts" link, seeded via `?host=`), but each alert's timeline still only ever shows that one episode's own events — there is no single view answering "show me everything that happened to this host's monitoring state, across every alert, in order" |
| 4.16 | A lighter, acknowledge-only permission | idea | v0.4a design doc, "API and permissions": acknowledging and snoozing require the existing monitoring write permission (`checks.write`); v0.4a introduces no new one. An ack is arguably a lighter act than rewriting thresholds or editing a check, and a separate, narrower permission (an operator can say "I'm on it" without being trusted to reconfigure monitoring) would be defensible — explicitly left as a backlog question rather than grown into the permission model under this milestone |
| 4.17 | Pulsix section in the end-user documentation (`NoiXdev/noix-docs`) | idea | No Pulsix section exists in the docs repo at all — `find src/content/docs -ipath "*pulsix*"` returns nothing, while every other NoiXdev product has one, across v0.1 through v0.4a. CLAUDE.md's standing instruction is to update the matching docs page in the same work item a user-visible feature ships in, but there has never been an install, getting-started or concepts page for a first feature page (alerting, this milestone) to sit beside — writing one now would be an orphan addressed to a reader with nowhere to have come from. This row is the section itself: whoever picks it up should plan install/getting-started/concepts first, with alerting (what an alert is, when it opens and closes, acknowledging, snoozing, silencing through a maintenance window, reading the timeline) as the first feature page once that foundation exists |

## v0.4a follow-ups

Deferred decisions and limits recorded while building the alert lifecycle
(2026-10-06). Each was found during implementation and is a conscious "not
in this milestone", not an oversight left in a commit message. Source:
`.superpowers/sdd/2026-10-06-v0.4a-alerts/progress.md`.

| # | Item | Status | Notes |
|---|------|--------|-------|
| 4.18 | No test exercises `alert_events.kind`'s own CHECK enum boundary | done | **Already covered when checked, 2026-10-26 — no code written.** `TestEventKindsMatchTheSchema` (`alert_test.go`) does more than the row asks: it READS the migrations and parses their CHECK lists rather than comparing against a hand-written copy, pins `EventKinds()` against `alert_events.kind` exactly and in order, pins `alert_route_events.kind` as EventKinds() minus `escalated` (with its own explanation of why a route must never be able to select that kind), and rejects an invented one through `ParseEventKind`. The row is also stale on the count: there are eight kinds, not seven — migration 00056 widened the CHECK for `escalated`, and that widening is precisely what an earlier version of this test slept through, which is why it now reads 00056 rather than 00049. The only thing left unexercised is an actual INSERT per kind against a live database, which would prove nothing the parsed CHECK list does not already state |
| 4.20 | `Reconcile`'s `closeOrFlap` takes a `found bool` only to return `nil` | done | 2026-10-26. The guard is at the call site now, which also makes the two branches of that switch symmetric: an ok tuple with no open alert is nothing to do, read in the same place as a warn tuple with no open alert being an open. `closeOrFlap`'s doc comment says it is only ever called for a tuple that HAS one |
| 4.21 | `alerts` store: two sources of truth for the default mute reason | done | 2026-10-26. `normalize` (validate.go) reads through `reasonOrDefault` (store_sql.go) now instead of restating `if r == "" `. One rule, one statement: with two, the copy that moved would have decided the default by which path a caller happened to take — the service's validated write, or a `Window` built directly for the store. `reasonOrDefault`'s own doc comment names it as where that decision lives for the package, so the next reader looks in one place |
| 4.22 | `normalize`'s acceptance of `Reason=silence` is never exercised directly | done | 2026-10-26. The two tests around it covered the default (an empty reason becomes maintenance) and the refusal (anything else is `ErrInvalidInput`); nothing drove the arm that matters most. Deleting `ReasonSilence` from the switch would have sent every silence window to the default branch and had it refused as an unknown reason — with the suite still green, because the refusal test passes for the wrong input and the default test names no reason at all. Verified negatively: removing the arm fails the new test |
| 4.23 | `maintenance.Store.Update` with `TargetMonitoringGroup` has no round-trip test | done | 2026-10-26, and the round trip the row asked for is the WEAKER half of what it needs. The three target columns are mutually exclusive, so retargeting has to SET all three — the two being left to NULL and the one being moved to — and a monitoring-group window created and updated in place never exercises that clearing at all. The test changes the KIND on every leg (host → monitoring group → organization) and reads the row's own three columns, because the DTO cannot show a leftover: `Get` resolves one `TargetID` out of whichever column matches the kind, so a row claiming two targets at once would read back correctly. It also pins that the silence reason survives a retarget, and that an unknown monitoring group is refused on Update exactly as on Create |
| 4.24 | `alerts` store: writes history for rows it may not have changed | done | 2026-10-26, as the row prescribed — `RaiseAlertSeverity`, `SetAlertFlapping` and `CloseAlert` are `:execrows` now and a zero-row result is `ErrNotFound`, so the no-write case is unreachable by construction. **The event write was not the guard it looked like:** `alert_events.alert_id` references `alerts(id)` ALONE, not `(org_id, id)`, so an alert belonging to ANOTHER organization left the UPDATE empty while the event insert SUCCEEDED — a history row filed under the caller's organization about an alert it never touched. `ActionFlapped` writes no event at all (there is no "flapped" kind), so its no-op was invisible even in principle. Only the severity action was already covered, by the `GetAlertSeverity` read in front of it — which answered a raw `no rows` rather than `ErrNotFound`, so a caller matching on the sentinel had to know which action it had asked for; it gives the same answer as the other two now. The existing cross-organization RLS test got sharper on the way: it can assert "this matched nothing" outright instead of inferring it from a column that did not move |
| 4.25 | `alerts` store: `GetAlertSeverity` re-reads a value the write could report itself | done | 2026-10-26. `RaiseAlertSeverity` is `:one` now and returns the severity it replaced, so `GetAlertSeverity` is gone — one statement instead of two per `ActionSeverity`. The window was real, not just a round trip: the two statements run inside the evaluator's transaction under READ COMMITTED, so a commit in between left the SELECT's answer stale while the UPDATE saw the newer row, and the `severity_raised` decision hung on the stale one. The row's own suggestion — plain `RETURNING` — could not express it, because `RETURNING` yields the NEW values; a `FROM alerts AS prev` self-join could not either, since its scan keeps the statement's snapshot while the UPDATE's EvalPlanQual re-reads the row, which is the same disagreement moved inside one statement. What ships is a `WITH prev AS (SELECT … FOR UPDATE)` CTE: the lock makes the re-read the row this statement then overwrites. PostgreSQL 18's own `RETURNING OLD.severity` says it directly and should replace the CTE once sqlc can parse it — 1.31.1 answers `column "severity" does not exist`. Zero rows is `pgx.ErrNoRows` now rather than a row count, mapped to the same `ErrNotFound` 4.24 gave the other three writes. `TestRaiseAlertSeverityReportsTheSeverityItReplaced` pins the contract; returning the new value instead was verified to fail it and `TestExecuteRaisesPeakButNeverLowersIt` both |
| 4.26 | `internal/alerts/store_sql.go` is past the file-size split signal | done | 2026-10-26, in THREE cuts rather than the one the row names — it was written at 653 lines and the file had reached 912 (partly from 4.24 and 4.31 landing in it), so `store_sql_read.go` alone would have left 670 behind, still over the signal. The dividing line turned out not to be read-versus-write but **whose transaction it is**: `store_sql.go` 365 (contract, cursor codec, conversions), `store_sql_execute.go` 218 (the evaluator's write path, all of it inside the CALLER's per-host claim), `store_sql_read.go` 240 (what the API and the notification follower read, on the pool), `store_sql_actions.go` 162 (the four operator actions, each opening a transaction of its own — which is precisely why they are not more of the execute file). `OpenForHost` is formally a read and stays with `Execute`: it is the input those actions are computed from, on the same transaction, and splitting the pair would hide that they are one exchange. **The test files are deliberately left:** the row mentions `store_sql_test.go` in passing (653 then, 930 now) and `store_sql_task6_test.go` is 1336, but the package already carries eight test files grouped by milestone, so an ordering principle exists; re-cutting them is churn with no reader it helps, and the row prescribes no seam for them. If that is wanted it deserves its own row and its own argument |
| 4.27 | `alerts.Detail` re-declares `Alert` and `Suppression` instead of embedding `View` | done | 2026-10-26. `Detail` embeds `View`; a Detail IS a View with a timeline. Field promotion means every reader is unchanged — `d.Alert`, `d.HostName` and `d.Suppression` read exactly as before — so the whole change is the struct and the one literal that builds it. What it buys is the failure the row names: a field added to `View` no longer misses `Detail` until someone remembers to add it twice |
| 4.28 | `occurrenceAt` re-implements `maintenance.ActiveAt`'s own interval test | done | 2026-10-26. The one-off branch builds a `maintenance.Window` and calls `ActiveAt`, the same way the recurring branch beside it already did; the hand-written `now.Before(start) \|\| !now.Before(end)` is gone. Whether a stretch is half-open at its end is a convention `internal/maintenance` owns, and stating it twice gave the two somewhere to drift — an operator would have seen a window cover its final instant on the alerts page and not on the calendar. It also inherits 4.29's treatment for free: a one-off row with no start or end is now reported as unevaluable rather than read as "not now", which is what `ActiveAt` already said about it |
| 4.29 | A maintenance or silence window whose cron or timezone stops parsing goes silently inactive | done | 2026-10-26, and **the row's first half was already out of date**: `HostsInMaintenance` does not fail closed silently — it returns `evaluate maintenance window %s: %w` and fails the run loudly. The real remaining case was the quiet one the row names second. `occurrenceAt` (`internal/alerts/store_sql.go`) answered "inactive" for both "this window does not cover now" and "this window can no longer be evaluated at all", and nothing recorded the difference — so a bad cron un-muted every alert the window covered and the only trace was the page itself, during a silence an operator had declared. It returns an error alongside the bool now, and `WindowsForHost` logs it per window and carries on: suppression stays best-effort, because failing the call would take alerting down for the whole host over one bad row, but it is no longer silent. The log names the window, the organization, the host and the parse failure, and says the CONSEQUENCE ("is suppressing nothing") rather than only that something failed — the test asserts that wording, since a line saying only "error" would leave the operator exactly where they started. A missing timezone or duration on a recurring row is reported the same way and names which half is absent. Pinned against a real database with three windows — a bad cron, an unknown zone and a healthy one — because the third is the claim that matters as much as the first two: one broken row must not take the others down |
| 4.30 | The suppression CTE and `maintenance.HostsInMaintenance` disagree on which hosts a group covers | done | Settled with the v0.4a final review's Important #2: the divergence is correct and now documented on both sides. The CTE (`ListMaintenanceWindowsForHost`) covers every member because an unmanaged host still reports checks and still raises alerts; `HostsInMaintenance` covers managed hosts only because a run that cannot touch a host has no reason to be guarded on its account. Each doc comment names the other |
| 4.31 | `alerts` store: a zero-row update means two different things | done | 2026-10-26, and the shape was worse than the row recorded. Only `AcknowledgeAlert` and `SnoozeAlert` carry `closed_at IS NULL`, so only those two are ambiguous — and **each guessed the other's meaning**: Acknowledge always answered `ErrInvalidInput` ("alert is closed"), Snooze always `ErrNotFound`, so each was wrong in exactly the case the other got right. It also reaches an operator, contrary to the row's "handlers map both to the same response": `alert_handlers.go` maps `ErrInvalidInput` → 422 and `ErrNotFound` → 404, so acknowledging an id that never existed answered 422 about an alert being closed, and snoozing one that had just closed answered 404 about an alert plainly on screen. Both now read the row on the zero-row path only — inside the same transaction as the failed update, so it costs nothing normally and cannot disagree with it. `Unacknowledge`/`Unsnooze` are deliberately untouched: no `closed_at` predicate, one meaning, `ErrNotFound` already right — and the test pins that they still SUCCEED on a closed alert, since a guard copied across by symmetry would take away the last chance to undo a mistaken acknowledgement on an episode that has ended |
| 4.32 | `AlertSink` orchestration tests: `stubTx` embeds a nil `pgx.Tx` | done | 2026-10-26. `stubTx` implements the interface — eleven one-line methods — instead of embedding a nil. "Never actually used" was a property of today's tests, not of the type: the first orchestration test to reach for the transaction would have failed as a nil-pointer dereference inside pgx, with a stack saying nothing about why. Each method panics by name now, and the message names the decision the author then faces — a real transaction through `testdb`, or a real behaviour on the stub. A panic rather than `t.Fatalf` on purpose: threading a `*testing.T` through would mean changing `newFakeEvalStore`'s signature and every call site, for no gain over a message that already says everything |
| 4.33 | `was_flapping` is wired but never asserted end to end | done | 2026-10-26, and it was worse than unasserted: `alertOut`, the API tests' own decode struct, **did not carry the field at all**, so no test could have read it even by accident. The column itself is covered by the store (`TestExecuteOpenSeedsWasFlappingFromCheckState`); what nothing covered is the single line that hands it to a client, `WasFlapping: a.WasFlapping` in `toAlertDTO` — flipped, dropped or hard-wired, it would have shipped silently, because `seedOpenAlert` writes no `check_states` row and `InsertAlert` seeds the column FROM that row. The test seeds two tuple states before opening, one flapping and one not, on two hosts: a lone true assertion passes just as well against a hard-wired true, so the pair is what makes the field prove it is reading something. Both the detail and the list path are checked, since a divergence between them would be its own bug. Verified negatively by wiring the assignment to false |
| 4.34 | `AlertsPage`'s own tests were written after the page, not before | won't do | Process note, not a code defect: TDD was not followed at the page level (component-level red was real; the page-level tests came after). Closed 2026-10-26 as a record rather than as work: a lapse in the order two files were written cannot be undone by writing anything now, and the thing the order exists to protect — coverage — is in place (`AlertsPage.test.tsx`, 18 cases, beside `AlertRow` 30, `AlertTimeline` 15, `HostAlertsCard` 9). Re-deleting the page to re-derive it from red would buy the appearance of process and nothing else. The row stays in the backlog as the record it is |
| 4.35 | Task 3's migration commit also carries the sqlc-generated `Alert` and `AlertEvent` structs from migration 00049 (Task 1) | won't do | **Nothing to do, 2026-10-26.** It describes a commit already in history: the generated types landed one commit later than the migration that produced them. Correct code, bundled awkwardly, on a branch that is not rewritten. Recording the observation is the whole value — it is a note about how a past commit was cut, not a defect in the tree |
| 4.36 | A voided acknowledgement reads as acknowledged again once the severity falls back | done | Settled by v0.4d's design decision 2, forced by escalation needing one unambiguous answer to "is this acknowledged" rather than two that disagree: `alerts.acknowledged_severity` is now cleared when the `ack_voided` event is written (migration 00057 relaxed `alerts`' CHECK constraint from a biconditional to a one-directional form to permit it, keeping `acknowledged_at` as the record that an acknowledgement happened at all). So an alert acked at `warn`, risen to `crit`, fallen back to `warn` now reads as unacknowledged everywhere — the list filter, the host card, the timeline — matching what its own timeline already showed, and its escalation chain keeps climbing instead of reading as settled. Accepted cost, named in the decision: an alert flapping across a severity boundary has to be acknowledged more than once |
| 4.37 | `AlertsPage` can show "No alerts match this view." and "Load more" at the same time | done | 2026-10-26, exactly as the row prescribed: the empty-state line is suppressed while `alerts.hasNextPage`, so "nothing matched" is only ever said once the cursor is exhausted and it is actually known. The cause stands and is correct — the `muted` filter is applied in Go AFTER the page is cut (`alerts.Service.List`), so an empty page with `next_cursor` set is a legitimate answer and following the cursor yields the full set — it was only the copy that contradicted itself |
| 4.38 | `writeAlertAction`'s transaction is only "its own" by caller discipline | idea | `db.DB.Begin` returns the SHARED transaction inside `WithReadTx` (`db/scope.go`), so the `Commit` in `writeAlertAction` would end a caller's outer read transaction early for every other store reading through it. Unreachable today — `WithReadTx` has one user, the inventory renderer, which never writes an alert — and the doc comment now says so rather than claiming otherwise; the row exists so the first caller that puts an alert write inside a read transaction is not the one to discover it |
| 4.39 | A reversible operator action splits an alert episode in two | idea | Un-monitoring a host, disabling a check, or editing a monitoring-group selector so the host drops out of it changes the resolved set, so the tuple becomes unmeasured, its alert closes `stale`, and the next tick that judges it `warn`/`crit` again opens a NEW episode — losing the acknowledgement, the snooze and the peak to the closed row. Not a false close (nothing measures the tuple meanwhile) and strictly better than the previous behaviour of staying open for ever, but an operator who toggles a check off and on again has to acknowledge twice. Decide whether a re-open within some window should adopt the previous episode's acknowledgement, or whether splitting is simply the honest answer |
| 4.40 | The alert list's `limit` query parameter advertises no bounds in OpenAPI | done | 2026-10-26, both routes together as the row asked. `minimum:"1" maximum:"100" default:"20"` on `alertListInput.Limit` and `hostListInput.Limit` — the same three tags the notify, schedule and trigger list inputs already carried, so this closes an inconsistency as much as a gap. `default` states `clampLimit`'s own zero case rather than changing it (20 is each package's `DefaultListLimit`), so an absent parameter resolves to exactly what it always did. What DOES change: `?limit=5000` is now a 422 instead of a silently trimmed page of 100 — a client asking for 5000 and receiving 100 with no signal cannot tell a short page from the end of the list. The test asserts the document and the behaviour together, because a tag nobody enforces describes nothing. The generated SPA client moved by one line (the stale "the server clamps it" prose); openapi-typescript does not emit numeric constraints as types, so the document itself is where the bounds live and where the test reads them |

## v0.4d follow-ups

A deferred limitation recorded while building escalation chains
(2026-10-16). Found during implementation and left deliberately, not an
oversight left in a commit message. Source:
`.superpowers/sdd/2026-10-16-v0.4d-escalation-chains/progress.md`.

| # | Item | Status | Notes |
|---|------|--------|-------|
| 4.41 | The escalation clock samples a maintenance occurrence at two instants, not over an interval | idea | `eligible_since`'s maintenance anchor (design decision 5's third bullet) is computed by asking whether an occurrence covers the chain at one sampled instant — the chain's own anchor — rather than across the gap since the last tick. A maintenance window that both opens and closes strictly between two consecutive ticks is therefore never seen as having covered the alert at all, so the next rung's delay is measured from an anchor that is too old and fires early. The failure is one-directional and bounded: a rung can fire sooner than the design intends, never later, and the chain is never silently abandoned — "too early," not "never." Flagged during Task 6's implementation and re-verified at Task 9, where it remains untested by design (the e2e gate does not exercise maintenance at all). Closing it needs an interval-aware occurrence query — did any occurrence cover any instant in `[last tick, this tick]` — in place of the current point sample at `now` |

## v1.0 · Release

| # | Item | Status | Notes |
|---|------|--------|-------|
| 7.1 | Security review, OWASP audit | in progress | Run 1 done 2026-10-30: `docs/security/2026-10-30-audit-run-1/` (9 confirmed, 8 needs-validation, 1 rejected; coverage partial). Follow-ups are 7.7–7.24 below. Run 2 done 2026-11-01: `docs/security/2026-11-01-audit-run-2/` (12 confirmed, 7 needs-validation, 0 rejected; coverage partial); follow-ups 7.25–7.44 |
| 7.2 | Backup and restore | done | **Done 2026-11-03.** Every backup is one encrypted file (key derived from `PULSIX_MASTER_KEY`, which is never inside it) holding the whole database, optionally without the metric history. Instance administrators get a Backups tab under Settings: destination (a folder on the server or S3-compatible storage, with Test connection), a daily schedule in a time zone with grandfather-father-son retention (manual backups are never pruned), the metric-history switch, Back up now, Download, Delete and a Restore… dialog that shows the command to copy. A failed scheduled backup shows on the page and is mailed to instance administrators when SMTP is configured. Restore is a command, not a button: `docker compose run --rm cli backup`, `backup key`, `restore --list`, `restore [--from …] [--replace] [--yes] <name>|-`; it verifies the whole file before it wipes anything (a corrupt file or the wrong key leaves the database untouched), refuses while a manager is connected, and a failure after the wipe is re-runnable. The one-off commands run through a label-free `cli` compose service, because a one-off `manager` container would carry its Traefik labels. `worker add --replace` re-enrols the bundled worker after a restore. Spec `docs/superpowers/specs/2026-11-02-backup-restore-design.md`; end-user page in `noix-docs` (`pulsix/admin/backups`). |
| 7.3 | Upgrade path and migrations policy | done | **Done 2026-11-03.** The manager and `worker add` refuse a database schema newer than the binary (`db.ErrSchemaNewer`); workers and agents carry a `version_status` (`internal/versioncompat`) shown as "Update required" / "Newer than manager" badges (shown, not enforced); `docs/upgrades.md` holds the migration and upgrade rules, the compose README the upgrade and downgrade steps. |
| 7.4 | Documentation site | done | **Closed 2026-11-03:** the end-user documentation is docs.noix.dev (`NoiXdev/noix-docs`, branch `pulsix`). |
| 7.5 | Docker images on Docker Hub (noixdev/pulsix-*) and Harbor, agent binaries on GitHub releases, all from one `vX.Y.Z` tag | planned | single version for manager, worker, agent |
| 7.6 | Make repo public, landing page on noix.dev | planned | repo is private until then; no tech stack in public copy |
| 7.47 | After a restore, agents and workers that renewed their certificate since the backup are locked out | done | **Done 2026-11-03 (option a, owner decision).** A restore writes a marker (`internal/restoremark`); after it the manager adopts a worker or agent certificate the instance issued strictly between the backup and the restore, for an existing non-revoked identity, newer than every registered certificate — for agents only when its key matches the host's newest certificate (`agent_certificates.public_key_sha256`, migration 00077; a backup taken before that update has no fingerprints, so its agents enroll again). Each adoption is audited. From the 7.2 final review (I1). The manager accepts a certificate only when the database knows its serial (`internal/grpcsrv/auth.go`); an agent renewal registers the new serial and revokes the old one, a worker renewal records the new one. Certificates live 90 days and renew at two-thirds, so a backup older than about 60 days predates a renewal of most agents and workers: after the restore they show as offline / not connecting and their logs say to enroll anew. Today the operator enrolls them again (documented in the CLI's restore output, the compose README and the end-user backups page); the bundled compose worker re-enrolls itself (`worker add --replace` and the worker re-reading its bundle). Options: (a) adopt a presented certificate that is unexpired, signed by the instance CA and names a host/worker the database knows, with a NotBefore newer than that host's/worker's registered certificates, registering its serial on first use; (b) keep a grace list of renewed serials outside the database the backup covers. Needs a security decision first: (a) widens what a stolen but superseded certificate can do, (b) adds state a restore must not lose. |
| 7.48 | Backup robustness follow-ups | done | **Done 2026-11-03.** (1) A backup with no progress for 10 minutes is cancelled as stalled (an S3 link below ~28 KiB/s counts as stalled, since one 16 MiB part is read before it uploads). (2) A scheduled slot with only interrupted attempts is mailed once (`backup_runs.notified_at`, migration 00078, backfilled for existing rows). (3) Leftover `*.pxbak.partial` files are removed at the start of each run. From the 7.2 final review. (1) A stall guard: a hung folder destination (a dead network mount) or a hung dump blocks the ticker and holds the backup lock indefinitely; bound the transfer by progress, not by a fixed total time. (2) Mail the instance administrators when a day's scheduled slot ends with only interrupted (abandoned) attempts, which today is not mailed. (3) Clean up stale `*.partial` files the folder destination leaves behind when the manager stops mid-backup. |

## v1.0 · security audit run 1 follow-ups

From `docs/security/2026-10-30-audit-run-1/` (2026-10-30, `main` at b13f3c6). 7.7–7.15 are confirmed (reproduced locally and verified twice independently); 7.16–7.23 are leads that need one deployment or database fact, without severity.

| # | Item | Status | Notes |
|---|------|--------|-------|
| 7.7 | A monitored host can run commands on the worker through its agent facts | done | **Closed 2026-10-30.** `render.hostVars` now passes facts through `inertFacts`: every fact string is wrapped with `ansiblevars.Inert` (`{"__ansible_unsafe": …}`), so Ansible takes it as literal text, and a fact key starting with `__ansible` (`ansiblevars.ReservedKey`) is dropped instead of being read as a marker. The wrapping happens in `render.Render` itself, so the inventory preview shows facts as Ansible receives them, marked. The worker-image reproduction of the audit (a wrapped value stays literal under `ansible-playbook` with the script inventory) is the behaviour this relies on. User docs: noix-docs `pulsix` 3f2eae5. Was: **High.** agent-reported facts reached the script inventory as trusted templates; a fact like `{{ lookup('pipe','id') }}` ran in the controller when a play read `pulsix_facts`. Fingerprint `internal/inventory/render/render.go:hostVars:pulsix_facts-trusted-template` |
| 7.8 | Concurrent requests bypass the auth rate limit | done | **Closed 2026-10-30.** `clientip.Limiter` gains `Reserve` (claims a slot under the same lock as the check, before the handler runs) and `Refund` (hands it back, only within the window it was taken in) and loses `Charge`; `authRateLimitMiddleware` reserves first and refunds 2xx, 5xx and 429. A burst of 20 concurrent wrong logins against a limit of 3 now lets 3 through (was 20). Trade-off: a successful sign-in holds its slot while it runs, so a burst of concurrent sign-ins from one address can briefly see a 429. Not done: a per-account second-factor failure counter independent of the address. Was: **Medium.** the budget was checked before the handler and charged after it (`internal/api/auth_rate_limit.go`) |
| 7.9 | Run-output redaction misses secrets mixing `"`/`\` with `&`/`<`/`>` | done | **Closed 2026-10-30.** `redactValues` now adds every JSON spelling a run can print (`jsonSpellings`): Go's HTML-escaping `json.Marshal` form and, written by hand, Python's `json.dumps(ensure_ascii=False)` form that ansible-core's result display uses (escapes only `"`, `\\` and control characters). A secret mixing a quote or backslash with `&`, `<` or `>` is redacted in both. Test `TestRedactsPythonJSONSpelling`. Not done: `ensure_ascii=True` (`\\uXXXX` for non-ASCII) and YAML spellings. Was: **Medium.** The redactor adds only Go's HTML-escaping JSON spelling (`internal/worker/runner/redact.go:54`); ansible's display uses Python `json.dumps`, which leaves `&<>` literal, so such a secret reaches viewers in `stdout` (reproduced with real ansible-runner output). Fix: also add the Python spelling (escape only `"`, `\`, control characters) |
| 7.10 | Unmailed invite links let one tenant capture another tenant's later invitations | done | **Closed 2026-10-30.** `tenancy.Service.AcceptInvite` takes the acceptor (the caller's MFA-complete browser session, never a bearer token; `api.inviteAcceptor`) and checks the invitation first without using it up (`PeekInvite`): an address whose account has its own password joins only when the acceptor is that account, otherwise `ErrSignInRequired` (409) and the invitation stays open. A new account still needs a name and an 8-character password, now enforced in the service; the request body's `display_name` and `password` are optional so a signed-in account sends the token alone. The SPA's invite page offers "Join organization" when signed in and, on a 409, asks the holder to sign in and open the link again. Tests `TestAcceptInviteNeedsTheExistingAccountsOwnSession`, `TestAcceptInviteCannotCaptureAnotherOrgsInvitation`, `TestAcceptInviteNewAccountNeedsAPassword`, `TestInviteAcceptExistingAccountNeedsItsOwnSession`, InviteAcceptPage tests. User docs: noix-docs `pulsix` 9724c1a. Residual: whoever redeems an unmailed link for an address with no account yet still creates that account (the address's holder can then not sign in to it — a denial, no longer a capture of later invitations). Was: **Medium.** Without SMTP, `Service.Invite` hands the accept link to the inviter, and `AcceptInvite` treats the token as mailbox proof (`internal/tenancy/service.go:363,398-440`). Any MFA-complete user can create an org, invite someone's address, redeem the link and own that account; a later invite from another org then adds the membership (up to admin) to the attacker's account. Fix: an existing account joins only with its holder's own MFA-complete session; never treat an unmailed redemption as a verified address |
| 7.11 | Base64 redaction only matches secrets at a 3-byte-aligned offset | done | **Closed 2026-10-30.** `base64Spellings` also adds, for each of the three byte offsets, the secret's aligned core (from that offset, cut to whole 3-byte groups, at least `minBase64Core` = 6 bytes) unpadded in the standard and URL alphabets; one of them always sits on a group boundary of the surrounding buffer, so a secret inside a slurped file is redacted at any offset, leaving at most a few edge bytes. Test `TestRedactsBase64AtAnyOffset`; `TestBase64SpellingsStayWithinWhatWeCanName` now requires every spelling to be at least 8 characters. Was: **Low.** `base64Spellings` encodes the secret alone (`internal/worker/runner/redact.go:116`), so a `slurp` of `pwd=<secret>` leaks it to viewers in about two of three placements. Fix: also list the alignment-independent cores for offsets 0/1/2 (minimum length) |
| 7.12 | Plain value under a field reclassified as secret is masked on reads but not redacted in runs | done | **Closed 2026-10-30.** `Renderer.secrets` also adds a plain, non-empty string under a field declared secret to the run's redact list, matching what reads mask (`schema.MaskDeclared`). `TestStringFieldTurnedSecretIsMaskedInThePreview` extended. Was: **Low.** `Renderer.secrets` adds only sealed values to the redact list (`internal/inventory/renderer.go:272`), while reads mask every non-empty secret-typed value (backlog 2.21). Until the next save, a printed value reaches viewers in the run log. Fix: also add plain strings under secret fields; optionally reseal on `SetSchema` |
| 7.13 | Agent check-result item names can forge lines in alert notifications | done | **Closed 2026-10-30.** Ingest refuses control characters and U+2028/U+2029 in `check_key`, `item` and `metric` (`checks.breaksLine` in `withinContract`), and `notify.alertItemsText` passes every field through `oneLine` (U+FFFD for the same characters), so rows stored before the fix cannot break the format either. Tests `TestRecordObservationsRejectsLineBreaksAndControlCharacters`, `TestAlertItemsTextKeepsOneLinePerAlert`. Was: **Low.** `checks.withinContract` bounds `item` only by length (`internal/checks/samples.go:174`) and `alertItemsText` concatenates it raw (`internal/notify/render.go:223`), so a host agent can add forged alert rows, phishing text or `@everyone` to e-mail and `{{ alerts.items }}` webhook bodies. Fix: refuse control characters at ingest and neutralise them in `alertItemsText` |
| 7.14 | A pinned jump host's key is not enforced on routes that also contain an unpinned password-only hop | done | **Closed 2026-10-30.** `mergeInventory` fails the run closed when a route with any pinned jump host cannot be aliased (misaligned chain, a hop that cannot be scripted, or a failed rewrite; `pinnedHop`), instead of dropping the whole rewrite and connecting without the pin. A raw chain with no pinned hop is unchanged. Test `TestMergeInventoryFailsClosedWhenAPinnedRouteCannotBeAliased`. Was: **Low.** `mergeInventory` drops the whole alias rewrite when an unpinned hop has no private key, leaving the raw `ProxyJump` chain under `Host *` with `accept-new` (`internal/worker/runner/inventory.go:103-113`); an impostor of the pinned bastion was accepted in the worker image. Applies to runs and bootstrap/rotate/revoke. Fix: fail the run closed when a route containing any pinned bastion cannot be fully aliased |
| 7.15 | `/schemas/*` serves the API's JSON Schemas without authentication | done | **Closed 2026-10-30.** huma's `SchemasPath` moves to `/api/schemas`, and `requireMFAForExplorer` gates the `/api/schemas/` prefix like the spec. Test `TestSchemasRouteIsGatedLikeTheSpec`. The generated SPA types are unchanged. Was: **Low.** huma's default `SchemasPath` stays at `/schemas`, outside the MFA gate on `/api/openapi*` (`internal/api/api.go:433`). Backlog 1.29 assumed the route did not exist. Fix: `cfg.SchemasPath = "/api/schemas"` and gate that prefix too |
| 7.16 | `tag.yml` runs a shared workflow from mutable `@main` with all secrets | done | **Confirmed and closed 2026-11-01 (pin).** `NoiXdev/github-workflows`' `main` has no branch protection, so anyone with write access there controlled code that received every secret of this repository through `secrets: inherit`. `tag.yml` now calls the shared workflow at commit `fb91e97` (its main on 2026-11-01), which uses only `GITHUB_TOKEN` and `DISCORD_WEBHOOK_RELEASE_NOTIFICATION`. Still open, outside this repo: the shared workflow should declare that one secret under `workflow_call.secrets` so this caller can drop `inherit`; its own `actions/announce-release@main` reference should be pinned too (it receives only the webhook); `main` there should be protected; and the registry secrets belong in a protected `release` environment (1.38). `tag.yml` stays disarmed. **Follow-up 2026-11-01:** branch `fix/go-release-declare-secrets` (c27d736) in `NoiXdev/github-workflows` declares `DISCORD_WEBHOOK_RELEASE_NOTIFICATION` under `workflow_call.secrets` (optional; `inherit` callers keep working) and updates the Go blueprint; once it is merged there, pin `tag.yml` to the merge commit and replace `secrets: inherit` with that one secret. The repository's own `actions/…@main` self-references stay floating, as that repository's README decides. Was: Lead, no severity. `tag.yml:48-49` calls `NoiXdev/github-workflows/...@main` with `secrets: inherit` and `contents: write`, so whoever can change that repo's `main` gets the Docker Hub/Harbor credentials. Blocker: who can write that branch (hosted setting). Fix regardless: pin to a SHA, pass only needed secrets, move registry secrets into a protected `release` environment (see 1.38) |
| 7.17 | Release job pulls QEMU/BuildKit images by mutable default tags | done | **Confirmed and closed 2026-11-01.** At the pinned action commits the defaults are `docker.io/tonistiigi/binfmt:latest` (setup-qemu-action v4.4.0's `image` input) and buildx's `moby/buildkit:buildx-stable-1` (setup-buildx-action v4.4.1 without `driver-opts`), both mutable and run privileged next to the registry credentials. `release.yml` now pins both by digest (`binfmt@sha256:400a4873…`, `buildkit@sha256:cec9f139…`, the tags' targets on 2026-11-01). Not verified against a real release run: CI is disabled. Was: Lead, no severity. `release.yml:99,102` pins the actions but not the images they run privileged next to the registry credentials. Blocker: the actions' default image references at the pinned SHAs. Check the last release log; pin `image:`/`driver-opts` by digest |
| 7.18 | Agent certificate renewal racing a revocation can re-register the agent | done | **Confirmed and closed 2026-11-01.** Reproduced on Postgres: `Register` (the renewal's path) after a committed `Revoke` re-inserted the `host_agents` row and left the new serial live and unrevoked. Renewal now goes through `agents.Service.Renew(reg, fromSerial)` / `SQLStore.Renew`, which locks the serial the agent authenticated with (`LockLiveAgentCertificate`, `SELECT … FOR UPDATE` on a live certificate of that host) in the swap's transaction and returns `ErrNotFound` when it is revoked or replaced; `agentapi.Renew` answers PermissionDenied and records nothing. Under the opposite ordering (renewal first) the revoke's later `DELETE` of the `host_agents` row still disables the new serial. Tests `TestAgentRenewAfterRevokeDoesNotReRegister`, `TestRenewRacingARevocationIsRefused`. Was: Lead, no severity. `agentapi.Service.Renew` calls `Register` without re-checking the serial (`internal/agentapi/service.go:250`); `UpsertHostAgent` re-inserts a row `Revoke` just deleted (seen on the fake store). Blocker: the Postgres interleaving. Plan: integration test on `testdb`; fix with a liveness re-check inside `Register`'s transaction, as `AgentStream` does |
| 7.19 | Session id is not rotated when MFA completes | done | **Closed 2026-11-01 (fixed regardless of the deployment fact).** The cookie-injection position the lead needs is a deployment question, but the code half was confirmed: a pending session's cookie stayed valid after the second factor upgraded it. `Sessions.MarkMFAComplete` now replaces the session's secret (`MarkSessionMFAComplete` sets `secret_hash`) and returns the new cookie value; TOTP confirm, TOTP verify and passkey registration finish send it as `Set-Cookie`, so the cookie a session had before its factor stops authenticating. The session id stays the same, so a running job log stream is not cut. Tests `TestSecondFactorReplacesTheSessionCookie`, `TestSessionMarkMFAComplete`. Not done: the `__Host-` cookie prefix — it needs `Secure`, so it would break plain-HTTP development, and renaming the cookie signs every user out once; worth doing together with a deployment-mode switch. Was: Lead, no severity. `MarkMFAComplete` upgrades the session in place (`internal/auth/session.go:115`), and the cookie has no `__Host-` prefix, so a planted pending session (victim's password known) is upgraded by the victim's own TOTP. Blocker: a cookie-injection position in the deployment. Fix regardless: issue a new session on MFA completion; use `__Host-pulsix_session` |
| 7.20 | A duplicate playbook path in one org blocks a shared template for every org | done | **Confirmed and closed 2026-11-01.** Reproduced on Postgres: a second `playbooks` row at the same path in one organization made `CreateTemplateWithProjections` fail for every organization (with a misleading "re-check colliding organizations" error after the aborted transaction). `InsertProjectedTemplates` now resolves the playbook through `LEFT JOIN LATERAL … ORDER BY created_at, id LIMIT 1`, and `InsertProjectedTemplate`/`UpdateProjectedTemplate` order their existing `LIMIT 1` the same way, so the oldest match wins everywhere. Test `TestSQLStoreCreateTemplateWithProjectionsToleratesADuplicatePlaybookPath`. Not done: a unique index on `(org_id, repo_id, path)` — an installation that already holds duplicates would fail the migration; it needs a cleanup step first. Was: Lead, no severity. `InsertProjectedTemplates` LEFT JOINs `playbooks` without `LIMIT`/`DISTINCT` (`internal/db/queries/shared.sql:316-321`) and nothing makes `(org_id, repo_id, path)` unique, so the bulk insert hits a unique violation and rolls back for all orgs with a misleading 409. Blocker: Postgres run. Fix: `DISTINCT ON`/`LATERAL ... LIMIT 1` plus an additive unique index |
| 7.21 | Worker-uid file operations follow paths the play group can change, and the play can kill its supervisor | done | **Confirmed (process half) and closed 2026-11-01.** Reproduced in the worker image: a play that detaches `setsid nohup sleep` and then `kill -9`s its supervisor (same uid) leaves the sleep running after the job is reported. The loop now sweeps the play uid when it starts and after every job, before the work dir is removed (`Loop.sweepPlayUID`): the worker binary's new sweep mode (`supervisor.SweepArg`, `kill(-1, SIGKILL)`, dispatched by `cmd/worker`, `cmd/manager` and the in-image test binary) runs through the setuid helper as the play uid. A worker runs one job at a time, so every play-uid process between jobs is a leftover. File half, fixed structurally rather than reproduced: `newWorkDir` keeps the work dir 0700 and `runner.ShareWorkDir` opens its root last, after every run file is written, so no play-uid process can enter it while the worker writes or chmods (the data dir's 0710 already kept a leftover from finding a new dir's random name, which is why this half was not exploitable on its own). Tests `TestAPlayThatKillsItsSupervisorDoesNotOutliveItsJob` (in-image; fails with the sweep disabled), `TestLoopSweepsThePlayUIDAtStartAndAfterEachJob`, `TestSweepPlayUIDRunsTheSweepAsThePlayUID`, `TestSweepKillsEverythingItMaySignal`, `TestNewWorkDirStaysPrivateWhenPlayUIDIsSet`, `TestShareWorkDirOpensTheRootLast`. Residual: during a job the play can still kill its own supervisor (it shares the uid); what it leaves now dies at the job's end. Moving the supervisor to a uid of its own is the full fix, and needs a second helper uid. Was: Lead, no severity. The work dir is group-writable (02770) before the worker writes into it, and `writeTextFile`/`ShareWorkDir` follow symlinks (`internal/worker/runner/layout.go:67`, `share.go:56`); a play can SIGKILL its same-uid supervisor and leave a setsid'd process running (seen in the image). Blocker: the full cross-uid chain against a live worker. Fix: materialise privately, share with no-follow fd operations, keep the supervisor out of the play's signal reach or sweep play-uid processes between jobs |
| 7.22 | The job log SSE stream keeps running after access is revoked | done | **Confirmed and closed 2026-11-01.** Reproduced: a viewer removed from the organization, and a session signed out, both kept receiving the stream until the job ended. `writeJobStream` now re-checks access every `jobStreamAccessInterval` (2 s) with `streamAccessHeld`: the session still valid and MFA-complete (`auth.Sessions.StillValid`) or the token still usable (`auth.Tokens.StillUsable`, new query `APITokenUsable`), the membership still there, and the role (narrowed by the token's) still allowing jobs.read. When access has ended it sends one `error` frame and ends; a check that cannot be made ends the stream too. Tests `TestJobStreamEndsWhenAccessEnds`, `TestSQLTokenStoreUsableAppliesTheResolveFilter`. Residual: a log line already in flight may still reach the stream within one interval. Was: Lead, no severity. `writeJobStream` authorises once (`internal/api/job_sse.go:40`) and streams until the job ends; removal, token revocation or deactivation do not cut it. Blocker: DB-backed test. Fix: re-check access on the 500 ms tick |
| 7.23 | A worker-chosen event timestamp can pin a shared jump host's observation state for the whole org | done | **Confirmed and closed 2026-11-01.** Reproduced on Postgres: a connection-test result dated a century ahead was recorded with that time, and a hop entry for a bastion that is not on the tested host's route was recorded too. `grpcsrv.eventAt` now clamps a worker's event time to the manager's clock, `recordTest` clamps the observation time again, and `SQLQueue.hopsOnRoute` keeps only hops whose bastion is on the host's rendered jump chain (a route that cannot be rendered drops all hop entries, logged). Tests `TestRecordTestKeepsTheWorkersClaimsToItsOwnRoute`, `TestEventAtNeverLaterThanTheServerClock`. Residual: a worker can still report a false observation for a bastion on its own host's route; that is the trust a zone's worker holds. Was: Lead, no severity. `eventAt` keeps the worker's timestamp unclamped (`internal/grpcsrv/events.go:118`) and `recordTest` stores it as the observation time; the newest-by-`at` queries then let one future-dated hop entry block or mislead every zone using that bastion. Blocker: DB-backed test. Fix: clamp event time to manager time, check hop ids against the rendered route |

| 7.25 | Any enrolled agent can trigger unlimited organization-wide resolves against the shared database by sending heartbeats in a loop | done | **Fixed 2026-11-02.** Resolve work per agent identity is now bounded on the server. `StreamRegistry.allowResolve` lets one serial's heartbeats resolve at most once per `heartbeatResolveInterval` (10 s; agents heartbeat every 30 s, so a well-behaved one never meets it). It is kept per serial, not per stream, so reconnecting does not reset it. Inside the interval a heartbeat is acknowledged and nothing more. A cached answer was rejected on purpose, since it could be older than a push the agent already applied. `track` allows at most `maxStreamsPerSerial` (2) open streams per serial: a third ends the oldest with ResourceExhausted ('superseded'), not the revocation's Unauthenticated. Tests: throttle_test.go (100 heartbeats inside one interval resolve once, three streams resolve once, a third stream ends the oldest); three convergence tests now advance the harness clock between heartbeats. Not done: the per-organization bundle cache in front of `loadResolveBundle` (backlog 3.19's mitigation), which this bound makes a performance question rather than a security one. Finding: **Medium.** processHeartbeat (internal/agentapi/stream.go:166-173) treats every received Heartbeat as a reason to run an uncached, organization-wide ResolveHost, before and regardless of the version comparison. Nothing on the server bounds how often one agent identity can trigger it: there is no rate limit or minimum interval in receiveMessages or processHeartbeat, no cache in checks.Service.loadResolveBundle, no per-serial stream cap in StreamRegistry.track, and no stream or tap limit on the gRPC server. Fix direction: Bound resolve work per agent identity on the server. In processHeartbeat, if this stream resolved less than a minimum interval ago (for example 10 s), skip the resolve and answer from the last ResolvedSet. Put a short-TTL per-organization bundle cache in front of loadResolveBundle (backlog 3.19's … Fingerprint `internal/agentapi/stream.go:processHeartbeat:unthrottled-org-wide-resolve-per-heartbeat` |
| 7.26 | Second-factor guessing on /auth/totp/verify is limited only per source address, so a password holder can brute-force the TOTP code | done | **Fixed 2026-11-02.** Every check of an account's confirmed second factor (a TOTP code on /auth/totp/verify, DELETE /auth/totp or a re-enrolment, and a recovery code) now spends one attempt of the account's own budget before the code is looked at (`auth.TOTP.attempt`). Migration 00073 adds `totp_secrets.failed_factor_attempts` and `factor_locked_until`. `ClaimFactorAttempt` counts and locks in one conditional UPDATE, so parallel guesses from many addresses get exactly the free attempts (tested with 40 at once). Four attempts are free, the fifth and every later one lock the factor for a minute, doubling, at most an hour, and a success resets both columns. A locked account answers 429 without checking the code, and the login page says to wait instead of 'invalid code'. Tests: totp_attempts_test.go (service, fake store), totp_attempts_sql_test.go (backoff, the one-hour cap over 2000 attempts, concurrency), TestTOTPVerifyLocksTheAccountAfterRepeatedFailures (HTTP) and loginMachine.test.ts. Passkeys are unaffected. Finding: **Medium.** authRateLimitMiddleware (internal/api/auth_rate_limit.go:74-91) keys its budget only on the resolved client address via clientip.Limiter.Reserve (internal/clientip/limiter.go:105-127), whose key() counts IPv4 per address and IPv6 per /64 (limiter.go:165-174). The verify handler (internal/api/mfa_handlers.go:164-178), verifyFactorCode (mfa_handlers.go:50-67) and TOTP.Verify/verifyAndConsume (internal/auth/totp.go:229-296) keep no failed-attempt state per user or per session; the only TOTP write on the path is … Fix direction: Add a per-user (or per pending session) failed second-factor counter in the database (additive migration) and check it before verifying. Past a small threshold (for … Fingerprint `internal/api/mfa_handlers.go:totpVerify:no-per-account-second-factor-attempt-limit` |
| 7.27 | Unauthenticated POST /api/v1/setup/complete runs a 64 MiB argon2id hash on every call, even after setup is complete, with no rate or concurrency limit | done | **Fixed 2026-11-02.** `setup.Service.Complete` now asks `AdminExists` before hashing and refuses a completed instance with ErrAlreadyCompleted (409) without any argon2id work. The check inside InTx stays for two first runs racing each other. POST /api/v1/setup/complete also carries `withAuthRateLimit`, so it shares the sign-in routes' per-address budget of failed attempts, and a 409 or 400 is charged like one. Process-wide concurrency of argon2id was not bounded: after this change the only unauthenticated hash on this route is on an instance that has not been set up yet. Tests: TestCompleteOnACompletedInstanceDoesNotHash (the hash is injectable through export_test.go and counted) and TestAuthRateLimitTripsOnSetupComplete. Finding: **Medium.** Service.Complete calls auth.HashPassword (internal/setup/service.go:237) before the AdminExists gate inside InTx (service.go:243-250). registerSetupRoutes attaches only withAudit, not withAuthRateLimit, to the public route (internal/api/setup_handlers.go:107). | 7.29 | Pending invitations outlive the inviting admin's removal, demotion or deactivation and cannot be revoked, so a removed admin can regain admin access | done | **Fixed 2026-11-02.** An invitation now carries its sender's authority and lapses with it. `PeekInvite` and `TakeInvite` (and the new list) require the sender to still be an active (not deactivated) member of the invite's organization with a role that may manage members; the roles come from `tenancy.Allows(…, PermMembersManage)` via `inviteIssuerRoles` rather than being repeated in SQL. | 7.35 | A leftover play process can kill the between-jobs sweep, which runs as the same play uid and fails open | done | **Fixed 2026-11-02 (fail closed; the window itself remains).** `sweepPlayUID` no longer trusts the sweep's exit status. | 7.44 | Security audit run 3: cover what run 2 deferred | done | **Ran 2026-11-03** (scoped to run 2's gaps and every change since 4f9c7103, 48 agent invocations): `docs/security/2026-11-03-audit-run-3/`, follow-ups 7.49–7.62. Run 2 deferred 4 units for budget (critics c02/c03): agent-reported fact and check-item cardinality in persisted org state; the zone worker's certificate renewal and reconnect client; the shared rule-evaluation ticker under one organization's rules and selectors; the cross-organization `/me/activity` feed after a membership ends (no run reviewed `internal/audit/store_sql.go`). Five run-1 covered units with unchanged source were not re-passed (agent installer, notification delivery policy, repository definitions, unscoped tickers, worker enrollment). Start from run 2's ledger (`~/security-audit-skill/pulsix/run-2`) |
| 7.45 | A key rotation does not remove the old key from the credential's own account when runs log in as a different one | done | **Fixed 2026-11-02.** The revocation names the bootstrap account as well: `revokeKindPayload` adds `also_users` = [the credential's username, or `credentials.DefaultLoginUser` when it has none; `bootstrapAccount`, which the bootstrap payload now uses too] whenever that differs from the account runs log in as. The worker passes it on as `pulsix_also_users` (a JSON list, empty rather than missing). `pulsix.revoke_key.yml` looks every named account up with getent, requires the run account to exist, and removes the previous key from each account that exists, matching any authorized_keys line that carries the key's base64 blob as a word, whatever options or comment surround it. A missing extra account is no failure. Tests: TestRevocationAlsoNamesTheCredentialsOwnAccount (jobs), TestRevokeExecutorPassesTheOtherAccounts (worker), TestRevokePlaybookClearsTheOtherAccountsToo (the embedded playbook as root on the worker image's ansible-core: both accounts lose the old key, keep the other one, and a missing account passes). Writing that test found 7.46. Finding: Follow-up of 7.33 (2026-11-02). Since 7.33 a rotation installs, verifies and revokes on the account runs log in as. Bootstrap, however, installs the credential's key for the credential's own username (\`bootstrapKindPayload\`'s \`pulsix_user\`). So on a host whose own SSH username differs from the credential's, the bootstrapped key can also sit in the credential's account, and a rotation leaves it there. The fix direction from audit run 2: the revocation also removes the old key from the credential's own account where that account exists. That needs \`pulsix.revoke_key.yml\` to skip an account that does not exist (an \`ansible.builtin.getent\` lookup before \`authorized_key\`), a second account in the revoke payload, and a real-ansible test, since the fake runner cannot show the module's behaviour on a missing user. Low: it needs the operator to have run bootstrap for one account and then pointed the host at another. |
| 7.46 | The system playbooks (bootstrap, rotate_key, revoke_key) called a module ansible-core does not ship, so none of them could run in the worker image | done | **Found and fixed 2026-11-02, while writing 7.45's real-ansible test.** All three embedded playbooks (\`internal/worker/playbooks\`) used \`ansible.builtin.authorized_key\`. That module lives in the ansible.posix collection, and the worker image installs ansible-core 2.21.4 without collections. ansible-playbook therefore refused each of them at load ("couldn't resolve module/action 'ansible.builtin.authorized_key'"), before any task ran: one-time bootstrap and SSH key rotation could not succeed on the shipped worker. The suite never saw it, because the fake runner does not run playbooks and the real-ansible e2e mode covers no system job. The playbooks now use only ansible-core's own modules: \`getent\` for the account's home, \`file\` for an owner-only .ssh (0700), \`lineinfile\` to add the key (authorized_keys 0600) or to remove every line carrying the old key's blob. Bootstrap still creates the account and the sudo rule. Installing the ansible.posix collection into the image was rejected, to keep the image free of an extra download and dependency. Tests run each embedded playbook as root on the worker image's own ansible-core (\`internal/e2e/revoke_playbook_test.go\`: TestBootstrapPlaybookCreatesTheAccountWithTheKey, TestRotatePlaybookInstallsTheNewKey, TestRevokePlaybookClearsTheOtherAccountsToo). All three failed with the load error before the change. |

## v1.0 · security audit run 3 follow-ups

From `docs/security/2026-11-03-audit-run-3/` (2026-11-03, `main` at 7ae1808, scoped to run 2's gaps and the changes since 4f9c7103). 7.49–7.53 are confirmed (reproduced locally and verified twice independently), all low; 7.54–7.60 are leads that need a database, a deployment fact or an owner decision, without severity; 7.61–7.62 are units the run deferred for budget. 7.37 (release secrets) is still open and unchanged. Decisions taken with the owner on 2026-11-03 are in each row.

| # | Item | Status | Notes |
|---|------|--------|-------|
| 7.49 | An API token that may not mint tokens can still mint worker and agent enrollment tokens whose identities survive its revocation | done | **Fixed 2026-11-03.** Both creates call `requireInteractiveSession` before `requireOrg`; listing and withdrawing stay open to tokens. Confirmed, low. `POST /orgs/{org}/worker-enrollments` (admin) and `POST /orgs/{org}/agents/tokens` (operator) sit on the bearer-enabled org group and call `requireOrg` only, so a `pxt_` token mints a worker or agent identity that keeps working after the token is revoked: the variant 7.31 left open. **Decision:** both creates require an interactive session, like token and membership minting; listing and withdrawing stay open to tokens. Automated agent roll-out by API token is given up on purpose |
| 7.50 | Forged plaintext backup headers make scheduled retention delete every authentic backup, including the one just written | done | **Fixed 2026-11-03.** The header carries an HMAC-SHA256 keyed by `HeaderKey` (HKDF label `pulsix backup header v1`), the manifest repeats it and `Verify` compares it; `ParseObjectName` accepts only canonical names (fractional-second aliases were a bypass found in review); `Prune` counts only verified, past-dated scheduled objects and never the run's own backup. Backups made before this fix list as unverified, still download and restore, and are never pruned — the manager logs how many it kept on each prune run. Confirmed, low. `Prune` decides from the unauthenticated plaintext header (`CreatedAt`, `Kind`), so anyone who can only add objects to the backup folder or bucket makes the manager delete every real scheduled backup on each run. **Fix:** prune always keeps the backup the run just wrote; the header carries a MAC keyed by an HKDF of the master key (own label); an object whose MAC is missing or wrong counts as unverified, is never pruned and never fills a tier; the name's stamp and kind must match the header; objects claiming a creation time after the run are ignored |
| 7.51 | `restore --list` writes terminal control sequences from unauthenticated backup headers and object names | done | **Fixed 2026-11-03.** `restore --list` prints names and header strings through `printable`; `ReadHeader` refuses unknown kinds and unprintable versions. Confirmed, low. Escape non-printable runes in every name and header string `restore --list` prints; `ReadHeader` refuses a kind other than scheduled/manual and a non-printable `pulsix_version` (such objects list as damaged) |
| 7.52 | A mount point with a quote and a non-UTF-8 byte still poisons the agent's stream after 7.40 | done | **Fixed 2026-11-03.** `reportableItem` and `withinContract` require valid UTF-8; the agent session drops a chunk that fails client-side marshalling instead of re-buffering it. Confirmed, low. gopsutil keeps the raw bytes when unquoting fails, proto3 cannot encode the item, and the agent re-buffers and replays the same message on every reconnect, so the host goes silent while it still looks seen. **Fix:** `reportableItem` and the manager's `withinContract` require valid UTF-8; the agent session drops a chunk it cannot encode instead of re-buffering it |
| 7.53 | Run-output redaction misses a secret containing both quote kinds when a play prints it inside a container interpolated into a string | done | **Fixed 2026-11-03.** `newRedactor` adds the CPython repr spellings (the secret's own quote choice and the always-single-quote form) and their JSON forms. Confirmed, low (reproduced with ansible-core 2.21.4 in the worker image). Python repr escapes `'` as `\'` when both quote kinds occur; `newRedactor` gains repr spellings and their JSON-escaped forms |
| 7.54 | A restore reactivates sessions, API tokens, certificates and memberships revoked after the backup | done | **Fixed 2026-11-03 as decided.** The load deletes every session; the restore output, `deploy/compose/README.md` and the end-user docs name what stays at the backup's state (API tokens, certificates, memberships, disabled users, passwords, passkeys, second factors and recovery codes, open invitations). Agent and worker enrollment tokens also come back (a used or withdrawn token is redeemable until it expires): the end-user docs say so, the restore output does not yet. Lead (owner decision). **Decision:** a restore ends every browser session (everyone signs in again); API tokens, worker/agent certificates, memberships and disabled users stay at the backup's state, and the restore output, `deploy/compose/README.md` and the end-user docs say so and what to check afterwards. The README's "a revoked worker's certificate stops working at once" gets the restore caveat |
| 7.55 | Restore checks for a running manager only once, before the confirmation and verification | done | **Fixed 2026-11-03.** `RestoreOptions.BeforeWipe` rechecks for managers right before the wipe; `load` share-locks and refuses non-empty `users`/`organizations`/`org_memberships` with `ErrWrittenDuringRestore`, which the CLI explains (stop every manager, rerun with --replace). Lead (needs Postgres to time). **Decision:** harden without measuring: check for managers again right before the wipe, and make the load refuse when `users`, `organizations` or `org_memberships` already hold rows |
| 7.56 | One host agent can grow `hosts.facts` without bound | done | **Fixed 2026-11-03.** `RecordFacts` enforces the agent's key contract; `UpsertHostFacts` replaces instead of merging when the merged set would exceed `MaxStoredFacts` (128). Lead (needs Postgres to measure). **Decision:** bound it: the manager enforces the agent's own fact-key contract (`[a-z0-9_]`, 1–32 characters) and a host's stored facts are capped at 128 keys (a push that would exceed it replaces the stored set instead of merging) |
| 7.57 | One host agent can persist unbounded check tuples that every evaluation of that host re-reads | done | **Fixed 2026-11-03.** Update-first ingest with a per-batch count; new tuples past `MaxTuplesPerHost` (4096) are dropped and counted in `Ingest.OverCap`. Two concurrent batches can overshoot by one batch. Lead (needs Postgres to measure). **Decision:** cap distinct (check_key, item, metric) tuples per host at 4096; observations for new tuples past the cap are dropped and counted, existing tuples keep updating |
| 7.58 | One tenant's window-heavy rule can starve the instance-wide rule evaluation ticker | done | **Fixed 2026-11-03.** `MaxWindowsPerRule` (16) at save; `SET LOCAL statement_timeout = '10s'` per host; a failed host gets `check_eval_cursor.retry_after = now()+60s` (migration 00076) and both claim queries skip it until then — a `next_due_at` postpone, as first planned, still let a dirty failed host sort ahead of healthy ones. Lead (needs TimescaleDB to time). **Decision:** at most 16 distinct window aggregates per rule expression (refused at save); each host's evaluation runs with a 10 s statement timeout; a host whose evaluation fails is postponed 60 s instead of heading the next batch again |
| 7.59 | The compose worker snippet writes the live enrollment token into the compose file; the README still shows the token as a `docker run` argument | done | **Fixed 2026-11-03.** The compose YAML reads `${PULSIX_ENROLL_TOKEN:-}` (an empty default, so later `ps`/`down`/upgrades still work) and the create response carries `compose_up_command`; the README example uses the environment form with `--init` and the play uid. Lead (deployment fact). **Decision:** the compose YAML no longer contains the token (it reads `${PULSIX_ENROLL_TOKEN}` from the shell) and the dialog shows the start command that supplies it; `deploy/compose/README.md` uses the same environment form as the docker snippet |
| 7.60 | Webhook policy still dials the IPv6 metadata addresses of Akamai/Linode and Scaleway | done | **Fixed 2026-11-03** as decided. Lead (provider facts). **Decision:** add `fd00:a9fe:a9fe::/64` (Akamai/Linode, `fd00:a9fe:a9fe::1`) and `fd00:42::/64` (Scaleway, `fd00:42::42`) to the blocked ranges; the rest of fc00::/7 stays allowed |
| 7.61 | One unreadable or special entry in the backup folder or bucket fails or blocks every backup listing, and prune runs that listing under the instance-wide backup lock | done | **Fixed 2026-11-03.** The folder listing skips non-regular entries and opens with `O_NOFOLLOW|O_NONBLOCK`; a per-entry error becomes that object's `HeaderErr` in both backends; `Open` also refuses non-regular files (a symlinked backup can no longer be downloaded or restored). Deferred by the run (final-clean critic). **Decision:** harden: the folder listing skips non-regular files (no open on a FIFO or symlink), and an entry that cannot be read or described is listed as damaged instead of failing the whole listing, in both backends |
| 7.62 | An agent's oversized facts could make a job's claim response exceed the 64 MiB worker limit | done | **Fixed 2026-11-03.** Today's behaviour was wrong: gRPC refused the oversize response after the claim had committed and the job looped through the reaper. `ClaimJob` now checks the size and fails the job at once with an error naming size and limit. Deferred by the run (post-wave critic), never reviewed. **Decision:** check what an oversize claim response does to the committed claim and the org's queue; if it loops or blocks, fail that job with a clear error instead. 7.56's cap is the main mitigation |
| 7.63 | Leftovers from the 7.49–7.62 reviews | done | **Done 2026-11-03.** Restore output names enrollment tokens and every re-enroll case; `runHost` logs say postponing only with a live context; the agent drops results with invalid UTF-8 in check_key, item, metric or label and sanitises error text; test hygiene. (1) The restore output does not yet name agent and worker enrollment tokens, which a restore makes redeemable again until they expire (the end-user docs do). (2) `internal/rules/due_sql.go` `runHost` logs "postponing" on a commit failure or panic even when the batch was cut short and nothing is postponed. (3) The agent could check `utf8.ValidString` on every result before sending, so the session's drop of an unencodable chunk stays a backstop; a drop can lose chunks already queued on that stream. (4) Minor test and comment hygiene listed in the branch's reviews (e.g. the displaced `panicStore` doc comment in `internal/rules/ticker_test.go`, the vacuous size regex in the oversize-claim test) |
| 7.64 | Leftovers from the 7.3/7.47/7.48 reviews | done | Package `docs/superpowers/plans/2026-11-05-7x-leftovers.md`, branch `fix/7.x-leftovers` (2026-11-05). Items 1, 2, 4 and 5 were already done on 2026-11-03 (`0f3b667`, `ec1f2a5`, `3b3f249`, `c30bd1f`) and verified again. Item 3: `go test -race -count=20 ./internal/backup/` ran clean (0 data races, 1 790 s; a single run takes about 90 s on a loaded machine, so the 900 s timeout of the brief is too short for 20 runs) — not reproduced, closed without a change |

## v1.0 · UI overhaul: host view and its follow-ups

From the owner's first look at the hosts pages on a fresh local stack (2026-11-03). Design: `docs/superpowers/specs/2026-11-03-host-view-design.md`. The host view is the template the other list and detail views follow, using the building blocks in `web/src/ui/detail-layout.tsx` (`DetailLayout`, `SidebarFacts`, `Section`, `DisabledHint`) and `web/src/ui/list-row.tsx` (`ListRow`, `RowMenu`).

| # | Item | Status | Notes |
|---|------|--------|-------|
| 7.65 | Host view overhaul: host page as sidebar and sections, clickable list rows with a working row menu, one add and edit dialog, `Switch` for every boolean setting, runs filtered by host | done | **Done 2026-11-03** on `feat/host-view`. The host page is a sticky sidebar (identity, key facts, capability switches, tags, Run command, Edit, section navigation) beside the sections State, Alerts, Checks, Runs, Connection & SSH, Inventory and Facts; nothing is clipped. | 7.66 | First-run setup requires a setup token | done | **Done 2026-11-03** on `feat/setup-token`. The token is derived from the master key (HKDF-SHA256, 10 bytes, base32, `XXXX-XXXX-XXXX-XXXX`), not stored, so every manager and every restart shows the same value. While setup is open the manager logs the token and a `/setup?token=` link at every start; `pulsix-manager setup-token` (compose: `docker compose run --rm cli setup-token`) prints both. The wizard's first step asks for the token, the link pre-fills it, and a wrong token is a 403 charged against the setup rate limit. A completed instance answers 409 whatever token it is sent. End-user docs: first-setup page in `noix-docs`. |

## Decisions (short)

- Stack: Go (manager, worker, agent), React/TypeScript SPA, PostgreSQL + TimescaleDB, gRPC over mTLS, no broker.
- **One installation per service provider.** A provider runs their own Pulsix; their customers are organizations inside it. There is deliberately no provider entity above the organization, and resources shared across customers belong to the *installation* — the shape `instance_settings` and `instance_ca` already have. Rejected: a provider level on one shared installation. It would make every policy, role, query and navigation path carry a table with one row in the common case, and it would replace physical separation (own database, own process, own master key) with a policy predicate — a step down for a product that holds other people's SSH keys. If a multi-provider installation is ever needed, the path is a mechanical migration: add `provider_id`, backfill every instance-level row to the single provider, widen the policies. Decided 2026-10-19.
- Multi-tenant and self-hostable. Manager HA, workers per zone.
- Host capabilities `managed` (Ansible via SSH) and `monitored` (agent and/or remote checks) are independent.
- Inventory groups (Ansible, schema builder) and monitoring groups (tag selectors, alerting) are separate concepts.
- Agent config is hybrid: checks from GUI, secrets local only.
- One version and one tag for manager, worker and agent; manager accepts same and previous minor version.
- Check engine layered like Checkmk/Zabbix: plugins in code, definitions and rules as data, inheritance plugin → org → group → host → item, discovery, custom checks in Nagios or JSON format.
- Status colours (ok/warn/crit/unknown) are reserved for states; cyan is the action colour.
- GitHub repo `NoiXdev/pulsix` starts **private**; goes public with the v1.0 launch.
- Test policy: every feature ships with tests; E2E scenarios (agent, monitoring, Ansible, HA, auth, tenancy, status pages) are milestone gates; coverage enforced in CI.
